      Tomato

      1. Identity Block

      Scientific name: Solanum lycopersicum. Family: Solanaceae. Common names: tomato, garden tomato, slicing tomato, paste tomato, cherry tomato. Source class: extension-supported practice for regional planting and management; peer-reviewed mechanism where specifically cited; practitioner doctrine only when explicitly labeled. Functional definition: a frost-sensitive warm-season fruiting vegetable managed in North Texas as a spring and fall crop rather than a midsummer fruit-set crop.

      1. Composition & Properties

      Tomato is an herbaceous perennial grown as an annual. Determinate cultivars produce a concentrated crop; indeterminate cultivars continue vegetative and reproductive growth until heat, frost, disease, or management terminates production. Fruit types include cherry, grape, slicer, beefsteak, Roma/paste, and specialty heirlooms. Disease-resistance codes are central to quality control. For North Texas, V, F, N, T, and A packages matter: Verticillium wilt, Fusarium wilt, root-knot nematode, tobacco/tomato mosaic, and Alternaria-related resistance packages. Exact seed-catalog code meanings must be confirmed on the label.

      For manuscript use, classify this entry as a warm-season fruiting solanaceous vegetable rather than by grocery category alone. Its practical properties in a North Texas garden are defined by season, plant architecture, marketable organ, and how quickly quality is lost when the crop is mistimed. It fills the role of flagship high-value crop that justifies staking, transplants, irrigation, scouting, and variety selection. That role matters because the same bed can only hold one crop at a time; the crop must justify its season slot, water demand, and pest-management burden.

      1. Mechanism of Action

      Tomato succeeds when root establishment, flowering, pollination, and early fruit enlargement happen before sustained summer heat disrupts reproduction. In North Texas, tomato plants can look lush in June or July while setting little fruit because pollen release, pollen viability, and flower retention fail under hot days and warm nights. This crop is therefore managed by calendar and reproductive biology, not by foliage growth alone. The disease triangle remains the governing disease framework: susceptible host, viable pathogen, and favorable environment. Resistant varieties, sanitation, airflow, drip irrigation, and bed rotation reduce risk; nutrition alone does not prevent Fusarium wilt, bacterial spot, early blight, or root-knot nematode. Tomato does NOT overcome summer heat-set failure because extra nitrogen, compost tea, or living soil is added. It also does NOT become disease-proof because a bed has high organic matter. If the pathogen, host, and weather align, disease can progress rapidly.

      The operating mechanism is root establishment and fruit set before heat; pollen viability, night temperature, water transport, and canopy airflow determine whether flowers become fruit. The grower is not merely keeping a plant alive; the grower is protecting the specific developmental stage that creates the harvest. When that stage is interrupted, later vigor rarely repairs the lost yield. This is especially important in North Texas, where spring can move from cool to hot quickly and where late-summer plantings must beat the first freeze. Management should therefore begin with the calendar and the target harvest organ, then move to fertility, irrigation, and pest response.

      The primary limiter is sustained summer heat, bacterial spot/leaf spot carryover, Fusarium/root-knot history, and stink bug pressure. This limiter should be handled through the Disease Triangle and IPM Hierarchy rather than through single-product thinking. A susceptible host, a viable pathogen or pest population, and favorable weather or canopy conditions produce damage. Cultural controls - timing, rotation, spacing, drip irrigation, sanitation, mulch, row cover, trellising, resistant varieties, and harvest discipline - carry more of the system than rescue sprays. Once the crop has passed the correct growth stage, a product cannot usually restore the missing developmental window.

      1. Planting and Establishment

      Spring transplant window: March 1-April 30, with March 15-April 15 the tighter North Central Texas production target. Fall transplant window: June 20-July 15, with July 1-July 15 often the practical window for fall tomatoes. Soil temperature: 60 F minimum at 6-8 inches for transplants. Use stocky disease-free transplants; harden before planting; stake, cage, or weave at transplanting. Mulch after soil warms. Recommended resistant varieties include Celebrity VFNT, Carnival VFNT, President VFNT, Merced VF1F2ST, Heatwave VF1F2SA, Champion VFNT, First Lady VFNT, Superfantastic VFN, Roma VF, Surefire VF1, Big Beef, Heatmaster, Defiant, and Iron Lady where available 2026 availability.

      Use the planting window as a production boundary, not a casual suggestion. For North Texas, the working calendar is: spring transplants March 1-April 30 with March 15-April 15 preferred; fall transplants June 20-July 15 with July 1-July 15 as the tighter production window. The soil-temperature requirement is 60 F minimum at 6-8 inches for transplants. Soil temperature should be checked in the morning before sun warms the bed and should be stable, not a one-afternoon spike. When the crop is transplanted, transplant size and hardening matter; when direct-seeded, seedbed crusting and moisture continuity matter more than most amendments.

      Establishment should be built around: raised or well-shaped beds, drip irrigation, mulch after warming, cages or weave at transplanting, and no solanaceous residue in disease beds. In Blackland clay, bed shape is a first-order practice. Raised beds, broad ridges, and controlled traffic prevent the common pattern of a crop looking acceptable aboveground while roots sit in compacted or poorly aerated soil. Drip irrigation is the default because it reduces leaf wetness, preserves soil structure, and allows water to be delivered without splashing pathogens onto lower leaves or fruit.

      Recommended and defensible varieties or variety classes include: Celebrity VFNT, Carnival VFNT, President VFNT, Merced VF1F2ST, Heatwave VF1F2SA, Champion VFNT, First Lady VFNT, Superfantastic VFN, Roma VF, Surefire VF1, Big Beef, Heatmaster, Defiant, and Iron Lady where available current availability. Disease-resistant variety language must be read literally. A resistance code means resistance or tolerance to a named pathogen, race, or disease complex; it does not mean general garden toughness. For manuscript lock, current seed availability, exact resistance race codes, and regional performance should be verified against the final seed-source list and county extension notes.

      1. Interactions

      Tomato pairs with drip irrigation, clean mulch, open airflow, resistant varieties, and solanaceous rotation. Do not follow tomato with pepper, eggplant, potato, or tomatillo in a disease-prone bed when another bed is available. Overhead irrigation increases leaf wetness and foliar disease. Excess nitrogen produces foliage and delays reproductive balance. Blossom-end rot is managed through water consistency, root health, salinity control, and calcium transport, not by routine foliar calcium folklore alone diagnostic threshold.

      1. Contraindications & Failure Modes

      Do not plant into cold wet clay or buy transplants with leaf spots, distorted growth, aphids, or stem lesions. Do not keep a diseased tomato crop as chop-and-drop mulch. Common failures include late spring planting, heat-set failure, weak staking, overhead watering, susceptible heirlooms in diseased beds, bacterial spot from infected transplants, root-knot nematode, and stink bug or leaffooted bug injury. Neem oil is not applied above 85 F, and wettable sulfur is not applied within two weeks of neem oil or another oil.

      Do not plant this crop when the known limiter is already present and unmanaged: sustained summer heat, bacterial spot/leaf spot carryover, Fusarium/root-knot history, and stink bug pressure. Do not buy diseased transplants, do not use unknown planting stock for production beds, and do not leave infected residue as surface mulch in a bed that will host a related crop. The most expensive failure is the one that carries pathogen or pest pressure forward into the next planting window.

      1. Diagnostics & Verification

      Successful stands show new growth, flowering before severe heat, and fruit set before June heat dominates. If flowers drop without fruit, check temperature history before applying fertilizer. If lower leaves yellow upward, inspect lesion pattern, splash, and airflow. Harvest at first color break: once green fruit begins to show yellow, salmon, pink, or red, it is physiologically mature enough to ripen off the vine. This reduces cracking, birds, stink bugs, and heat loss.

      Verification starts with the crop-specific harvest indicator: first color break for slicers and paste tomatoes; cherry types can be picked fully colored when pest pressure is low. This is the standard to use before interpreting plant size as success. A large plant without the correct harvest-stage signal is not a successful crop; it is biomass. Record planting date, soil temperature, cultivar, first flower or head date where applicable, first harvest date, and the reason the crop ended. Those notes are the basis for improving the next season.

      1. Lifecycle, Storage & Shelf Life

      Transplants usually need 8-10 weeks to production under favorable conditions. Spring crops peak before extreme heat; fall crops must ripen before mid-November frost risk. Store breaker fruit at room temperature out of direct sun. Remove diseased residue at termination and inspect roots for galls or vascular symptoms.

      Lifecycle handling should follow the crop's real endpoint. Storage and postharvest rule: breaker fruit at room temperature out of direct sun; avoid chilling green or breaker fruit unless decay risk overrides flavor. For annual vegetables, the lifecycle also includes residue decisions. Healthy residue can be composted or used in appropriate mulch systems; diseased residue, nematode-infested roots, and pest-infested fruit should be removed or hot-composted where the composting system is capable of sanitation compost sanitation protocol.

      1. Sourcing & Quality Control

      Buy seed or transplants from suppliers that disclose disease codes. Confirm whether F means race 1 only or includes F1/F2/F3, and confirm T code meaning. Fall tomato success often depends on sourcing large healthy transplants by late June or early July. Clearance-rack transplants are experimental, not production stock.

      For this crop, the quality-control emphasis is the match between source and North Texas use: Celebrity VFNT, Carnival VFNT, President VFNT, Merced VF1F2ST, Heatwave VF1F2SA, Champion VFNT, First Lady VFNT, Superfantastic VFN, Roma VF, Surefire VF1, Big Beef, Heatmaster, Defiant, and Iron Lady where available current availability. A novelty variety can be grown as a trial, but production beds should reserve most space for adapted, documented cultivars. Keep seed lots labeled through harvest so that failures can be tied to cultivar, planting date, and bed rather than being remembered as a vague seasonal disappointment.

      1. North Texas Note

      Primary North Texas diseases: early blight/leaf spot complex, Fusarium wilt, root-knot nematode, bacterial spot/speck where infected transplants and wet foliage align county priority. Primary pests: tomato hornworm, aphids/whiteflies, stink bugs/leaffooted bugs, spider mites, and nematodes. Manage tomato as a spring/fall crop, not a July fruit-set crop.

      North Texas management priority: match the crop to alkaline Blackland Prairie clay, erratic rainfall, chloraminated municipal water, and routine 100-110 F summer heat. The primary disease priorities are early blight/leaf spot complex, Fusarium wilt, bacterial spot/speck, root-knot nematode, and viral disease where vectors are active. The primary pest priorities are tomato hornworm, aphids, whiteflies, stink bugs, leaffooted bugs, spider mites, and root-knot nematode. These are not abstract lists; they determine the planting window, rotation group, irrigation method, row-cover timing, scouting route, and whether the bed should be pivoted to a different crop.

      1. Cross-References

      Disease Triangle; IPM Hierarchy; Seasonal Succession and Bed Pivoting SOP; early blight; Fusarium wilt; root-knot nematode; Internal provisional chapters 29, 39, 42.

      1. Sources & Confidence Note

      Sources by class: Extension: Texas A&M AgriLife tomato guidance; Rockwall and Tarrant/North Central Texas planting and variety guides; Cornell disease-resistant tomato varieties. Peer-reviewed: Sato, Peet, and Thomas 2000 on heat stress and tomato fruit set DOI in final bibliography. Confidence note: High confidence for planting windows, heat-set failure, first-color-break harvest, and resistance-code logic; local disease ranking and annual variety availability require verification.

      Source classes for this entry: Extension: Texas A&M AgriLife tomato guidance; Rockwall and Tarrant/North Central Texas planting guides; Cornell disease-resistant variety list. Peer-reviewed: Sato, Peet, and Thomas 2000 tomato heat-set physiology DOI. Practitioner doctrine: companion-planting, tonic, foliar-feed, microbial-inoculant, and input-stacking claims are not treated as mechanism unless a source class supports the mechanism directly. items remain for current seed availability, exact Phase K entry titles, and county-level pest/disease ranking.

      Pepper

      1. Identity Block

      Scientific name: Capsicum annuum primarily; specialty chiles may include other Capsicum species scope. Family: Solanaceae. Common names: bell pepper, sweet pepper, chile pepper, jalapeno, serrano, cayenne, banana pepper, poblano. Source class: extension-supported practice for regional planting and management; peer-reviewed mechanism where specifically cited; practitioner doctrine only when explicitly labeled. Functional definition: a frost-sensitive warm-season fruiting vegetable with better summer persistence than tomato but high bacterial spot risk.

      1. Composition & Properties

      Pepper is a woody-stemmed warm-season perennial grown as an annual. Fruit types include blocky bell peppers, elongated sweet peppers, jalapenos, serranos, cayennes, wax peppers, poblanos, and specialty chiles. Bell peppers can be harvested mature green or fully ripe red, yellow, orange, purple, or chocolate depending on cultivar. Branches are brittle, so harvest requires cutting or gentle snapping. Heat tolerance is higher than tomato, but plants still suffer from drought, flower drop, sunscald, spider mites, and bacterial spot.

      For manuscript use, classify this entry as a warm-season fruiting solanaceous vegetable rather than by grocery category alone. Its practical properties in a North Texas garden are defined by season, plant architecture, marketable organ, and how quickly quality is lost when the crop is mistimed. It fills the role of heat-advantaged solanaceous crop for bells, sweet peppers, and chiles. That role matters because the same bed can only hold one crop at a time; the crop must justify its season slot, water demand, and pest-management burden.

      1. Mechanism of Action

      Pepper succeeds by establishing in warm soil, maintaining steady root function, flowering through hot weather, and setting fruit under conditions that often stop tomato. Its North Texas advantage is relative heat tolerance, not immunity to stress. Bacterial spot is the crop-defining disease risk: susceptible cultivar, infected seed or transplant, warm wet foliage, and splash or handling spread create outbreaks. Resistant varieties and dry foliage management are more reliable than rescue sprays. Pepper does NOT become protected from bacterial spot because the fruit is pungent, because capsaicin is present, or because the bed is biologically active. Pepper also does NOT need to be kept on the plant until fully ripe for every harvest; green harvest maintains production while selected fruit can ripen for flavor.

      The operating mechanism is warm-root establishment, repeated flowering, and pod development through heat that often stops tomato fruit set. The grower is not merely keeping a plant alive; the grower is protecting the specific developmental stage that creates the harvest. When that stage is interrupted, later vigor rarely repairs the lost yield. This is especially important in North Texas, where spring can move from cool to hot quickly and where late-summer plantings must beat the first freeze. Management should therefore begin with the calendar and the target harvest organ, then move to fertility, irrigation, and pest response.

      The primary limiter is bacterial spot from infected seed/transplants and wet foliage, plus drought, sunscald, mites, and brittle branch breakage. This limiter should be handled through the Disease Triangle and IPM Hierarchy rather than through single-product thinking. A susceptible host, a viable pathogen or pest population, and favorable weather or canopy conditions produce damage. Cultural controls - timing, rotation, spacing, drip irrigation, sanitation, mulch, row cover, trellising, resistant varieties, and harvest discipline - carry more of the system than rescue sprays. Once the crop has passed the correct growth stage, a product cannot usually restore the missing developmental window.

      1. Planting and Establishment

      Spring transplant window: March 30-May 30 in local guides, with April 1-May 1 the main production target. Fall transplant window: July 1-July 25 for many pepper types, with some local tables extending later for fast cultivars. Soil temperature: 70 F minimum. Space about 18 inches apart in rows at least 3 feet apart or equivalent bed spacing. Local varieties include Big Bertha, Jupiter, Summer Sweet 860, Gypsy, Cubanelle, TAM Mild Jalapeno, Mucho Nacho, Super Cayenne, and serrano/cayenne types. Bacterial spot resistant bell options include Nitro S10, Sailfish, SVPB9386, Tarpon, Turnpike, Green Machine, Ninja S10, and related X10R/X5R lines where available current seed availability.

      Use the planting window as a production boundary, not a casual suggestion. For North Texas, the working calendar is: spring transplants March 30-May 30 with April as the practical target; fall transplants July 3-August 25, with July preferred for full harvest. The soil-temperature requirement is 70 F minimum for transplants. Soil temperature should be checked in the morning before sun warms the bed and should be stable, not a one-afternoon spike. When the crop is transplanted, transplant size and hardening matter; when direct-seeded, seedbed crusting and moisture continuity matter more than most amendments.

      Establishment should be built around: well-drained beds, drip irrigation, mulch after warm-up, cages or stakes for heavy bells, and rotation away from tomato/eggplant/potato. In Blackland clay, bed shape is a first-order practice. Raised beds, broad ridges, and controlled traffic prevent the common pattern of a crop looking acceptable aboveground while roots sit in compacted or poorly aerated soil. Drip irrigation is the default because it reduces leaf wetness, preserves soil structure, and allows water to be delivered without splashing pathogens onto lower leaves or fruit.

      Recommended and defensible varieties or variety classes include: Big Bertha, Jupiter, Summer Sweet 860, Gypsy, Cubanelle/Sweet Spot, TAM Mild Jalapeno, Mucho Nacho, Super Cayenne, plus bacterial-spot-resistant bells such as Green Machine, Ninja S10, Nitro S10, Sailfish, Turnpike, and related X10R lines catalog package. Disease-resistant variety language must be read literally. A resistance code means resistance or tolerance to a named pathogen, race, or disease complex; it does not mean general garden toughness. For manuscript lock, current seed availability, exact resistance race codes, and regional performance should be verified against the final seed-source list and county extension notes.

      1. Interactions

      Pepper pairs with drip irrigation, mulch, moderate fertility, staking where loaded, and rotation away from solanaceous crops. Irregular moisture contributes to blossom-end rot and fruit drop. Overhead irrigation, dense spacing, infected transplants, and working plants when wet increase bacterial spot risk. Pepper can follow spring lettuce or onions in a bed pivot, but should not follow tomato or eggplant in a disease bed.

      1. Contraindications & Failure Modes

      Do not plant into cold soil or buy transplants with leaf spots, aphids, or distorted foliage. Do not depend on copper alone for bacterial spot; copper resistance is possible local status. Common failures include early planting, water stress during bloom, bacterial spot, sunscald from defoliation, spider mites in hot dry weather, aphids, flea beetles on seedlings, and branch breakage during harvest. Neem is not used above 85 F and sulfur/oil intervals must be respected.

      Do not plant this crop when the known limiter is already present and unmanaged: bacterial spot from infected seed/transplants and wet foliage, plus drought, sunscald, mites, and brittle branch breakage. Do not buy diseased transplants, do not use unknown planting stock for production beds, and do not leave infected residue as surface mulch in a bed that will host a related crop. The most expensive failure is the one that carries pathogen or pest pressure forward into the next planting window.

      1. Diagnostics & Verification

      Healthy plants show new growth within two weeks of transplanting and set fruit before extreme heat. Bacterial spot appears as small dark lesions, often with yellowing and defoliation during wet weather. Harvest bell peppers shiny, firm, and dark green for green harvest; leave selected fruit to reach full color for ripe harvest. Jalapenos are mature green when full-sized and glossy; many hot peppers can also be harvested red.

      Verification starts with the crop-specific harvest indicator: two-stage harvest: mature green for production momentum and fully ripe red/yellow/orange/purple for flavor and color. This is the standard to use before interpreting plant size as success. A large plant without the correct harvest-stage signal is not a successful crop; it is biomass. Record planting date, soil temperature, cultivar, first flower or head date where applicable, first harvest date, and the reason the crop ended. Those notes are the basis for improving the next season.

      1. Lifecycle, Storage & Shelf Life

      Peppers begin production about 8-10 weeks after transplanting and continue until frost, drought, mites, or disease reduce vigor. Mature green fruit stores best above chilling temperatures; avoid long cold storage below about 45-50 F crop-type storage threshold.

      Lifecycle handling should follow the crop's real endpoint. Storage and postharvest rule: mature green and ripe fruit store briefly cool but not freezing; avoid chilling injury below crop-appropriate limits crop storage threshold. For annual vegetables, the lifecycle also includes residue decisions. Healthy residue can be composted or used in appropriate mulch systems; diseased residue, nematode-infested roots, and pest-infested fruit should be removed or hot-composted where the composting system is capable of sanitation compost sanitation protocol.

      1. Sourcing & Quality Control

      Buy seed/transplants by variety and disease package. In bacterial spot beds, prioritize BLS race resistance over novelty color. Confirm Phytophthora resistance where drainage is marginal. Reject transplants with any spotting because infected transplants are a major pathway into clean beds.

      For this crop, the quality-control emphasis is the match between source and North Texas use: Big Bertha, Jupiter, Summer Sweet 860, Gypsy, Cubanelle/Sweet Spot, TAM Mild Jalapeno, Mucho Nacho, Super Cayenne, plus bacterial-spot-resistant bells such as Green Machine, Ninja S10, Nitro S10, Sailfish, Turnpike, and related X10R lines catalog package. A novelty variety can be grown as a trial, but production beds should reserve most space for adapted, documented cultivars. Keep seed lots labeled through harvest so that failures can be tied to cultivar, planting date, and bed rather than being remembered as a vague seasonal disappointment.

      1. North Texas Note

      Pepper is a stronger summer crop than tomato but bacterial spot is the priority disease. Primary pests are aphids, flea beetles, leaf miners, spider mites, stink bugs, and pod-feeding caterpillars county priority. Harvest both green and ripe fruit to balance yield and flavor.

      North Texas management priority: match the crop to alkaline Blackland Prairie clay, erratic rainfall, chloraminated municipal water, and routine 100-110 F summer heat. The primary disease priorities are bacterial spot as the priority, plus Phytophthora blight in wet sites, tobamoviruses, and root issues in saturated clay. The primary pest priorities are aphids, flea beetles on young plants, leaf miners, thrips, spider mites, stink bugs, and caterpillars. These are not abstract lists; they determine the planting window, rotation group, irrigation method, row-cover timing, scouting route, and whether the bed should be pivoted to a different crop.

      1. Cross-References

      Disease Triangle; IPM Hierarchy; Seasonal Succession and Bed Pivoting SOP; bacterial spot of tomato and pepper; aphids; spider mites; Internal provisional chapters 29, 39, 42.

      1. Sources & Confidence Note

      Sources by class: Extension: Texas A&M pepper guidance; Rockwall/Tarrant planting and variety guides; Clemson bacterial spot guidance; Cornell and Rutgers disease-resistant pepper lists; Oklahoma State pepper production. Confidence note: High confidence for windows, soil temperature, harvest stages, and bacterial spot priority; exact race prevalence and current resistant cultivar availability need verification.

      Source classes for this entry: Extension: Texas A&M AgriLife pepper guidance; Rockwall and Tarrant guides; Clemson bacterial spot guidance; Cornell/Rutgers disease-resistant pepper lists. Practitioner doctrine: companion-planting, tonic, foliar-feed, microbial-inoculant, and input-stacking claims are not treated as mechanism unless a source class supports the mechanism directly. items remain for current seed availability, exact Phase K entry titles, and county-level pest/disease ranking.

      Eggplant

      1. Identity Block

      Scientific name: Solanum melongena. Family: Solanaceae. Common names: eggplant, aubergine, brinjal, Italian eggplant, Japanese eggplant. Source class: extension-supported practice for regional planting and management; peer-reviewed mechanism where specifically cited; practitioner doctrine only when explicitly labeled. Functional definition: a heat-persistent solanaceous fruiting vegetable whose main North Texas constraints are flea beetles early and spider mites in summer.

      1. Composition & Properties

      Eggplant is a tropical perennial grown as a warm-season annual. Plants usually reach 2-4 feet, with broad leaves, purple or white flowers, and fruit ranging from large oval Italian types to long Asian types and small specialty forms. Stems and calyxes can have spines. The fruit is harvested immature, while skin is glossy and seeds are soft. Eggplant tolerates heat better than tomato once established but is sensitive to cold soil and early pest injury.

      For manuscript use, classify this entry as a warm-season fruiting solanaceous vegetable rather than by grocery category alone. Its practical properties in a North Texas garden are defined by season, plant architecture, marketable organ, and how quickly quality is lost when the crop is mistimed. It fills the role of extended-season summer solanaceous crop with better heat persistence than tomato. That role matters because the same bed can only hold one crop at a time; the crop must justify its season slot, water demand, and pest-management burden.

      1. Mechanism of Action

      Eggplant succeeds by establishing in truly warm soil, building broad photosynthetic leaf area, flowering through heat, and continuing fruiting over an extended summer window. Its North Texas advantage is heat persistence; its weakness is leaf-area loss from pests. Flea beetles attack young plants and reduce early canopy. Spider mites multiply under hot, dry, dusty conditions and cause stippling, bronzing, webbing, and leaf collapse. Eggplant does NOT become low-maintenance because it likes heat. Heat tolerance does not protect it from spider mites, flea beetles, Verticillium wilt, Phomopsis fruit rot, or root stress in saturated clay.

      The operating mechanism is large-leaf canopy, warm roots, steady water, and repeated flowers producing tender fruit over a long summer season. The grower is not merely keeping a plant alive; the grower is protecting the specific developmental stage that creates the harvest. When that stage is interrupted, later vigor rarely repairs the lost yield. This is especially important in North Texas, where spring can move from cool to hot quickly and where late-summer plantings must beat the first freeze. Management should therefore begin with the calendar and the target harvest organ, then move to fertility, irrigation, and pest response.

      The primary limiter is flea beetle injury during establishment and spider mite flare-ups during hot dry weather. This limiter should be handled through the Disease Triangle and IPM Hierarchy rather than through single-product thinking. A susceptible host, a viable pathogen or pest population, and favorable weather or canopy conditions produce damage. Cultural controls - timing, rotation, spacing, drip irrigation, sanitation, mulch, row cover, trellising, resistant varieties, and harvest discipline - carry more of the system than rescue sprays. Once the crop has passed the correct growth stage, a product cannot usually restore the missing developmental window.

      1. Planting and Establishment

      Spring transplant window: April 1-April 30 after soil warms. Fall reset is possible only with large healthy transplants in late July to late August exact NTX fall date. Soil temperature: 75 F target for transplanting. Space 24-36 inches apart in fertile, well-drained raised beds. Recommended Texas varieties include Black Bell, Black Magic, Epic, Classic, Florida High Bush, Florida Market, Night Shadow, Ichiban, and Tycoon. Florida High Bush, Florida Market, and Florida Beauty have been noted for Phomopsis resistance in older disease guidance current availability and claims.

      Use the planting window as a production boundary, not a casual suggestion. For North Texas, the working calendar is: spring transplants April 1-April 30 after true warmth; fall resets only with large healthy transplants in late July-August local fall line. The soil-temperature requirement is 75 F practical transplant soil target in North Texas guides. Soil temperature should be checked in the morning before sun warms the bed and should be stable, not a one-afternoon spike. When the crop is transplanted, transplant size and hardening matter; when direct-seeded, seedbed crusting and moisture continuity matter more than most amendments.

      Establishment should be built around: raised warm beds, early flea beetle protection, drip irrigation, mulch after warming, and wide spacing for airflow. In Blackland clay, bed shape is a first-order practice. Raised beds, broad ridges, and controlled traffic prevent the common pattern of a crop looking acceptable aboveground while roots sit in compacted or poorly aerated soil. Drip irrigation is the default because it reduces leaf wetness, preserves soil structure, and allows water to be delivered without splashing pathogens onto lower leaves or fruit.

      Recommended and defensible varieties or variety classes include: Black Bell, Black Magic, Epic, Classic, Florida High Bush, Florida Market, Night Shadow, Ichiban, Tycoon, and Phomopsis-tolerant Florida lines where available current resistance claims. Disease-resistant variety language must be read literally. A resistance code means resistance or tolerance to a named pathogen, race, or disease complex; it does not mean general garden toughness. For manuscript lock, current seed availability, exact resistance race codes, and regional performance should be verified against the final seed-source list and county extension notes.

      1. Interactions

      Eggplant pairs with mulch after soil warms, drip irrigation, clean beds, and early insect protection. It should rotate away from tomato, pepper, potato, and tomatillo. Row cover can reduce early flea beetle damage but must be removed for pollinator access once flowering begins unless hand pollination is used. Avoid drought and dust, both of which favor spider mite outbreaks.

      1. Contraindications & Failure Modes

      Do not plant into cold soil or set small transplants into unprotected flea beetle pressure. Do not use broad-spectrum insecticides casually because predator disruption can worsen mites product-specific effects. Common failures include cold transplant shock, early flea beetle defoliation, drought stress, spider mites, overmature fruit, Verticillium wilt, leaf spots, and fruit rot. Neem above 85 F and sulfur/oil overlap are prohibited.

      Do not plant this crop when the known limiter is already present and unmanaged: flea beetle injury during establishment and spider mite flare-ups during hot dry weather. Do not buy diseased transplants, do not use unknown planting stock for production beds, and do not leave infected residue as surface mulch in a bed that will host a related crop. The most expensive failure is the one that carries pathogen or pest pressure forward into the next planting window.

      1. Diagnostics & Verification

      Successful plants show new leaf expansion within two weeks and minimal shot-holing. Flea beetles leave small holes; spider mites cause stippling and underside webbing. Harvest when fruit is one-third to full size, glossy, and before seeds harden. A thumbnail indentation that remains is a practical readiness sign. Cut fruit with pruners rather than tearing stems.

      Verification starts with the crop-specific harvest indicator: harvest glossy fruit before dull skin, hard seeds, or bitterness; use pruners because stems and calyxes can be spiny. This is the standard to use before interpreting plant size as success. A large plant without the correct harvest-stage signal is not a successful crop; it is biomass. Record planting date, soil temperature, cultivar, first flower or head date where applicable, first harvest date, and the reason the crop ended. Those notes are the basis for improving the next season.

      1. Lifecycle, Storage & Shelf Life

      Eggplant starts slowly in cool weather, accelerates with warm nights, and can fruit until frost if mites and disease are managed. Store harvested fruit around 45-50 F with high humidity for short periods home storage duration.

      Lifecycle handling should follow the crop's real endpoint. Storage and postharvest rule: short cool storage around 45-50 F with high humidity; handle gently to prevent bruising. For annual vegetables, the lifecycle also includes residue decisions. Healthy residue can be composted or used in appropriate mulch systems; diseased residue, nematode-infested roots, and pest-infested fruit should be removed or hot-composted where the composting system is capable of sanitation compost sanitation protocol.

      1. Sourcing & Quality Control

      Buy clean stocky transplants without stippling, webbing, flea beetle damage, or leaf spots. Disease-resistant choices are more limited than tomato and pepper, so clean starts, rotation, airflow, mulch, and sanitation carry more of the risk management.

      For this crop, the quality-control emphasis is the match between source and North Texas use: Black Bell, Black Magic, Epic, Classic, Florida High Bush, Florida Market, Night Shadow, Ichiban, Tycoon, and Phomopsis-tolerant Florida lines where available current resistance claims. A novelty variety can be grown as a trial, but production beds should reserve most space for adapted, documented cultivars. Keep seed lots labeled through harvest so that failures can be tied to cultivar, planting date, and bed rather than being remembered as a vague seasonal disappointment.

      1. North Texas Note

      Eggplant is an extended-season North Texas solanaceous crop. Primary pests are flea beetles in spring and spider mites in summer. Primary diseases are Verticillium wilt, Alternaria/early blight-type leaf spots, Phomopsis fruit rot, damping-off, and root/stem disease where drainage fails county prevalence.

      North Texas management priority: match the crop to alkaline Blackland Prairie clay, erratic rainfall, chloraminated municipal water, and routine 100-110 F summer heat. The primary disease priorities are Verticillium wilt, Alternaria/early-blight-type leaf spots, Phomopsis fruit rot, damping-off, and root disease in wet clay. The primary pest priorities are flea beetles, spider mites, aphids, Colorado potato beetle where present, stink bugs, and caterpillars. These are not abstract lists; they determine the planting window, rotation group, irrigation method, row-cover timing, scouting route, and whether the bed should be pivoted to a different crop.

      1. Cross-References

      Disease Triangle; IPM Hierarchy; Seasonal Succession and Bed Pivoting SOP; flea beetles; spider mites; Verticillium wilt; Internal provisional chapters 29, 39, 42.

      1. Sources & Confidence Note

      Sources by class: Extension: Texas A&M Easy Gardening: Eggplant; Texas Plant Disease Handbook; NC State eggplant pest guidance; Rockwall North Central Texas planting guide. Confidence note: High confidence for heat adaptation, cold sensitivity, pest priorities, and harvest indicators; fall timing and current disease-resistant seed options need verification.

      Source classes for this entry: Extension: Texas A&M AgriLife eggplant guidance; Texas Plant Disease Handbook; NC State eggplant pest guidance; Rockwall/Tarrant guides. Practitioner doctrine: companion-planting, tonic, foliar-feed, microbial-inoculant, and input-stacking claims are not treated as mechanism unless a source class supports the mechanism directly. items remain for current seed availability, exact Phase K entry titles, and county-level pest/disease ranking.

      Tomatillo

      1. Identity Block

      Scientific name: Physalis philadelphica / Physalis ixocarpa preferred taxonomy. Family: Solanaceae. Common names: tomatillo, husk tomato, Mexican husk tomato, Mexican green tomato, miltomate. Source class: extension-supported practice for regional planting and management; peer-reviewed mechanism where specifically cited; practitioner doctrine only when explicitly labeled. Functional definition: a warm-season husked solanaceous fruit crop that requires multiple plants for reliable pollination.

      1. Composition & Properties

      Tomatillo is a sprawling frost-sensitive annual in the nightshade family. The fruit develops inside a papery husk that enlarges before the fruit fills it. Fruit are usually green but may be purple, yellow, or specialty colors depending on cultivar. Plants are indeterminate and can sprawl or root at nodes if not supported. The crop is culturally similar to tomato but biologically distinct in pollination and harvest indicator.

      For manuscript use, classify this entry as a warm-season husked solanaceous fruit crop rather than by grocery category alone. Its practical properties in a North Texas garden are defined by season, plant architecture, marketable organ, and how quickly quality is lost when the crop is mistimed. It fills the role of less common but North Texas-appropriate salsa crop that fills a tomato-like cultural slot with different pollination needs. That role matters because the same bed can only hold one crop at a time; the crop must justify its season slot, water demand, and pest-management burden.

      1. Mechanism of Action

      Tomatillo succeeds through warm-season growth, insect pollination, and fruit expansion inside the calyx. Unlike most garden tomatoes, tomatillo is not reliably self-fruitful. Multiple compatible plants must flower at the same time for consistent fruit set. Empty husks usually indicate poor pollination, heat/drought stress, insufficient plant count, or low insect activity. Tomatillo does NOT substitute agronomically for green tomato. It has its own pollination requirement, crop timing, disease profile, and husk-based maturity indicator. It also does NOT become pest-free because it is less common; flea beetles, aphids, stink bugs, hornworms, mites, and solanaceous diseases remain relevant.

      The operating mechanism is insect-mediated cross-pollination among plants followed by fruit enlargement inside a papery husk. The grower is not merely keeping a plant alive; the grower is protecting the specific developmental stage that creates the harvest. When that stage is interrupted, later vigor rarely repairs the lost yield. This is especially important in North Texas, where spring can move from cool to hot quickly and where late-summer plantings must beat the first freeze. Management should therefore begin with the calendar and the target harvest organ, then move to fertility, irrigation, and pest response.

      The primary limiter is single-plant pollination failure, drought during bloom, sprawled humid canopy, and shared solanaceous disease/pest carryover. This limiter should be handled through the Disease Triangle and IPM Hierarchy rather than through single-product thinking. A susceptible host, a viable pathogen or pest population, and favorable weather or canopy conditions produce damage. Cultural controls - timing, rotation, spacing, drip irrigation, sanitation, mulch, row cover, trellising, resistant varieties, and harvest discipline - carry more of the system than rescue sprays. Once the crop has passed the correct growth stage, a product cannot usually restore the missing developmental window.

      1. Planting and Establishment

      Spring window: use tomato-like warm-season timing, March 1-April 30 after frost risk and with warm soil. Fall window: June 20-July 15 if large transplants are available NTX fall reliability. Soil temperature: use 60 F minimum, preferably warmer tomatillo-specific threshold. Plant at least two plants, preferably three, 18-24 inches apart with cages or trellis support. Varieties include Toma Verde, Rendidora, Purple De Milpa, Purple Coban, and Pineapple. Disease-resistance packages are not commonly standardized for tomatillo claims.

      Use the planting window as a production boundary, not a casual suggestion. For North Texas, the working calendar is: spring transplant March 1-April 30 after frost; fall transplant June 20-July 15 only with strong starts and irrigation local fall reliability. The soil-temperature requirement is 60 F minimum, preferably warmer, using tomato-like transplant thresholds tomatillo-specific soil temperature. Soil temperature should be checked in the morning before sun warms the bed and should be stable, not a one-afternoon spike. When the crop is transplanted, transplant size and hardening matter; when direct-seeded, seedbed crusting and moisture continuity matter more than most amendments.

      Establishment should be built around: plant at least two, preferably three, plants; cage or trellis to keep sprawling stems open and harvestable. In Blackland clay, bed shape is a first-order practice. Raised beds, broad ridges, and controlled traffic prevent the common pattern of a crop looking acceptable aboveground while roots sit in compacted or poorly aerated soil. Drip irrigation is the default because it reduces leaf wetness, preserves soil structure, and allows water to be delivered without splashing pathogens onto lower leaves or fruit.

      Recommended and defensible varieties or variety classes include: Toma Verde, Rendidora, Purple de Milpa, Purple Coban, Pineapple, and named green or purple market types; disease-resistance claims are limited current claims. Disease-resistant variety language must be read literally. A resistance code means resistance or tolerance to a named pathogen, race, or disease complex; it does not mean general garden toughness. For manuscript lock, current seed availability, exact resistance race codes, and regional performance should be verified against the final seed-source list and county extension notes.

      1. Interactions

      Tomatillo pairs with pollinator habitat, drip irrigation, mulch, caging, and solanaceous rotation. Avoid broad-spectrum insecticide during bloom. Do not use tomatillo as a rotation break after tomato, pepper, eggplant, potato, or another tomatillo. Trellising improves airflow and harvest access and reduces fruit contact with wet soil.

      1. Contraindications & Failure Modes

      Do not plant a single tomatillo and expect reliable production. Do not direct-seed late into heavy clay and expect it to beat heat. Common failures include empty husks, late planting, poor pollination, drought during flowering, sprawl-related humidity, flea beetles, aphids, stink bugs, and foliar disease. Neem and sulfur safety limits apply.

      Do not plant this crop when the known limiter is already present and unmanaged: single-plant pollination failure, drought during bloom, sprawled humid canopy, and shared solanaceous disease/pest carryover. Do not buy diseased transplants, do not use unknown planting stock for production beds, and do not leave infected residue as surface mulch in a bed that will host a related crop. The most expensive failure is the one that carries pathogen or pest pressure forward into the next planting window.

      1. Diagnostics & Verification

      Successful plants show branching, abundant flowers, bee activity, and husks that fill with firm fruit. Harvest when fruit fills the husk and the husk begins to dry, tan, or split. Fruit often detach easily when ready. Empty husks should trigger pollination and stress diagnosis before fertilization.

      Verification starts with the crop-specific harvest indicator: harvest when fruit fills the husk and husks turn tan, dry, or split; do not wait for tomato-like red ripeness. This is the standard to use before interpreting plant size as success. A large plant without the correct harvest-stage signal is not a successful crop; it is biomass. Record planting date, soil temperature, cultivar, first flower or head date where applicable, first harvest date, and the reason the crop ended. Those notes are the basis for improving the next season.

      1. Lifecycle, Storage & Shelf Life

      Tomatillo produces continuously under warm conditions until frost, disease, drought, or heat stress ends the crop. Harvest weekly once fruit begins to mature. Store fruit in husks in a cool ventilated place or refrigerator for short periods storage duration by source.

      Lifecycle handling should follow the crop's real endpoint. Storage and postharvest rule: store in husks under cool, ventilated conditions for short periods; remove decayed husks before storage. For annual vegetables, the lifecycle also includes residue decisions. Healthy residue can be composted or used in appropriate mulch systems; diseased residue, nematode-infested roots, and pest-infested fruit should be removed or hot-composted where the composting system is capable of sanitation compost sanitation protocol.

      1. Sourcing & Quality Control

      Buy enough seed or transplants for at least two plants. Confirm days to maturity. Avoid transplants with aphids, flea beetle injury, distorted leaves, or leaf spots. Because disease codes are limited, clean seed and crop management are more important than catalog resistance claims.

      For this crop, the quality-control emphasis is the match between source and North Texas use: Toma Verde, Rendidora, Purple de Milpa, Purple Coban, Pineapple, and named green or purple market types; disease-resistance claims are limited current claims. A novelty variety can be grown as a trial, but production beds should reserve most space for adapted, documented cultivars. Keep seed lots labeled through harvest so that failures can be tied to cultivar, planting date, and bed rather than being remembered as a vague seasonal disappointment.

      1. North Texas Note

      Tomatillo is North Texas-appropriate but should be treated as a supported, pollinator-dependent solanaceous crop. Primary diseases are early blight/Alternaria-type leaf spot, anthracnose, viral diseases, and general solanaceous sanitation concerns local ranking. Primary pests are flea beetles, aphids, hornworms, stink bugs/leaffooted bugs, and spider mites.

      North Texas management priority: match the crop to alkaline Blackland Prairie clay, erratic rainfall, chloraminated municipal water, and routine 100-110 F summer heat. The primary disease priorities are early blight/Alternaria-type leaf spot, anthracnose, viral disease, damping-off, and general solanaceous sanitation issues. The primary pest priorities are flea beetles, aphids, hornworms, stink bugs, leaffooted bugs, spider mites, and cucumber beetles where present. These are not abstract lists; they determine the planting window, rotation group, irrigation method, row-cover timing, scouting route, and whether the bed should be pivoted to a different crop.

      1. Cross-References

      Disease Triangle; IPM Hierarchy; Seasonal Succession and Bed Pivoting SOP; flea beetles; aphids; early blight; Internal provisional chapters 27, 29, 39, 42.

      1. Sources & Confidence Note

      Sources by class: Extension: Texas A&M tomatillo crop guide; Utah State tomatillo pollination guidance; South Dakota State tomatillo guidance; NC State tomatillo cultivar notes; Rockwall planting guide.       Source classes for this entry: Extension: Texas A&M Aggie Horticulture tomatillo guide; Utah State tomatillo pollination guide; South Dakota State tomatillo harvest guidance; NC State plant profile.
