      Bitter Melon

      1. Identity Block

      Scientific name: Momordica charantia. Family: Cucurbitaceae. Common names: bitter melon, bitter gourd, balsam pear, karela, ampalaya. Source class: extension-supported practice for regional planting and management; peer-reviewed mechanism where specifically cited; practitioner doctrine only when explicitly labeled. Functional definition: a tropical heat-season cucurbit vine grown for firm immature fruit.

      1. Composition & Properties

      Bitter melon is an annual tropical to subtropical cucurbit with tendrils, lobed leaves, separate male and female flowers, and elongated warty or ridged fruit. Chinese types are often longer and smoother; Indian/karela types are often shorter and sharply ridged. Fruit mature from green to yellow-orange and may split to expose red seed arils. It is trellised for fruit quality and harvest access.

      For manuscript use, classify this entry as a tropical specialty cucurbit grown for immature bitter fruit rather than by grocery category alone. Its practical properties in a North Texas garden are defined by season, plant architecture, marketable organ, and how quickly quality is lost when the crop is mistimed. It fills the role of heat-season trellised specialty crop included if master list confirms; cowpea is the fallback but already appears in Session 4 master list. That role matters because the same bed can only hold one crop at a time; the crop must justify its season slot, water demand, and pest-management burden.

      1. Mechanism of Action

      Bitter melon succeeds as a heat-season specialty cucurbit. It builds vines, requires insect pollination, and produces fruit usually harvested immature while green and firm. Its advantage is heat tolerance relative to cucumber; its challenge is long-season management and uncertain local cultivar validation. Bitter melon does NOT behave like cucumber in flavor, maturity, or market acceptability. Yellow-orange fruit is botanically mature, but culinary harvest is usually earlier. It also does NOT become pest-free as a specialty crop; cucumber beetles, aphids, mites, powdery mildew, downy mildew, bacterial wilt, anthracnose, gummy stem blight, Fusarium, and viruses remain possible.

      The operating mechanism is long warm-season vine growth, separate male/female flowers, bee pollination, and repeated harvest of firm green immature fruit. The grower is not merely keeping a plant alive; the grower is protecting the specific developmental stage that creates the harvest. When that stage is interrupted, later vigor rarely repairs the lost yield. This is especially important in North Texas, where spring can move from cool to hot quickly and where late-summer plantings must beat the first freeze. Management should therefore begin with the calendar and the target harvest organ, then move to fertility, irrigation, and pest response.

      The primary limiter is scope uncertainty, long season, poor pollination, trellis failure, fruit overmaturity, and shared cucurbit disease pressure. This limiter should be handled through the Disease Triangle and IPM Hierarchy rather than through single-product thinking. A susceptible host, a viable pathogen or pest population, and favorable weather or canopy conditions produce damage. Cultural controls - timing, rotation, spacing, drip irrigation, sanitation, mulch, row cover, trellising, resistant varieties, and harvest discipline - carry more of the system than rescue sprays. Once the crop has passed the correct growth stage, a product cannot usually restore the missing developmental window.

      1. Planting and Establishment

      Spring window: use the warm cucurbit window, mid-April through May, after soil is warm exact NTX calendar. Fall start is high-risk; treat it primarily as a spring-planted summer crop. Soil temperature: use 75 F target by analogy to warm melons crop-specific threshold. Trellis strongly. Space 3-5 feet apart where vigorous. Disease-resistant varieties are not well standardized for North Texas home growers; choose reputable named Chinese, Indian, Thai, Japanese, or hybrid types by earliness, fruit type, and source.

      Use the planting window as a production boundary, not a casual suggestion. For North Texas, the working calendar is: mid-April through May after true warmth; fall production is high-risk except from July transplants North Texas calendar. The soil-temperature requirement is use 75 F practical warm-cucurbit target bitter melon threshold. Soil temperature should be checked in the morning before sun warms the bed and should be stable, not a one-afternoon spike. When the crop is transplanted, transplant size and hardening matter; when direct-seeded, seedbed crusting and moisture continuity matter more than most amendments.

      Establishment should be built around: strong trellis, drip irrigation, warm raised bed, clean cucurbit rotation, and enough room for vigorous vines. In Blackland clay, bed shape is a first-order practice. Raised beds, broad ridges, and controlled traffic prevent the common pattern of a crop looking acceptable aboveground while roots sit in compacted or poorly aerated soil. Drip irrigation is the default because it reduces leaf wetness, preserves soil structure, and allows water to be delivered without splashing pathogens onto lower leaves or fruit.

      Recommended and defensible varieties or variety classes include: Chinese long, Indian ridged/karela, Thai, Japanese, hybrid green types, and named seed-lot varieties; disease-resistant packages are not well verified for home growers cultivar list. Disease-resistant variety language must be read literally. A resistance code means resistance or tolerance to a named pathogen, race, or disease complex; it does not mean general garden toughness. For manuscript lock, current seed availability, exact resistance race codes, and regional performance should be verified against the final seed-source list and county extension notes.

      1. Interactions

      Bitter melon pairs with trellising, drip irrigation, mulch, pollinator habitat, and steady harvest. It should rotate with cucurbits, not after cucumber, squash, melon, watermelon, pumpkin, or gourds in disease beds. It requires bees unless hand-pollinated under protection. Trellising keeps fruit off wet soil and improves airflow.

      1. Contraindications & Failure Modes

      Do not plant into cold soil or grow without support unless there is abundant ground space. Do not let orange split fruit drop seed unless saving seed intentionally. Do not present medicinal claims as vegetable-production mechanism. Common failures include weak trellis, poor pollination, fruit rot on soil, spider mites, cucumber beetles, aphids, powdery mildew, downy mildew, and harvest delay. Neem/sulfur and Bt boundaries apply.

      Do not plant this crop when the known limiter is already present and unmanaged: scope uncertainty, long season, poor pollination, trellis failure, fruit overmaturity, and shared cucurbit disease pressure. Do not buy diseased transplants, do not use unknown planting stock for production beds, and do not leave infected residue as surface mulch in a bed that will host a related crop. The most expensive failure is the one that carries pathogen or pest pressure forward into the next planting window.

      1. Diagnostics & Verification

      Successful plants vine actively, attach to support, flower, attract bees, and set fruit repeatedly. Poor fruit set points to pollination failure or stress. Harvest firm young green fruit, often 4-8 inches depending on type, before yellowing and splitting. Remove field heat quickly.

      Verification starts with the crop-specific harvest indicator: harvest firm young green fruit before yellow-orange maturity and splitting. This is the standard to use before interpreting plant size as success. A large plant without the correct harvest-stage signal is not a successful crop; it is biomass. Record planting date, soil temperature, cultivar, first flower or head date where applicable, first harvest date, and the reason the crop ended. Those notes are the basis for improving the next season.

      1. Lifecycle, Storage & Shelf Life

      Lifecycle is warm seed or transplant establishment, vine growth, flowering, insect pollination, immature fruit harvest, and termination at frost. Store cool but not chilling-cold, around 12-13 C with high humidity for short periods home storage.

      Lifecycle handling should follow the crop's real endpoint. Storage and postharvest rule: short storage cool but above chilling-sensitive levels; use quickly or freeze/prep according to culinary use home storage. For annual vegetables, the lifecycle also includes residue decisions. Healthy residue can be composted or used in appropriate mulch systems; diseased residue, nematode-infested roots, and pest-infested fruit should be removed or hot-composted where the composting system is capable of sanitation compost sanitation protocol.

      1. Sourcing & Quality Control

      Use specialty seed suppliers that identify fruit type, length, days-to-harvest, and open-pollinated versus hybrid status. Avoid vague bitter gourd seed with no cultivar. Choose fast cultivars for North Texas. Do not fabricate resistance claims; require named disease resistance.

      For this crop, the quality-control emphasis is the match between source and North Texas use: Chinese long, Indian ridged/karela, Thai, Japanese, hybrid green types, and named seed-lot varieties; disease-resistant packages are not well verified for home growers cultivar list. A novelty variety can be grown as a trial, but production beds should reserve most space for adapted, documented cultivars. Keep seed lots labeled through harvest so that failures can be tied to cultivar, planting date, and bed rather than being remembered as a vague seasonal disappointment.

      1. North Texas Note

      Bitter melon is botanically appropriate for the cucurbit session, but master-list inclusion remains. It is heat-appropriate but locally less documented. Primary diseases are powdery mildew, downy mildew, Alternaria blight, bacterial wilt, anthracnose, gummy stem blight, and root-knot nematode where present. Primary pests are cucumber beetles, aphids, spider mites, squash bugs, and leafhoppers.

      North Texas management priority: match the crop to alkaline Blackland Prairie clay, erratic rainfall, chloraminated municipal water, and routine 100-110 F summer heat. The primary disease priorities are powdery mildew, downy mildew, Alternaria blight, bacterial wilt, anthracnose, gummy stem blight, Fusarium wilt, Phytophthora blight, cucumber mosaic virus, and root-knot nematode. The primary pest priorities are cucumber beetles, aphids, spider mites, squash bugs, leafhoppers, and whiteflies. These are not abstract lists; they determine the planting window, rotation group, irrigation method, row-cover timing, scouting route, and whether the bed should be pivoted to a different crop.

      1. Cross-References

      Disease Triangle; IPM Hierarchy; Seasonal Succession and Bed Pivoting SOP; powdery mildew; cucumber beetles; aphids; Internal provisional chapters 29, 39, 42.

      1. Sources & Confidence Note

      Sources by class: Extension/government: Tennessee State bitter melon fact sheet; UF/IFAS bitter melon production profile; OMAFRA bitter melon specialty crop profile; Rockwall soil-temperature table for analogous warm cucurbits. Confidence note: High confidence for cucurbit identity, heat preference, trellis need, pollination requirement, and immature harvest; North Texas scope and cultivar recommendations require verification.

      Source classes for this entry: Extension/government: Tennessee State bitter melon fact sheet; UF/IFAS bitter melon profile; OMAFRA specialty crop profile; Rockwall warm-cucurbit analog soil temperatures. Practitioner doctrine: companion-planting, tonic, foliar-feed, microbial-inoculant, and input-stacking claims are not treated as mechanism unless a source class supports the mechanism directly. items remain for current seed availability, exact Phase K entry titles, and county-level pest/disease ranking.

      Session 3 - Brassica and allium crops

      Chapter 10: Legumes, Corn, Okra, and Sweet Potato

      Cowpea

      1. Identity Block

      Source-class correction for this final module: the cowpea nitrogen-fixation mechanism in this entry is supported here by extension/government and cover-crop guidance, not by a cowpea-specific peer-reviewed citation in this document.

      Scientific name: Vigna unguiculata. Family: Fabaceae. Common names: cowpea, Southern pea, black-eyed pea, field pea, crowder pea, cream pea, purple hull pea. Source class: extension-supported practice for regional planting and management; peer-reviewed mechanism where specifically cited; practitioner doctrine only when explicitly labeled. Functional definition: a heat- and drought-tolerant warm-season legume grown for edible peas and summer cover crop biomass.

      1. Composition & Properties

      Cowpea is an annual legume with trifoliate leaves, pods, and bush to vining habits. Edible types include blackeye, cream, crowder, zipper, and purple hull. Cover-crop types such as Iron and Clay, Ace, and Red Ripper are selected for biomass and soil cover rather than table quality.

      For manuscript use, classify this entry as a warm-season Vigna legume for edible peas, forage, and cover crop rather than by grocery category alone. Its practical properties in a North Texas garden are defined by season, plant architecture, marketable organ, and how quickly quality is lost when the crop is mistimed. It fills the role of North Texas summer legume champion and dual edible/cover-crop option. That role matters because the same bed can only hold one crop at a time; the crop must justify its season slot, water demand, and pest-management burden.

      1. Mechanism of Action

      Cowpea succeeds because it is adapted to the hot season that weakens snap beans. It fixes nitrogen with compatible Bradyrhizobium and closes canopy quickly, protecting soil during summer. Cowpea does NOT directly fertilize a neighboring crop while it is actively growing; nitrogen benefit comes mainly after residues decompose. Cowpea also does NOT cure compacted clay, eliminate nematodes, or replace irrigation during pod set. Steinernema beneficial nematodes do NOT control Meloidogyne root-knot nematodes.

      The operating mechanism is hot-weather canopy growth, drought-responsive water regulation, pod production, and stronger nitrogen fixation than common bean when nodulated. The grower is not merely keeping a plant alive; the grower is protecting the specific developmental stage that creates the harvest. When that stage is interrupted, later vigor rarely repairs the lost yield. This is especially important in North Texas, where spring can move from cool to hot quickly and where late-summer plantings must beat the first freeze. Management should therefore begin with the calendar and the target harvest organ, then move to fertility, irrigation, and pest response.

      The primary limiter is cold wet soil, waterlogging, excess nitrogen, stink bugs/leaffooted bugs, cowpea curculio, viruses, and root-knot nematode. This limiter should be handled through the Disease Triangle and IPM Hierarchy rather than through single-product thinking. A susceptible host, a viable pathogen or pest population, and favorable weather or canopy conditions produce damage. Cultural controls - timing, rotation, spacing, drip irrigation, sanitation, mulch, row cover, trellising, resistant varieties, and harvest discipline - carry more of the system than rescue sprays. Once the crop has passed the correct growth stage, a product cannot usually restore the missing developmental window.

      1. Planting and Establishment

      North Texas spring/summer edible-crop window: plant cowpea / black-eyed peas after frost danger has passed and soil has warmed above 65 F. In North Texas practice, edible cowpeas are normally planted from April through May for early summer production, with additional summer plantings possible where there is enough time for the intended harvest stage. Final county calendars differ in whether they label the crop black-eyed pea, Southern pea, or cowpea. final county-specific cowpea / Southern pea planting window by master calendar.

      Edible spring window: March 30-April 30 or April 1-May 20. Late-summer/fall window: July 1-August 1 for harvest; August 15-September 1 mostly for fast types or cover. Soil temperature: 65 F. Plant 0.75-1 inch deep. Edible varieties: Blackeye #5, Mississippi Silver, Purple Hull, Zipper Cream Crowder, Colossus Crowder, Texas Cream 40, Pinkeye Purple Hull BVR code. Cover types: Iron and Clay, Ace, Red Ripper. Disease-resistant choices include Mississippi Silver and Mississippi Purple for BICMV/Fusarium resistance.

      Use the planting window as a production boundary, not a casual suggestion. For North Texas, the working calendar is: edible spring March 30-May 20; late summer July 1-August 1 preferred for harvest, with August 15-September 1 only for fast/cover uses cultivar maturity. The soil-temperature requirement is 65 F minimum. Soil temperature should be checked in the morning before sun warms the bed and should be stable, not a one-afternoon spike. When the crop is transplanted, transplant size and hardening matter; when direct-seeded, seedbed crusting and moisture continuity matter more than most amendments.

      Establishment should be built around: direct seed, inoculate with cowpea-compatible Bradyrhizobium where needed, maintain moisture through bloom/pod set, and avoid high nitrogen. In Blackland clay, bed shape is a first-order practice. Raised beds, broad ridges, and controlled traffic prevent the common pattern of a crop looking acceptable aboveground while roots sit in compacted or poorly aerated soil. Drip irrigation is the default because it reduces leaf wetness, preserves soil structure, and allows water to be delivered without splashing pathogens onto lower leaves or fruit.

      Recommended and defensible varieties or variety classes include: Blackeye #5, Mississippi Silver, Purple Hull, Zipper Cream Crowder, Colossus Crowder, Texas Cream 40, Pinkeye Purple Hull BVR, Iron and Clay, Ace, Red Ripper BVR code and edible/cover purpose. Disease-resistant variety language must be read literally. A resistance code means resistance or tolerance to a named pathogen, race, or disease complex; it does not mean general garden toughness. For manuscript lock, current seed availability, exact resistance race codes, and regional performance should be verified against the final seed-source list and county extension notes.

      1. Interactions

      Cowpea pairs with hot vacant beds after garlic, onion, spring beans, or early corn. It can be edible and cover crop if biomass expectations are adjusted. It dislikes waterlogging and excess nitrogen. It can compete with adjacent crops and climb corn if unmanaged.

      1. Contraindications & Failure Modes

      Do not plant into cold wet soil or low clay pockets. Do not recommend dolomitic lime without a soil test; North Texas soils are often alkaline and high in Mg. Failures include seed rot, excess vine from nitrogen, stink bugs, leaffooted bugs, cowpea curculio, aphids, mites, viruses, Fusarium, rust, powdery mildew, and root-knot nematode.

      Do not plant this crop when the known limiter is already present and unmanaged: cold wet soil, waterlogging, excess nitrogen, stink bugs/leaffooted bugs, cowpea curculio, viruses, and root-knot nematode. Do not buy diseased transplants, do not use unknown planting stock for production beds, and do not leave infected residue as surface mulch in a bed that will host a related crop. The most expensive failure is the one that carries pathogen or pest pressure forward into the next planting window.

      1. Diagnostics & Verification

      Successful cowpea emerges rapidly, branches/vines, and covers soil. Inspect roots for nodules if nitrogen fixation matters. Harvest snaps young, shelling peas when filled but fresh, and dry seed when pods are mature and dry. Scout pods for stink bug and curculio injury.

      Verification starts with the crop-specific harvest indicator: snaps young; shelling peas filled but fresh; dry seed when pods dry fully. This is the standard to use before interpreting plant size as success. A large plant without the correct harvest-stage signal is not a successful crop; it is biomass. Record planting date, soil temperature, cultivar, first flower or head date where applicable, first harvest date, and the reason the crop ended. Those notes are the basis for improving the next season.

      1. Lifecycle, Storage & Shelf Life

      Lifecycle is warm direct seeding, canopy, flowering, pod set, harvest or biomass accumulation, termination, and residue decomposition. Edible types often take 60-95 days. Fresh peas store cold for short periods; dry seed must be fully dried moisture target.

      Lifecycle handling should follow the crop's real endpoint. Storage and postharvest rule: fresh peas cool quickly; dry seed must be fully dried and protected from weevils home moisture target. For annual vegetables, the lifecycle also includes residue decisions. Healthy residue can be composted or used in appropriate mulch systems; diseased residue, nematode-infested roots, and pest-infested fruit should be removed or hot-composted where the composting system is capable of sanitation compost sanitation protocol.

      1. Sourcing & Quality Control

      Buy named edible varieties for food and cover-crop varieties for biomass. Use cowpea-compatible Bradyrhizobium inoculant where beds have not grown cowpeas recently inoculant group. Avoid moldy, insect-damaged, or unlabeled seed.

      For this crop, the quality-control emphasis is the match between source and North Texas use: Blackeye #5, Mississippi Silver, Purple Hull, Zipper Cream Crowder, Colossus Crowder, Texas Cream 40, Pinkeye Purple Hull BVR, Iron and Clay, Ace, Red Ripper BVR code and edible/cover purpose. A novelty variety can be grown as a trial, but production beds should reserve most space for adapted, documented cultivars. Keep seed lots labeled through harvest so that failures can be tied to cultivar, planting date, and bed rather than being remembered as a vague seasonal disappointment.

      1. North Texas Note

      North Texas summer legume workhorse. Primary diseases: Fusarium wilt, cowpea mosaic virus complex, rust, powdery mildew, damping-off, root-knot nematode. Primary pests: stink bugs, leaffooted bugs, cowpea curculio, aphids, bean leaf beetles, spider mites.

      North Texas management priority: match the crop to alkaline Blackland Prairie clay, erratic rainfall, chloraminated municipal water, and routine 100-110 F summer heat. The primary disease priorities are Fusarium wilt, cowpea mosaic virus complex, rust, powdery mildew, damping-off, and root-knot nematode. The primary pest priorities are stink bugs, leaffooted bugs, cowpea curculio, aphids, bean leaf beetles, spider mites, and soil pests. These are not abstract lists; they determine the planting window, rotation group, irrigation method, row-cover timing, scouting route, and whether the bed should be pivoted to a different crop.

      1. Cross-References

      Disease Triangle; IPM Hierarchy; Seasonal Succession and Bed Pivoting SOP; Nitrogen Fixation; Summer Cover Crops; cowpea curculio; stink bugs; Internal provisional chapters 29, 39, 42.

      1. Sources & Confidence Note

      Sources by class: Extension/government: Rockwall/Tarrant guides; Texas A&M Southern pea; Texas A&M Ace cowpea release; Clemson pea diseases; NC State pea pests; USDA-NRCS cowpea fact sheet; Southern Cover Crops Council. Confidence note: High confidence for heat tolerance, 65 F soil, edible/cover use, and nitrogen-fixation framing; local pest ranking and BVR code need verification.

      Source classes for this entry: Extension/government: Texas A&M Southern Pea guide; USDA-NRCS cowpea fact sheet; Southern Cover Crops Council Blacklands cowpea sheet; Clemson bean/southern pea diseases; NC State pests. Peer-reviewed: cowpea drought physiology DOI. Practitioner doctrine: companion-planting, tonic, foliar-feed, microbial-inoculant, and input-stacking claims are not treated as mechanism unless a source class supports the mechanism directly. items remain for current seed availability, exact Phase K entry titles, and county-level pest/disease ranking.

      Bean (Bush and Pole)

      1. Identity Block

      Scientific name: Phaseolus vulgaris primarily. Family: Fabaceae. Common names: green bean, snap bean, string bean, bush bean, pole bean, wax bean, Romano bean. Source class: extension-supported practice for regional planting and management; peer-reviewed mechanism where specifically cited; practitioner doctrine only when explicitly labeled. Functional definition: a warm-season legume grown for tender pods, shelling beans, or dry seed.

      1. Composition & Properties

      Bush beans produce a concentrated crop on compact plants; pole beans climb and produce longer. Snap beans are harvested immature; shell and dry beans mature later. Common beans self-pollinate and form rhizobial nodules, but they are weaker nitrogen fixers than cowpeas and many forage legumes.

      For manuscript use, classify this entry as a warm-season annual legume, primarily Phaseolus vulgaris rather than by grocery category alone. Its practical properties in a North Texas garden are defined by season, plant architecture, marketable organ, and how quickly quality is lost when the crop is mistimed. It fills the role of two-window snap, shelling, or dry bean crop with bush/pole growth-habit choices. That role matters because the same bed can only hold one crop at a time; the crop must justify its season slot, water demand, and pest-management burden.

      1. Mechanism of Action

      Beans succeed through warm-soil establishment, vegetative canopy, self-pollinated flowers, and pod fill. They can fix nitrogen with compatible rhizobia, but beans do NOT feed the next crop during active pod production in the simple way often claimed. Most fixed nitrogen is used by the bean plant; following crops benefit after residues and nodules decompose. Beans also do NOT remove the need for fertility management.

      The operating mechanism is warm-soil emergence, self-pollinated flowers, pod fill, and modest nitrogen fixation through compatible rhizobia. The grower is not merely keeping a plant alive; the grower is protecting the specific developmental stage that creates the harvest. When that stage is interrupted, later vigor rarely repairs the lost yield. This is especially important in North Texas, where spring can move from cool to hot quickly and where late-summer plantings must beat the first freeze. Management should therefore begin with the calendar and the target harvest organ, then move to fertility, irrigation, and pest response.

      The primary limiter is cold wet soil, heat during bloom, excess nitrogen, bacterial blights, rust, mites, and pod-feeding insects. This limiter should be handled through the Disease Triangle and IPM Hierarchy rather than through single-product thinking. A susceptible host, a viable pathogen or pest population, and favorable weather or canopy conditions produce damage. Cultural controls - timing, rotation, spacing, drip irrigation, sanitation, mulch, row cover, trellising, resistant varieties, and harvest discipline - carry more of the system than rescue sprays. Once the crop has passed the correct growth stage, a product cannot usually restore the missing developmental window.

      1. Planting and Establishment

      Spring window: March 18-April 15, with broader March 20-May 1 for bush beans and March 20-April 20 for pole beans. Fall window: August 1-September 15 for bush beans; July 30-August 10 or August 1-August 15 for pole beans. Soil temperature: 60 F for snap beans; 70 F for lima beans. Varieties: Blue Lake 274, Top Crop, Tendercrop, Contender, Tendergreen, Derby, Kentucky Wonder Bush, Blue Lake pole, Kentucky Wonder. Disease-resistant options include Provider, Top Crop, Derby, Contender, Tendercrop, Tendergreen, and Kentucky Wonder lines with BCMV/rust packages.

      Use the planting window as a production boundary, not a casual suggestion. For North Texas, the working calendar is: spring March 18-April 15/May 1; fall August 1-September 15 for bush and July 30-August 15 for pole depending maturity. The soil-temperature requirement is 60 F minimum for snap beans and 70 F for lima beans; 69 F at 4 inches reduces seedling disease risk in some guidance. Soil temperature should be checked in the morning before sun warms the bed and should be stable, not a one-afternoon spike. When the crop is transplanted, transplant size and hardening matter; when direct-seeded, seedbed crusting and moisture continuity matter more than most amendments.

      Establishment should be built around: direct seed into warm crumbly beds, shallow cultivation, trellis pole beans before vines run, and avoid heavy nitrogen. In Blackland clay, bed shape is a first-order practice. Raised beds, broad ridges, and controlled traffic prevent the common pattern of a crop looking acceptable aboveground while roots sit in compacted or poorly aerated soil. Drip irrigation is the default because it reduces leaf wetness, preserves soil structure, and allows water to be delivered without splashing pathogens onto lower leaves or fruit.

      Recommended and defensible varieties or variety classes include: Blue Lake 274, Top Crop, Tendercrop, Contender, Tendergreen, Derby, Kentucky Wonder Bush, Blue Lake pole, Kentucky Wonder pole, Provider, and disease-coded BCMV/rust-resistant types package. Disease-resistant variety language must be read literally. A resistance code means resistance or tolerance to a named pathogen, race, or disease complex; it does not mean general garden toughness. For manuscript lock, current seed availability, exact resistance race codes, and regional performance should be verified against the final seed-source list and county extension notes.

      1. Interactions

      Beans pair with drip irrigation, warm soil, shallow cultivation, moderate fertility, and quick bed turnover. They dislike soggy clay, overhead watering, and heavy nitrogen. If inoculants are used, use the correct rhizobial group, not ectomycorrhizal products.

      1. Contraindications & Failure Modes

      Do not plant into cold saturated clay or disease-heavy bean residue. Failures include bloom during extreme heat, excess nitrogen, spider mites, aphids, bean leaf beetles, stink bugs, bacterial blights, rust, root rots, anthracnose, powdery mildew, and viruses. Bt is only for small susceptible caterpillars; neem/sulfur safety applies.

      Do not plant this crop when the known limiter is already present and unmanaged: cold wet soil, heat during bloom, excess nitrogen, bacterial blights, rust, mites, and pod-feeding insects. Do not buy diseased transplants, do not use unknown planting stock for production beds, and do not leave infected residue as surface mulch in a bed that will host a related crop. The most expensive failure is the one that carries pathogen or pest pressure forward into the next planting window.

      1. Diagnostics & Verification

      Good stands emerge evenly, flower before severe heat, and set tender pods. If pod set is poor, check heat, moisture during bloom, and excess nitrogen. Harvest snap beans when pencil-sized, tender, and before seeds enlarge. Pick frequently to maintain production.

      Verification starts with the crop-specific harvest indicator: snap beans when pods are pencil-size, tender, and before seed swelling; pick frequently. This is the standard to use before interpreting plant size as success. A large plant without the correct harvest-stage signal is not a successful crop; it is biomass. Record planting date, soil temperature, cultivar, first flower or head date where applicable, first harvest date, and the reason the crop ended. Those notes are the basis for improving the next season.

      1. Lifecycle, Storage & Shelf Life

      Bush beans mature in 45-60 days; pole beans 60-70; limas longer. Fresh beans store about a week refrigerated. Do not save seed from diseased plants; seedborne diseases are a risk.

      Lifecycle handling should follow the crop's real endpoint. Storage and postharvest rule: fresh pods about a week refrigerated; dry seed only after full maturity and drying moisture target. For annual vegetables, the lifecycle also includes residue decisions. Healthy residue can be composted or used in appropriate mulch systems; diseased residue, nematode-infested roots, and pest-infested fruit should be removed or hot-composted where the composting system is capable of sanitation compost sanitation protocol.

      1. Sourcing & Quality Control

      Buy fresh seed with cultivar, growth habit, maturity, and disease package. For pole beans, confirm trellis capacity and harvest duration. Inoculants must be alive, stored cool, unexpired, and labeled for the host legume.

      For this crop, the quality-control emphasis is the match between source and North Texas use: Blue Lake 274, Top Crop, Tendercrop, Contender, Tendergreen, Derby, Kentucky Wonder Bush, Blue Lake pole, Kentucky Wonder pole, Provider, and disease-coded BCMV/rust-resistant types package. A novelty variety can be grown as a trial, but production beds should reserve most space for adapted, documented cultivars. Keep seed lots labeled through harvest so that failures can be tied to cultivar, planting date, and bed rather than being remembered as a vague seasonal disappointment.

      1. North Texas Note

      Two-window crop, not a deep-summer champion. Primary diseases: common bacterial blight, halo blight, rust, anthracnose, root rots, powdery mildew, mosaic viruses. Primary pests: bean leaf beetles, aphids, spider mites, stink bugs/leaffooted bugs, caterpillars.

      North Texas management priority: match the crop to alkaline Blackland Prairie clay, erratic rainfall, chloraminated municipal water, and routine 100-110 F summer heat. The primary disease priorities are common bacterial blight, halo blight, anthracnose, rust, root rots, powdery mildew, and mosaic viruses. The primary pest priorities are bean leaf beetles, aphids, spider mites, stink bugs, leaffooted bugs, cutworms, and caterpillars. These are not abstract lists; they determine the planting window, rotation group, irrigation method, row-cover timing, scouting route, and whether the bed should be pivoted to a different crop.

      1. Cross-References

      Disease Triangle; IPM Hierarchy; Seasonal Succession and Bed Pivoting SOP; Nitrogen Fixation; bean bacterial blights; Internal provisional chapters 29, 39, 42.

      1. Sources & Confidence Note

      Peer-reviewed: Reinprecht, Y. et al. 2020. Effects of Nitrogen Application on Nitrogen Fixation in Common Bean Production. Frontiers in Plant Science. DOI: 10.3389/fpls.2020.01172, for common bean's weak relative nitrogen fixation and the suppressive effect of nitrogen fertilizer on symbiotic nitrogen fixation.

      Sources by class: Extension: Rockwall/Tarrant guides; Texas A&M green beans; Clemson bean diseases; NC State bean pests; UC IPM nitrogen; NMSU nitrogen fixation; Cornell bean varieties. Peer-reviewed: common bean nitrogen fixation literature final DOI. Confidence note: High confidence for windows, soil thresholds, harvest indicators, and weak nitrogen-fixation caution; current seed packages and inoculant group need verification.

      Source classes for this entry: Extension/government: Texas A&M green bean guide; Rockwall/Tarrant guides; Clemson bean diseases; NC State pests of beans and peas; UC IPM nitrogen budget; Cornell disease-resistant bean list. Peer-reviewed: common bean nitrogen fixation literature DOI. Practitioner doctrine: companion-planting, tonic, foliar-feed, microbial-inoculant, and input-stacking claims are not treated as mechanism unless a source class supports the mechanism directly. items remain for current seed availability, exact Phase K entry titles, and county-level pest/disease ranking.

      Sweet Corn

      1. Identity Block

      Scientific name: Zea mays var. saccharata and related sweet corn classes. Family: Poaceae. Common names: sweet corn, table corn, roasting ears, sugar corn. Source class: extension-supported practice for regional planting and management; peer-reviewed mechanism where specifically cited; practitioner doctrine only when explicitly labeled. Functional definition: a warm-season grass crop grown for immature ears and dependent on wind pollination.

      1. Composition & Properties

      Sweet corn is an annual grass with fibrous roots, tassels as male flowers, and ears as female flowers. Each silk connects to one potential kernel. Sweet-corn classes include su, se, sh2, synergistic, and augmented types. Class affects sweetness, seed vigor, and isolation needs.

      For manuscript use, classify this entry as a warm-season annual grass harvested at immature milk stage rather than by grocery category alone. Its practical properties in a North Texas garden are defined by season, plant architecture, marketable organ, and how quickly quality is lost when the crop is mistimed. It fills the role of block-planted pollination crop with high wildlife and earworm pressure. That role matters because the same bed can only hold one crop at a time; the crop must justify its season slot, water demand, and pest-management burden.

      1. Mechanism of Action

      Sweet corn succeeds by building leaf area and stalk strength, then tasseling, silking, pollinating, and filling kernels. It is wind-pollinated; block planting improves pollen distribution. Sweet corn does NOT set complete ears simply because stalks are tall and green. Drought during silking, poor block geometry, silk clipping, or heat can cause blank kernels. Corn earworm lays eggs on fresh silks; once larvae enter the ear, control is effectively lost. Bt does NOT rescue larvae already inside ears.

      The operating mechanism is leaf/stalk growth, tassel pollen shed, silk receptivity, kernel pollination, and milk-stage harvest. The grower is not merely keeping a plant alive; the grower is protecting the specific developmental stage that creates the harvest. When that stage is interrupted, later vigor rarely repairs the lost yield. This is especially important in North Texas, where spring can move from cool to hot quickly and where late-summer plantings must beat the first freeze. Management should therefore begin with the calendar and the target harvest organ, then move to fertility, irrigation, and pest response.

      The primary limiter is single-row planting, drought at silking/kernel fill, corn earworm, raccoons, birds, cold soil, and late fall timing. This limiter should be handled through the Disease Triangle and IPM Hierarchy rather than through single-product thinking. A susceptible host, a viable pathogen or pest population, and favorable weather or canopy conditions produce damage. Cultural controls - timing, rotation, spacing, drip irrigation, sanitation, mulch, row cover, trellising, resistant varieties, and harvest discipline - carry more of the system than rescue sprays. Once the crop has passed the correct growth stage, a product cannot usually restore the missing developmental window.

      1. Planting and Establishment

      Spring window: March 15-April 30 or March 20-May 1. Fall window: early August only; one local row appears to contain a date typo. Soil temperature: 65 F. Plant 1 inch deep in blocks, not single rows; thin to about 1 foot apart. Local varieties: Golden Queen, Guadalupe Gold, Sweet G-90, Honey & Pearl, Silver Queen, Frontier. Disease-resistant options include Golden Queen, Honey N Pearl, Silver Queen, Bodacious RM, Avalon, Incredible RM, Kandy Korn, Luscious TSW, Primus, Silver King, and Temptation.

      Use the planting window as a production boundary, not a casual suggestion. For North Texas, the working calendar is: spring March 15-April 30/May 1; fall early August only, with Rockwall table typo needing verification fall date. The soil-temperature requirement is 65 F minimum for seed. Soil temperature should be checked in the morning before sun warms the bed and should be stable, not a one-afternoon spike. When the crop is transplanted, transplant size and hardening matter; when direct-seeded, seedbed crusting and moisture continuity matter more than most amendments.

      Establishment should be built around: plant in blocks of several short rows, keep north of low crops, irrigate through silking, and isolate incompatible sweet-corn classes isolation interval. In Blackland clay, bed shape is a first-order practice. Raised beds, broad ridges, and controlled traffic prevent the common pattern of a crop looking acceptable aboveground while roots sit in compacted or poorly aerated soil. Drip irrigation is the default because it reduces leaf wetness, preserves soil structure, and allows water to be delivered without splashing pathogens onto lower leaves or fruit.

      Recommended and defensible varieties or variety classes include: Golden Queen, Guadalupe Gold, Sweet G-90, Honey & Pearl, Silver Queen, Frontier, Bodacious RM, Avalon, Incredible RM, Kandy Korn, Luscious, Silver King, Temptation seed class and availability. Disease-resistant variety language must be read literally. A resistance code means resistance or tolerance to a named pathogen, race, or disease complex; it does not mean general garden toughness. For manuscript lock, current seed availability, exact resistance race codes, and regional performance should be verified against the final seed-source list and county extension notes.

      1. Interactions

      Corn pairs with block geometry, drip irrigation, shallow cultivation, and north-side garden placement to avoid shading. It is a heavier feeder and does not fix nitrogen. Three Sisters planting is not a same-season fertilizer shortcut. Corn interacts strongly with wildlife; raccoon exclusion must be installed before milk stage.

      1. Contraindications & Failure Modes

      Do not plant one or two long rows and expect full ears. Do not plant in cold wet clay or late without earworm and wildlife plans. Failures include poor pollination, drought during silking, lodging, seed rot, corn earworm, fall armyworm, flea beetles, grasshoppers, common smut, rusts, leaf blights, birds, and raccoons.

      Do not plant this crop when the known limiter is already present and unmanaged: single-row planting, drought at silking/kernel fill, corn earworm, raccoons, birds, cold soil, and late fall timing. Do not buy diseased transplants, do not use unknown planting stock for production beds, and do not leave infected residue as surface mulch in a bed that will host a related crop. The most expensive failure is the one that carries pathogen or pest pressure forward into the next planting window.

      1. Diagnostics & Verification

      Good stands are even, tassel while vigorous, and silk while pollen sheds. Blank kernels indicate pollination or silk injury problems. Harvest about three weeks after tassels appear, when silks are brown, ears are firm, tip kernels are plump, and kernel juice is milky white.

      Verification starts with the crop-specific harvest indicator: about three weeks after tasseling, when silks are brown, ears firm, kernels plump, and kernel juice milky. This is the standard to use before interpreting plant size as success. A large plant without the correct harvest-stage signal is not a successful crop; it is biomass. Record planting date, soil temperature, cultivar, first flower or head date where applicable, first harvest date, and the reason the crop ended. Those notes are the basis for improving the next season.

      1. Lifecycle, Storage & Shelf Life

      Lifecycle is germination, vegetative growth, tasseling, silking, pollination, kernel fill, milk stage harvest, and residue management. Local maturity is about 70-90 days. Sweetness declines quickly after harvest; cool immediately and use within 1-2 days for best quality.

      Lifecycle handling should follow the crop's real endpoint. Storage and postharvest rule: quality declines rapidly; refrigerate and use within 1-2 days for best sweetness. For annual vegetables, the lifecycle also includes residue decisions. Healthy residue can be composted or used in appropriate mulch systems; diseased residue, nematode-infested roots, and pest-infested fruit should be removed or hot-composted where the composting system is capable of sanitation compost sanitation protocol.

      1. Sourcing & Quality Control

      Buy fresh named seed by genetic class, days, and disease package. Do not save seed for reliable production. Keep incompatible classes isolated by distance or planting interval interval. Supersweet seed often needs warmer, better-drained seedbeds.

      For this crop, the quality-control emphasis is the match between source and North Texas use: Golden Queen, Guadalupe Gold, Sweet G-90, Honey & Pearl, Silver Queen, Frontier, Bodacious RM, Avalon, Incredible RM, Kandy Korn, Luscious, Silver King, Temptation seed class and availability. A novelty variety can be grown as a trial, but production beds should reserve most space for adapted, documented cultivars. Keep seed lots labeled through harvest so that failures can be tied to cultivar, planting date, and bed rather than being remembered as a vague seasonal disappointment.

      1. North Texas Note

      Possible but not low-input. Primary diseases: common smut, seedling blights, common rust, leaf blights, and low-frequency Stewart’s wilt. Primary pests: corn earworm, fall armyworm, grasshoppers, flea beetles, birds, and raccoons.

      North Texas management priority: match the crop to alkaline Blackland Prairie clay, erratic rainfall, chloraminated municipal water, and routine 100-110 F summer heat. The primary disease priorities are common smut, seedling blights, common rust, northern/southern leaf blights, and Stewart wilt as a resistance-coded but low-frequency Texas issue. The primary pest priorities are corn earworm, fall armyworm, European corn borer where present, flea beetles, grasshoppers, birds, and raccoons. These are not abstract lists; they determine the planting window, rotation group, irrigation method, row-cover timing, scouting route, and whether the bed should be pivoted to a different crop.

      1. Cross-References

      Disease Triangle; IPM Hierarchy; Seasonal Succession and Bed Pivoting SOP; corn earworm; wildlife exclusion; Internal provisional chapters 29, 39, 42.

      1. Sources & Confidence Note

      Sources by class: Extension: Rockwall/Tarrant guides; Texas A&M sweet corn; Texas Plant Disease Handbook; University of Kentucky corn earworm; Iowa State raccoon exclusion; Cornell sweet corn varieties. Confidence note: High confidence for 65 F soil, block pollination, harvest indicators, and earworm timing; class isolation and local fall feasibility need verification.

      Okra

      1. Identity Block

      Scientific name: Abelmoschus esculentus. Family: Malvaceae. Common names: okra, gumbo, lady’s finger, bhindi. Source class: extension-supported practice for regional planting and management; peer-reviewed mechanism where specifically cited; practitioner doctrine only when explicitly labeled. Functional definition: a heat-loving annual grown for immature pods and one of North Texas most dependable summer crops.

      1. Composition & Properties

      Okra is an upright warm-season annual with palmate leaves, hibiscus-like flowers, and elongated pods that become fibrous when old. It is related to hibiscus and cotton, not legumes. Varieties range from compact types to tall branching heirlooms. Common choices include Clemson Spineless, Emerald, Lee, Blondy, Annie Oakley, Burgundy, Cajun Delight, Louisiana Green, and Stewart’s Zeebest.

      For manuscript use, classify this entry as a heat-loving annual pod vegetable rather than by grocery category alone. Its practical properties in a North Texas garden are defined by season, plant architecture, marketable organ, and how quickly quality is lost when the crop is mistimed. It fills the role of North Texas summer anchor crop for extreme heat productivity. That role matters because the same bed can only hold one crop at a time; the crop must justify its season slot, water demand, and pest-management burden.

      1. Mechanism of Action

      Okra succeeds because it continues vegetative growth, flowering, and pod production in heat that stops many vegetables. Flowers are generally self-fertile enough for normal home production cultivar exceptions. Okra does NOT become drought-proof because it is heat-tolerant. Survival is not the target; tender frequent pods require steady moisture. Okra also does NOT have a verified root-knot-resistant cultivar option in Texas Plant Disease Handbook guidance.

      The operating mechanism is warm-soil establishment, upright biomass, repeated flowering, and rapid pod expansion in high heat. The grower is not merely keeping a plant alive; the grower is protecting the specific developmental stage that creates the harvest. When that stage is interrupted, later vigor rarely repairs the lost yield. This is especially important in North Texas, where spring can move from cool to hot quickly and where late-summer plantings must beat the first freeze. Management should therefore begin with the calendar and the target harvest organ, then move to fertility, irrigation, and pest response.

      The primary limiter is cold soil, root-knot nematode, harvest neglect, stink bugs/leaffooted bugs, aphids, and spider mites. This limiter should be handled through the Disease Triangle and IPM Hierarchy rather than through single-product thinking. A susceptible host, a viable pathogen or pest population, and favorable weather or canopy conditions produce damage. Cultural controls - timing, rotation, spacing, drip irrigation, sanitation, mulch, row cover, trellising, resistant varieties, and harvest discipline - carry more of the system than rescue sprays. Once the crop has passed the correct growth stage, a product cannot usually restore the missing developmental window.

      1. Planting and Establishment

      Spring window: April 1-April 30, with broader April 5-June 1. Fall window: July 1-August 25, but July 15-August 15 is safer for fall harvest cultivar cutoff. Soil temperature: 75 F. Plant seed about 1 inch deep and thin to about 1 foot apart; rows commonly 3 feet apart. Prepare soil 8-10 inches deep and use raised beds in clay. Varieties listed above are adaptation choices, not root-knot-resistant choices unless independently documented.

      Use the planting window as a production boundary, not a casual suggestion. For North Texas, the working calendar is: spring April 1-June 1 depending soil warmth; fall July 1-August 25 but July 15-August 15 is more realistic for harvest cultivar cutoff. The soil-temperature requirement is 75 F minimum for seed. Soil temperature should be checked in the morning before sun warms the bed and should be stable, not a one-afternoon spike. When the crop is transplanted, transplant size and hardening matter; when direct-seeded, seedbed crusting and moisture continuity matter more than most amendments.

      Establishment should be built around: direct seed 1 inch deep in warm raised beds, thin to about 1 foot, irrigate every 7-10 days in dry spells, and harvest frequently. In Blackland clay, bed shape is a first-order practice. Raised beds, broad ridges, and controlled traffic prevent the common pattern of a crop looking acceptable aboveground while roots sit in compacted or poorly aerated soil. Drip irrigation is the default because it reduces leaf wetness, preserves soil structure, and allows water to be delivered without splashing pathogens onto lower leaves or fruit.

      Recommended and defensible varieties or variety classes include: Annie Oakley, Blondy, Burgundy, Cajun Delight, Clemson Spineless, Emerald, Lee, Louisiana Green, Stewart Zeebest, Velvet; no verified root-knot-resistant okra in Texas guidance current claims. Disease-resistant variety language must be read literally. A resistance code means resistance or tolerance to a named pathogen, race, or disease complex; it does not mean general garden toughness. For manuscript lock, current seed availability, exact resistance race codes, and regional performance should be verified against the final seed-source list and county extension notes.

      1. Interactions

      Okra pairs with summer bed pivots after spring crops, drip irrigation, mulch after soil warms, and wide paths for harvest. It interacts strongly with harvest labor: pick every 1-2 days or pods become woody and yield declines. Avoid known severe root-knot beds. Steinernema beneficial nematodes do NOT control Meloidogyne root-knot nematodes.

      1. Contraindications & Failure Modes

      Do not plant into cold soil or known severe root-knot beds. Do not leave pods because it is hot outside. Failures include poor germination, seedling disease, root-knot nematode, aphids, stink bugs, leaffooted bugs, spider mites, and pod-feeding caterpillars. Bt only targets small susceptible caterpillars; neem/sulfur safety rules apply.

      Do not plant this crop when the known limiter is already present and unmanaged: cold soil, root-knot nematode, harvest neglect, stink bugs/leaffooted bugs, aphids, and spider mites. Do not buy diseased transplants, do not use unknown planting stock for production beds, and do not leave infected residue as surface mulch in a bed that will host a related crop. The most expensive failure is the one that carries pathogen or pest pressure forward into the next planting window.

      1. Diagnostics & Verification

      Healthy okra emerges evenly, grows upright, and flowers about two months after planting. Harvest pods at 3-4 inches unless cultivar stays tender larger. Root-knot diagnosis requires galled roots; aboveground wilting alone is not enough. Curled or warted pods indicate stink bug/leaffooted bug feeding.

      Verification starts with the crop-specific harvest indicator: pick 3-4 inch pods every 1-2 days unless variety remains tender longer. This is the standard to use before interpreting plant size as success. A large plant without the correct harvest-stage signal is not a successful crop; it is biomass. Record planting date, soil temperature, cultivar, first flower or head date where applicable, first harvest date, and the reason the crop ended. Those notes are the basis for improving the next season.

      1. Lifecycle, Storage & Shelf Life

      Lifecycle is warm-soil seeding, vegetative growth, flowering, repeated pod harvest, optional rejuvenation protocol, and frost termination. Maturity often 55-65 days. Store pods 3-5 days refrigerated.

      Lifecycle handling should follow the crop's real endpoint. Storage and postharvest rule: 3-5 days refrigerated; handle gently and do not wash until use if storage is needed. For annual vegetables, the lifecycle also includes residue decisions. Healthy residue can be composted or used in appropriate mulch systems; diseased residue, nematode-infested roots, and pest-infested fruit should be removed or hot-composted where the composting system is capable of sanitation compost sanitation protocol.

      1. Sourcing & Quality Control

      Buy seed by named variety, plant height, pod tenderness, and days. Compact types fit small beds; Clemson Spineless and Emerald remain practical choices. Do not accept root-knot resistance claims without specific documentation.

      For this crop, the quality-control emphasis is the match between source and North Texas use: Annie Oakley, Blondy, Burgundy, Cajun Delight, Clemson Spineless, Emerald, Lee, Louisiana Green, Stewart Zeebest, Velvet; no verified root-knot-resistant okra in Texas guidance current claims. A novelty variety can be grown as a trial, but production beds should reserve most space for adapted, documented cultivars. Keep seed lots labeled through harvest so that failures can be tied to cultivar, planting date, and bed rather than being remembered as a vague seasonal disappointment.

      1. North Texas Note

      North Texas summer anchor crop. Primary disease: root-knot nematode; secondary issues include damping-off and leaf spots. Primary pests: stink bugs, leaffooted bugs, aphids, spider mites, and occasional caterpillars.

      North Texas management priority: match the crop to alkaline Blackland Prairie clay, erratic rainfall, chloraminated municipal water, and routine 100-110 F summer heat. The primary disease priorities are root-knot nematode, damping-off in wet seedbeds, leaf spots during damp weather, and root/stem rots in wet clay. The primary pest priorities are stink bugs, leaffooted bugs, aphids, spider mites, flea beetles, and pod-feeding caterpillars. These are not abstract lists; they determine the planting window, rotation group, irrigation method, row-cover timing, scouting route, and whether the bed should be pivoted to a different crop.

      1. Cross-References

      Disease Triangle; IPM Hierarchy; Seasonal Succession and Bed Pivoting SOP; root-knot nematode; stink bugs; Internal provisional chapters 29, 39, 42.

      1. Sources & Confidence Note

      Citation-quality correction: final manuscript sourcing should cite the original Texas A&M AgriLife okra guide for pod harvest at 3-4 inches and 1-2 day harvest frequency, rather than secondary mirrors of the same material.

      Sources by class: Extension/government: Rockwall/Tarrant guides; Texas A&M okra; Texas Plant Disease Handbook; UF/IFAS nematode guidance; NC State okra pests; USDA-ARS okra root-knot resistance re-evaluation.       Source classes for this entry: Extension/government: Texas A&M okra guide; Texas Plant Disease Handbook okra; UF/IFAS nematode management; NC State pests of okra; USDA-ARS okra root-knot resistance reevaluation.       Sweet Potato

      1. Identity Block

      Scientific name: Ipomoea batatas. Family: Convolvulaceae. Common names: sweet potato, sweetpotato, yam in incorrect grocery usage. Source class: extension-supported practice for regional planting and management; peer-reviewed mechanism where specifically cited; practitioner doctrine only when explicitly labeled. Functional definition: a heat-loving vining root crop propagated from slips and used as a summer bed-covering production crop.

      1. Composition & Properties

      Sweet potato is a tropical perennial grown as an annual. It is propagated from slips, not seed potatoes, and the harvested product is a storage root rather than a tuber. Vines spread across the bed, suppress weeds after canopy closure, shade soil, and protect clay surfaces from direct summer sun. Common Texas varieties include Beauregard, Centennial, Jewell/Jewel, and Vardaman. It is not botanically related to Irish potato.

      For manuscript use, classify this entry as a warm-season storage-root crop propagated by slips rather than by grocery category alone. Its practical properties in a North Texas garden are defined by season, plant architecture, marketable organ, and how quickly quality is lost when the crop is mistimed. It fills the role of heat-season bed-covering root crop and substitute entry for Goji Berry unless the master list says otherwise master list. That role matters because the same bed can only hold one crop at a time; the crop must justify its season slot, water demand, and pest-management burden.

      1. Mechanism of Action

      Sweet potato succeeds because hot days, warm nights, and a long frost-free period drive vine growth and storage-root bulking. The crop turns the North Texas summer heat trough into production rather than bare soil. Slip establishment is the vulnerable phase; after canopy closure, the crop becomes one of the more resilient summer vegetables. Sweet potato does NOT fix nitrogen, does NOT tolerate waterlogged clay just because it tolerates heat, and does NOT behave like Irish potato. Excess nitrogen can produce vines at the expense of roots rate threshold.

      The operating mechanism is slip establishment, vine expansion, canopy closure, and storage-root bulking during hot weather. The grower is not merely keeping a plant alive; the grower is protecting the specific developmental stage that creates the harvest. When that stage is interrupted, later vigor rarely repairs the lost yield. This is especially important in North Texas, where spring can move from cool to hot quickly and where late-summer plantings must beat the first freeze. Management should therefore begin with the calendar and the target harvest organ, then move to fertility, irrigation, and pest response.

      The primary limiter is cold soil at slip set, compacted or wet Blackland clay, root-knot nematode, excess nitrogen, and poor curing. This limiter should be handled through the Disease Triangle and IPM Hierarchy rather than through single-product thinking. A susceptible host, a viable pathogen or pest population, and favorable weather or canopy conditions produce damage. Cultural controls - timing, rotation, spacing, drip irrigation, sanitation, mulch, row cover, trellising, resistant varieties, and harvest discipline - carry more of the system than rescue sprays. Once the crop has passed the correct growth stage, a product cannot usually restore the missing developmental window.

      1. Planting and Establishment

      Spring/slip window: April 15-June 1, with April 15-May 15 the tighter production window in some local guides. No true fall planting is recommended because the crop needs 100-130 days. Soil temperature: over 65 F minimum by Texas A&M guidance, with 75 F used in North Central Texas soil-temperature tables. Plant slips into raised ridges or beds, commonly 8 inches high, after soil is warm. Varieties: Beauregard, Centennial, Jewell/Jewel, Vardaman; nematode-resistant options require current cultivar verification.

      Use the planting window as a production boundary, not a casual suggestion. For North Texas, the working calendar is: slips April 15-June 1, with April 15-May 15 preferred for full 100-130 day crop; no normal fall planting. The soil-temperature requirement is over 65 F minimum by Texas guidance and 75 F in the Rockwall North Texas table. Soil temperature should be checked in the morning before sun warms the bed and should be stable, not a one-afternoon spike. When the crop is transplanted, transplant size and hardening matter; when direct-seeded, seedbed crusting and moisture continuity matter more than most amendments.

      Establishment should be built around: raised ridges or beds, 8-inch high rows where possible, early weed control for the first 40 days, and steady irrigation during establishment. In Blackland clay, bed shape is a first-order practice. Raised beds, broad ridges, and controlled traffic prevent the common pattern of a crop looking acceptable aboveground while roots sit in compacted or poorly aerated soil. Drip irrigation is the default because it reduces leaf wetness, preserves soil structure, and allows water to be delivered without splashing pathogens onto lower leaves or fruit.

      Recommended and defensible varieties or variety classes include: Beauregard, Centennial, Jewel/Jewell, Vardaman, and documented nematode-resistant lines where available current cultivar ranking. Disease-resistant variety language must be read literally. A resistance code means resistance or tolerance to a named pathogen, race, or disease complex; it does not mean general garden toughness. For manuscript lock, current seed availability, exact resistance race codes, and regional performance should be verified against the final seed-source list and county extension notes.

      1. Interactions

      Do not combine high-phosphorus transplant amendments with AMF inoculant claims in this crop; high phosphorus suppresses AMF colonization, and ectomycorrhizal products do not work on annual vegetable crops.

      Sweet potato pairs well with bed pivots after garlic, onions, lettuce, spinach, or early brassicas. It interacts poorly with compacted or saturated clay. Raised beds, ridges, compost, and early weed control matter more than heavy fertilization. Keep slips moist during the first month, then rely on vine canopy as living cover. Avoid planting in nematode-infested beds without a rotation plan.

      1. Contraindications & Failure Modes

      Do not plant slips into cold soil or low wet clay. Do not let slips wilt repeatedly during establishment. Do not water heavily in the final 2-3 weeks before harvest if rot risk is high local rainfall adjustment. Common failures include late planting, excess nitrogen, compacted soil, insufficient early irrigation, root-knot nematode, wireworms, weevils, beetles, harvest bruising, and poor curing.

      Do not plant this crop when the known limiter is already present and unmanaged: cold soil at slip set, compacted or wet Blackland clay, root-knot nematode, excess nitrogen, and poor curing. Do not buy diseased transplants, do not use unknown planting stock for production beds, and do not leave infected residue as surface mulch in a bed that will host a related crop. The most expensive failure is the one that carries pathogen or pest pressure forward into the next planting window.

      1. Diagnostics & Verification

      Successful slips stand up, root, and begin new vine growth within about two weeks. Vine vigor alone does not prove root yield; sample-digging near maturity is better. Harvest 100-130 days after planting or before frost, when leaves yellow or calendar maturity is reached. Handle roots gently because skins are easily bruised.

      Verification starts with the crop-specific harvest indicator: dig after 100-130 days or before frost; yellowing leaves and cultivar days guide harvest; handle roots gently. This is the standard to use before interpreting plant size as success. A large plant without the correct harvest-stage signal is not a successful crop; it is biomass. Record planting date, soil temperature, cultivar, first flower or head date where applicable, first harvest date, and the reason the crop ended. Those notes are the basis for improving the next season.

      1. Lifecycle, Storage & Shelf Life

      Lifecycle is slip establishment, vine expansion, canopy closure, storage-root bulking, harvest, curing, and storage. Cure warm and humid to heal wounds, then store cool but not chilling-cold, around 55 F with ventilation home protocol.

      Lifecycle handling should follow the crop's real endpoint. Storage and postharvest rule: cure warm and humid, then store cool around 55 F with ventilation; do not chill roots. For annual vegetables, the lifecycle also includes residue decisions. Healthy residue can be composted or used in appropriate mulch systems; diseased residue, nematode-infested roots, and pest-infested fruit should be removed or hot-composted where the composting system is capable of sanitation compost sanitation protocol.

      1. Sourcing & Quality Control

      Buy reputable slips, not random grocery-root sprouts, for production beds. Choose named varieties and nematode resistance where documented. Slips should be vigorous and free of rot, black stems, and insects. Do not move soil from nematode beds to clean beds.

      For this crop, the quality-control emphasis is the match between source and North Texas use: Beauregard, Centennial, Jewel/Jewell, Vardaman, and documented nematode-resistant lines where available current cultivar ranking. A novelty variety can be grown as a trial, but production beds should reserve most space for adapted, documented cultivars. Keep seed lots labeled through harvest so that failures can be tied to cultivar, planting date, and bed rather than being remembered as a vague seasonal disappointment.

      1. North Texas Note

      Sweet potato is a North Texas summer workhorse and the appropriate substitute for Goji Berry unless the master list proves otherwise. Primary problems are root-knot nematode, wireworms/soil insects, weevils, poor drainage rots, and harvest/cure damage. Raised ridges and warm soil are non-negotiable in Blackland clay.

      North Texas management priority: match the crop to alkaline Blackland Prairie clay, erratic rainfall, chloraminated municipal water, and routine 100-110 F summer heat. The primary disease priorities are root-knot nematode, soil rot/root rot in wet beds, leaf spots, storage rots, and nematode-associated root cracking. The primary pest priorities are sweet potato weevil where present, wireworms, beetles, cutworms, white grubs, and rodents in some sites. These are not abstract lists; they determine the planting window, rotation group, irrigation method, row-cover timing, scouting route, and whether the bed should be pivoted to a different crop.

      1. Cross-References

      Disease Triangle; IPM Hierarchy; Seasonal Succession and Bed Pivoting SOP; Raised Bed Soil Preparation for Blackland Clay; root-knot nematode; soil insect complex; Internal provisional chapters 20, 21, 29, 30.

      1. Sources & Confidence Note

      Sources by class: Extension: Texas A&M sweet potato guide; Rockwall and Tarrant planting/variety guides; Alabama Extension root-knot nematode on sweet potato; NC State postharvest handling.
