      Session 2 - Cucurbit crops

      Chapter 11: Brassicas and Leafy Greens

      Broccoli / Cauliflower / Brussels Sprouts

      1. Identity Block

      Scientific name: Brassica oleracea crop groups. Family: Brassicaceae. Common names: broccoli, cauliflower, Brussels sprouts, cole crops. Source class: extension-supported practice for regional planting and management; peer-reviewed mechanism where specifically cited; practitioner doctrine only when explicitly labeled. Functional definition: cool-season brassicas grown for immature flower structures or axillary buds, with fall production favored in North Texas.

      1. Composition & Properties

      Broccoli forms a central immature flower head and side shoots; cauliflower forms a curd; Brussels sprouts form axillary buds along a stem. These crops require steady cool-season growth and are more sensitive to heat than leafy brassicas. Broccoli is the most forgiving, cauliflower and Brussels sprouts the least.

      For manuscript use, classify this entry as a cool-season cole crop group in Brassica oleracea rather than by grocery category alone. Its practical properties in a North Texas garden are defined by season, plant architecture, marketable organ, and how quickly quality is lost when the crop is mistimed. It fills the role of fall-primary head, curd, and sprout crops requiring early scheduling and caterpillar exclusion. That role matters because the same bed can only hold one crop at a time; the crop must justify its season slot, water demand, and pest-management burden.

      1. Mechanism of Action

      These crops succeed when enough leaf area is built before marketable head, curd, or sprout formation occurs in cool weather. Heat causes loose broccoli heads, ricey cauliflower curds, and Brussels sprouts that fail to size before frost. They do NOT make marketable heads simply because plants are green and large; timing, cultivar maturity, water, nitrogen, and pest-free foliage must align. Bt does NOT rescue late-stage caterpillar infestations; it works only on susceptible early-instar larvae that ingest it.

      The operating mechanism is steady cool-season vegetative growth followed by immature floral head, curd, or axillary sprout development before heat or hard freeze disrupts quality. The grower is not merely keeping a plant alive; the grower is protecting the specific developmental stage that creates the harvest. When that stage is interrupted, later vigor rarely repairs the lost yield. This is especially important in North Texas, where spring can move from cool to hot quickly and where late-summer plantings must beat the first freeze. Management should therefore begin with the calendar and the target harvest organ, then move to fertility, irrigation, and pest response.

      The primary limiter is late timing, buttoning, ricey cauliflower, Brussels sprouts running out of season, black rot/downy mildew, aphids, and caterpillar contamination. This limiter should be handled through the Disease Triangle and IPM Hierarchy rather than through single-product thinking. A susceptible host, a viable pathogen or pest population, and favorable weather or canopy conditions produce damage. Cultural controls - timing, rotation, spacing, drip irrigation, sanitation, mulch, row cover, trellising, resistant varieties, and harvest discipline - carry more of the system than rescue sprays. Once the crop has passed the correct growth stage, a product cannot usually restore the missing developmental window.

      1. Planting and Establishment

      For cauliflower, Snow Crown remains the local North Central Texas baseline, but disease-resistant cauliflower packages are less consistently documented in the public extension variety lists used here; require a current seed label naming downy mildew, black rot, Fusarium, bacterial leaf spot, or other specific resistance before presenting a cauliflower cultivar as disease-resistant. current disease-resistant cauliflower cultivars available to North Texas growers.

      Spring transplant: February 1-February 15, with some lists allowing February 15-March 1. Fall transplant: August 15-September 30 for broccoli/Brussels and August 15-September 20 for cauliflower; Brussels should be earliest. Soil temperature: 50-55 F. Varieties: Premium Crop, Emperor, Green Comet, Galaxy, Packman, Snow Crown, Prince Marvel, Royal Marvel, Green Magic, Marathon, Hestia, Jade Cross. Resistant options from catalog databases include Belstar, Fiesta, Green Magic, Marathon, Attis, Confidant, Dimitri, Doric, Mighty, and Nautic.

      Use the planting window as a production boundary, not a casual suggestion. For North Texas, the working calendar is: spring transplants February 1-February 15/early March; fall seed August 1-August 15 and transplants August 15-September 30, with Brussels early and cauliflower not late cultivar maturity. The soil-temperature requirement is 50-55 F practical transplant range for cole crops. Soil temperature should be checked in the morning before sun warms the bed and should be stable, not a one-afternoon spike. When the crop is transplanted, transplant size and hardening matter; when direct-seeded, seedbed crusting and moisture continuity matter more than most amendments.

      Establishment should be built around: firm transplants, raised beds, row cover during establishment, drip irrigation, nitrogen timed for steady growth, and rotation away from all brassicas. In Blackland clay, bed shape is a first-order practice. Raised beds, broad ridges, and controlled traffic prevent the common pattern of a crop looking acceptable aboveground while roots sit in compacted or poorly aerated soil. Drip irrigation is the default because it reduces leaf wetness, preserves soil structure, and allows water to be delivered without splashing pathogens onto lower leaves or fruit.

      Recommended and defensible varieties or variety classes include: Premium Crop, Emperor, Green Comet, Galaxy, Packman, Snow Crown, Prince Marvel, Royal Marvel, Green Magic, Diplomat, Marathon, Artwork, Hestia, Jade Cross, and disease-resistant Belstar/Marathon-type packages current seed. Disease-resistant variety language must be read literally. A resistance code means resistance or tolerance to a named pathogen, race, or disease complex; it does not mean general garden toughness. For manuscript lock, current seed availability, exact resistance race codes, and regional performance should be verified against the final seed-source list and county extension notes.

      1. Interactions

      They pair with row cover, drip irrigation, rotation, and steady fertility. Row cover works well because the harvest does not require pollination. Avoid late nitrogen surges, wet foliage, and planting after brassicas. In alkaline North Texas soils, do not recommend dolomitic lime unless soil test justifies it Mg.

      1. Contraindications & Failure Modes

      Do not plant cauliflower or Brussels late and expect quality. Do not use heat-trapping row cover in August without ventilation. Failures include buttoning, ricey curds, loose heads, aphids, loopers, imported cabbageworms, diamondback moth, harlequin bugs, black rot, downy mildew, Alternaria, and damping-off. Neem is not used above 85 F; sulfur and oil are not combined within two weeks.

      Do not plant this crop when the known limiter is already present and unmanaged: late timing, buttoning, ricey cauliflower, Brussels sprouts running out of season, black rot/downy mildew, aphids, and caterpillar contamination. Do not buy diseased transplants, do not use unknown planting stock for production beds, and do not leave infected residue as surface mulch in a bed that will host a related crop. The most expensive failure is the one that carries pathogen or pest pressure forward into the next planting window.

      1. Diagnostics & Verification

      Harvest broccoli when buds are tight; harvest cauliflower when curd is dense before loosening; blanch white cauliflower by covering curd if needed. Harvest Brussels sprouts from bottom upward at about 1-1.5 inches when firm. Diagnose black rot by V-shaped margin lesions and downy mildew by cool moist leaf spotting and underside growth local guide.

      Verification starts with the crop-specific harvest indicator: broccoli tight buds; cauliflower dense protected curd before riceyness; Brussels sprouts firm 1-1.5 inch sprouts from bottom upward. This is the standard to use before interpreting plant size as success. A large plant without the correct harvest-stage signal is not a successful crop; it is biomass. Record planting date, soil temperature, cultivar, first flower or head date where applicable, first harvest date, and the reason the crop ended. Those notes are the basis for improving the next season.

      1. Lifecycle, Storage & Shelf Life

      Lifecycle is transplant, establishment, leaf expansion, head/curd/sprout formation, harvest, and residue removal. Rockwall lists broccoli 60-80 days, cauliflower 70-90, Brussels 90-100. Cool promptly after harvest and remove diseased brassica residue.

      Lifecycle handling should follow the crop's real endpoint. Storage and postharvest rule: cool promptly; broccoli/cauliflower short storage; Brussels store better when firm and aphid-free. For annual vegetables, the lifecycle also includes residue decisions. Healthy residue can be composted or used in appropriate mulch systems; diseased residue, nematode-infested roots, and pest-infested fruit should be removed or hot-composted where the composting system is capable of sanitation compost sanitation protocol.

      1. Sourcing & Quality Control

      Buy clean, stocky transplants free of aphids, caterpillar frass, leaf spots, and rootbound stress. For seed, select days-to-maturity before specialty traits. Brussels sprouts need the shortest reliable maturity available.

      For this crop, the quality-control emphasis is the match between source and North Texas use: Premium Crop, Emperor, Green Comet, Galaxy, Packman, Snow Crown, Prince Marvel, Royal Marvel, Green Magic, Diplomat, Marathon, Artwork, Hestia, Jade Cross, and disease-resistant Belstar/Marathon-type packages current seed. A novelty variety can be grown as a trial, but production beds should reserve most space for adapted, documented cultivars. Keep seed lots labeled through harvest so that failures can be tied to cultivar, planting date, and bed rather than being remembered as a vague seasonal disappointment.

      1. North Texas Note

      Fall-primary in North Texas. Primary diseases: black rot, downy mildew, Alternaria leaf spot, blackleg/wirestem, damping-off exact titles. Primary pests: loopers, imported cabbageworms, diamondback moth, aphids, harlequin bugs, flea beetles.

      North Texas management priority: match the crop to alkaline Blackland Prairie clay, erratic rainfall, chloraminated municipal water, and routine 100-110 F summer heat. The primary disease priorities are black rot, downy mildew, Alternaria leaf spot, blackleg/wirestem, damping-off, Sclerotinia, and clubroot only where soil conditions/pathogen support it local occurrence. The primary pest priorities are cabbage loopers, imported cabbageworms, diamondback moths, aphids, harlequin bugs, flea beetles, and armyworms.       Cabbage

      1. Identity Block

      Scientific name: Brassica oleracea var. capitata. Family: Brassicaceae. Common names: cabbage, head cabbage, green cabbage, red cabbage, savoy cabbage. Source class: extension-supported practice for regional planting and management; peer-reviewed mechanism where specifically cited; practitioner doctrine only when explicitly labeled. Functional definition: a cool-season brassica grown for a compact terminal leaf head.

      1. Composition & Properties

      Cabbage is a biennial grown as an annual. The edible head is a compressed terminal bud of overlapping leaves. Types include round green, red, savoy, pointed, mini, and storage cabbage. It is more forgiving than cauliflower but still needs cool finishing weather and protection from caterpillars, aphids, harlequin bugs, and black rot.

      For manuscript use, classify this entry as a cool-season heading brassica rather than by grocery category alone. Its practical properties in a North Texas garden are defined by season, plant architecture, marketable organ, and how quickly quality is lost when the crop is mistimed. It fills the role of fall-priority head crop that is more forgiving than cauliflower but still vulnerable to harlequin bugs, black rot, and splitting. That role matters because the same bed can only hold one crop at a time; the crop must justify its season slot, water demand, and pest-management burden.

      1. Mechanism of Action

      Cabbage succeeds through uninterrupted leaf initiation, expansion, and head folding. Wrapper leaves support later head fill. Cabbage does NOT tolerate irregular late moisture harmlessly; rapid water uptake into a tight mature head causes splitting. It also does NOT become safe from black rot or harlequin bugs just because it is planted in fall. The disease triangle remains active.

      The operating mechanism is wrapper leaf expansion followed by compact terminal head formation; late water and overmaturity split tight heads. The grower is not merely keeping a plant alive; the grower is protecting the specific developmental stage that creates the harvest. When that stage is interrupted, later vigor rarely repairs the lost yield. This is especially important in North Texas, where spring can move from cool to hot quickly and where late-summer plantings must beat the first freeze. Management should therefore begin with the calendar and the target harvest organ, then move to fertility, irrigation, and pest response.

      The primary limiter is heat, excess moisture at maturity, black rot, downy mildew, aphids hidden in heads, caterpillar contamination, and harlequin bugs.       Kale / Collards

      1. Identity Block

      Scientific name: Brassica oleracea leafy groups. Family: Brassicaceae. Common names: kale, curly kale, lacinato kale, collards, collard greens. Source class: extension-supported practice for regional planting and management; peer-reviewed mechanism where specifically cited; practitioner doctrine only when explicitly labeled. Functional definition: cool-season leafy brassicas grown for repeated leaf harvest.

      1. Composition & Properties

      Kale and collards are leafy brassicas that do not form marketable heads. Collards have large smooth waxy leaves and stronger heat tolerance; kale includes curly, flat, lacinato, red, Russian/Siberian, and hybrid compact forms. Because the crop is leaf area, they are more forgiving than head brassicas and support cut-and-come-again harvest.

      For manuscript use, classify this entry as a cool-season leafy brassica group rather than by grocery category alone. Its practical properties in a North Texas garden are defined by season, plant architecture, marketable organ, and how quickly quality is lost when the crop is mistimed. It fills the role of repeated-harvest cold-hardy greens for fall, winter, and early spring. That role matters because the same bed can only hold one crop at a time; the crop must justify its season slot, water demand, and pest-management burden.

      1. Mechanism of Action

      Kale and collards succeed by continuous vegetative leaf growth. The grower harvests mature outer leaves while preserving the growing point. They do NOT need pollination, heading, curd formation, or fruit set for harvest, so row cover can remain longer than on fruit crops. Frost improves flavor in many plantings, but it does NOT cure pest damage, drought stress, or disease.

      The operating mechanism is continuous vegetative leaf production from a protected growing point; harvest outer leaves while retaining the crown. The grower is not merely keeping a plant alive; the grower is protecting the specific developmental stage that creates the harvest. When that stage is interrupted, later vigor rarely repairs the lost yield. This is especially important in North Texas, where spring can move from cool to hot quickly and where late-summer plantings must beat the first freeze. Management should therefore begin with the calendar and the target harvest organ, then move to fertility, irrigation, and pest response.

      The primary limiter is aphids in curled leaves, harlequin bug feeding, caterpillars, whiteflies, downy mildew, white rust, and summer heat quality decline. This limiter should be handled through the Disease Triangle and IPM Hierarchy rather than through single-product thinking. A susceptible host, a viable pathogen or pest population, and favorable weather or canopy conditions produce damage. Cultural controls - timing, rotation, spacing, drip irrigation, sanitation, mulch, row cover, trellising, resistant varieties, and harvest discipline - carry more of the system than rescue sprays. Once the crop has passed the correct growth stage, a product cannot usually restore the missing developmental window.

      1. Planting and Establishment

      Disease-resistant variety and seed-quality guidance: for North Texas, keep Blue Max, Georgia, Georgia Southern, Champion, Flash, Top Bunch, and Vates as the collard baseline, and Dwarf Blue Curled, Blue Knight, lacinato/dinosaur types, Red Russian, Winterbor, Starbor, and Redbor as practical kale categories. For disease-prone beds, do not imply resistance unless the seed label names the disease. Prioritize seed lots tested or certified as black-rot-free where black rot has been a problem. Candidate kale listings such as Squire Kale and Wan Shen Kale carry disease-resistance or mildew-resistance language in Cornell variety listings, but they require current seed-label confirmation and North Texas adaptation review before being presented as recommendations. current disease-resistant kale/collard cultivars and black-rot-free seed availability for North Texas growers.

      Do not let old brassica plants linger as pest reservoirs without management.       Lettuce

      1. Identity Block

      Scientific name: Lactuca sativa. Family: Asteraceae. Common names: lettuce, leaf lettuce, romaine, butterhead, Bibb, Batavian, crisphead. Source class: extension-supported practice for regional planting and management; peer-reviewed mechanism where specifically cited; practitioner doctrine only when explicitly labeled. Functional definition: a cool-season leafy crop grown for tender leaves or heads, with fall production favored in North Texas.

      1. Composition & Properties

      Lettuce types include looseleaf, romaine/cos, butterhead/Bibb, Batavian/summer crisp, and crisphead/iceberg. Looseleaf and romaine are more forgiving than crisphead in warm climates. Leaves are the crop, so spray injury, aphids, soil splash, and leaf diseases directly affect marketability.

      For manuscript use, classify this entry as a cool-season leafy annual rather than by grocery category alone. Its practical properties in a North Texas garden are defined by season, plant architecture, marketable organ, and how quickly quality is lost when the crop is mistimed. It fills the role of fall-primary leaf/head crop with short spring extension potential. That role matters because the same bed can only hold one crop at a time; the crop must justify its season slot, water demand, and pest-management burden.

      1. Mechanism of Action

      Lettuce succeeds by rapid cool-season leaf expansion before heat triggers bolting, bitterness, loose heads, tipburn, or seed-stalk formation. Lettuce does NOT become a North Texas summer crop because a variety is labeled heat tolerant. Heat-tolerant types extend spring and help early fall establishment; they do not make lettuce productive through routine 100 F heat. Lettuce does not require pollination for harvest.

      The operating mechanism is rapid tender leaf growth before heat and daylength induce bolting, bitterness, loose heads, or tipburn. The grower is not merely keeping a plant alive; the grower is protecting the specific developmental stage that creates the harvest. When that stage is interrupted, later vigor rarely repairs the lost yield. This is especially important in North Texas, where spring can move from cool to hot quickly and where late-summer plantings must beat the first freeze. Management should therefore begin with the calendar and the target harvest organ, then move to fertility, irrigation, and pest response.

      The primary limiter is hot germination conditions, spring heat, aphids, downy mildew, Sclerotinia drop, cutworms, and wrong lettuce type. This limiter should be handled through the Disease Triangle and IPM Hierarchy rather than through single-product thinking. A susceptible host, a viable pathogen or pest population, and favorable weather or canopy conditions produce damage. Cultural controls - timing, rotation, spacing, drip irrigation, sanitation, mulch, row cover, trellising, resistant varieties, and harvest discipline - carry more of the system than rescue sprays. Once the crop has passed the correct growth stage, a product cannot usually restore the missing developmental window.

      1. Planting and Establishment

      Disease-resistant varieties: for North Texas, prioritize local and heat-aware lettuce types first, then filter by disease package. Use Black Seeded Simpson, Salad Bowl, Red Sails, Green Ice, Tango, Buttercrunch, Summer Bibb, Parris Island, Valmaine, Plato II, Sierra, and Batavian/summer-crisp types where available. For disease-prone beds, Cornell lists Plato II with downy mildew and white mold/Sclerotinia resistance; Sandy with bacterial spot, downy mildew, and powdery mildew resistance; Sierra with downy mildew, lettuce mosaic virus, and tomato bushy stunt virus resistance; Super Jericho with downy mildew, powdery mildew, and lettuce mosaic virus resistance; and Valmaine with downy mildew resistance. These are candidate varieties, not automatic North Texas recommendations, until current seed availability, disease-race package, and local heat performance are verified. current lettuce seed availability and disease-race package.

      Spring window: February 1-March 31, with February 10-March 15 the main production window. Fall window: August 15-September 30 depending on type; fall is primary. Soil temperature: 45-50 F for spring planting; fall seedbeds must be kept below heat failure hot-soil method. Plant shallowly, 1/8-1/4 inch, and succession sow every 10-14 days. Varieties: Buttercrunch, Tom Thumb, Classic, Park’s Mission, Salad Bowl, Black Seeded Simpson, Red Sails, Romaine, Green Ice, Tango, Parris Island, Valmaine, Plato II. Resistant options include Sandy, Super Jericho, Green Star, Sierra, Plato II, Valmaine race package.

      Use the planting window as a production boundary, not a casual suggestion. For North Texas, the working calendar is: spring February 1-March 31, with February 10-March 15 primary; fall August 15-September 30 depending type, with fall as main season. The soil-temperature requirement is 45-50 F practical spring threshold; fall seed zone must be cooled below heat-inhibition levels priming method. Soil temperature should be checked in the morning before sun warms the bed and should be stable, not a one-afternoon spike. When the crop is transplanted, transplant size and hardening matter; when direct-seeded, seedbed crusting and moisture continuity matter more than most amendments.

      Establishment should be built around: fine seedbed, shallow seed with light exposure, shade cloth for warm fall starts, drip irrigation, succession sowing every 10-14 days, and row cover without overheating. In Blackland clay, bed shape is a first-order practice. Raised beds, broad ridges, and controlled traffic prevent the common pattern of a crop looking acceptable aboveground while roots sit in compacted or poorly aerated soil. Drip irrigation is the default because it reduces leaf wetness, preserves soil structure, and allows water to be delivered without splashing pathogens onto lower leaves or fruit.

      Recommended and defensible varieties or variety classes include: Buttercrunch, Ermosa, Esmeralda, Summer Bibb, Black Seeded Simpson, Green Ice, Red Sails, Salad Bowl, Tango, Freckles, Parris Island, Valmaine, Plato II, Sierra, Sandy, Super Jericho Bremia race package. Disease-resistant variety language must be read literally. A resistance code means resistance or tolerance to a named pathogen, race, or disease complex; it does not mean general garden toughness. For manuscript lock, current seed availability, exact resistance race codes, and regional performance should be verified against the final seed-source list and county extension notes.

      1. Interactions

      Lettuce pairs with drip irrigation, shade cloth during warm establishment, fine compost, row cover, and quick succession. It dislikes crusting, drought, hot covers, salty fertilizer bands, and wet foliage at night. High nitrogen supports leaf growth but excess soluble fertility in heat can produce soft growth and aphids.

      1. Contraindications & Failure Modes

      Do not plant crisphead as the reliability crop for North Texas. Do not seed into hot exposed fall soil without shade/moisture management. Failures include bolting, bitterness, poor germination, aphids, cutworms, slugs/snails, leafminers, downy mildew, Sclerotinia drop, damping-off, bottom rot, mosaic, and tipburn. Neem/sulfur and Bt boundaries apply.

      Do not plant this crop when the known limiter is already present and unmanaged: hot germination conditions, spring heat, aphids, downy mildew, Sclerotinia drop, cutworms, and wrong lettuce type. Do not buy diseased transplants, do not use unknown planting stock for production beds, and do not leave infected residue as surface mulch in a bed that will host a related crop. The most expensive failure is the one that carries pathogen or pest pressure forward into the next planting window.

      1. Diagnostics & Verification

      Harvest leaf lettuce as baby leaf, outer leaves, or cut above the crown for regrowth. Harvest romaine/butterhead when full before bolting. Sclerotinia drop shows lower leaf collapse, soft watery rot, white cottony growth, and black sclerotia.

      Verification starts with the crop-specific harvest indicator: baby leaf, outer-leaf cut-and-come-again, or full head before bolting; harvest cool and condition promptly. This is the standard to use before interpreting plant size as success. A large plant without the correct harvest-stage signal is not a successful crop; it is biomass. Record planting date, soil temperature, cultivar, first flower or head date where applicable, first harvest date, and the reason the crop ended. Those notes are the basis for improving the next season.

      1. Lifecycle, Storage & Shelf Life

      Maturity ranges 40-80 days. Succession prevents one large flush. Harvest in the cool of the day, remove field heat, drain washed leaves, and store cold/high humidity duration.

      Lifecycle handling should follow the crop's real endpoint. Storage and postharvest rule: cold humid short storage; drain leaves before sealing to avoid rot. For annual vegetables, the lifecycle also includes residue decisions. Healthy residue can be composted or used in appropriate mulch systems; diseased residue, nematode-infested roots, and pest-infested fruit should be removed or hot-composted where the composting system is capable of sanitation compost sanitation protocol.

      1. Sourcing & Quality Control

      Buy by type, days, heat tolerance, and disease package. For spring extension use looseleaf, romaine, butterhead, and Batavian. Avoid transplants with aphids in crowns, yellow leaves, stretched stems, or damping-off.

      For this crop, the quality-control emphasis is the match between source and North Texas use: Buttercrunch, Ermosa, Esmeralda, Summer Bibb, Black Seeded Simpson, Green Ice, Red Sails, Salad Bowl, Tango, Freckles, Parris Island, Valmaine, Plato II, Sierra, Sandy, Super Jericho Bremia race package. A novelty variety can be grown as a trial, but production beds should reserve most space for adapted, documented cultivars. Keep seed lots labeled through harvest so that failures can be tied to cultivar, planting date, and bed rather than being remembered as a vague seasonal disappointment.

      1. North Texas Note

      Fall-primary crop. Primary diseases: Sclerotinia drop, downy mildew, bottom rot/damping-off, lettuce mosaic virus, tipburn. Primary pests: aphids, cutworms, leafminers, slugs/snails, occasional caterpillars.

      North Texas management priority: match the crop to alkaline Blackland Prairie clay, erratic rainfall, chloraminated municipal water, and routine 100-110 F summer heat. The primary disease priorities are Sclerotinia drop, downy mildew, bottom rot/damping-off, lettuce mosaic virus, bacterial leaf spots, and tipburn as abiotic disorder. The primary pest priorities are aphids, cutworms, leaf miners, slugs/snails, caterpillars, and pillbugs in moist beds. These are not abstract lists; they determine the planting window, rotation group, irrigation method, row-cover timing, scouting route, and whether the bed should be pivoted to a different crop.

      1. Cross-References

      Disease Triangle; IPM Hierarchy; Seasonal Succession and Bed Pivoting SOP; Sclerotinia drop; aphids; Internal provisional chapters 20, 29, 39, 42.

      1. Sources & Confidence Note

      Sources by class: Extension: Rockwall/Tarrant guides; Texas A&M lettuce; Williamson County soil temperature; Collin County fall lettuce guidance; Texas Plant Disease Handbook; LSU lettuce; Cornell varieties. Confidence note: High confidence for fall-primary timing, heat-bolting mechanism, type selection, and harvest methods; race-specific disease resistance needs verification.

      Source classes for this entry: Extension/government: Texas A&M lettuce guide; Rockwall/Tarrant guides; Williamson County soil temperature guidance; Collin County fall vegetable guidance; Texas Plant Disease Handbook lettuce; Cornell lettuce resistance list; LSU lettuce guidance. Practitioner doctrine: companion-planting, tonic, foliar-feed, microbial-inoculant, and input-stacking claims are not treated as mechanism unless a source class supports the mechanism directly. items remain for current seed availability, exact Phase K entry titles, and county-level pest/disease ranking.

      Spinach

      1. Identity Block

      Scientific name: Spinacia oleracea. Family: Amaranthaceae. Common names: spinach, true spinach, savoy spinach, semi-savoy spinach, smooth-leaf spinach. Source class: extension-supported practice for regional planting and management; peer-reviewed mechanism where specifically cited; practitioner doctrine only when explicitly labeled. Functional definition: a cool-only leafy crop for fall, winter, and very early spring production.

      1. Composition & Properties

      Spinach is a cool-season annual grown for rosette leaves before bolting. Leaf types include savoy, semi-savoy, and smooth leaf. It is distinct from Malabar spinach, New Zealand spinach, and Swiss chard. Local varieties include Melody, Coho, Fall Green, Tyee, Bloomsdale, and Space.

      For manuscript use, classify this entry as a cool-season leafy annual rather than by grocery category alone. Its practical properties in a North Texas garden are defined by season, plant architecture, marketable organ, and how quickly quality is lost when the crop is mistimed. It fills the role of cool-only North Texas green for fall/winter and very short spring production. That role matters because the same bed can only hold one crop at a time; the crop must justify its season slot, water demand, and pest-management burden.

      1. Mechanism of Action

      Spinach succeeds by rapid tender leaf growth during cool conditions. Heat and long days push it into seed stalk production. Spinach does NOT become a North Texas summer crop because it is labeled slow-bolt. Slow-bolt varieties extend the window; they do not overcome June-August heat. Spinach also does not need pollination for harvest, so row cover can protect leaves without blocking crop production.

      The operating mechanism is rosette leaf production under cool temperatures before heat and long days trigger bolting. The grower is not merely keeping a plant alive; the grower is protecting the specific developmental stage that creates the harvest. When that stage is interrupted, later vigor rarely repairs the lost yield. This is especially important in North Texas, where spring can move from cool to hot quickly and where late-summer plantings must beat the first freeze. Management should therefore begin with the calendar and the target harvest organ, then move to fertility, irrigation, and pest response.

      The primary limiter is hot fall seed zone, late spring planting, white rust, downy mildew, aphids, leaf miners, and old seed. This limiter should be handled through the Disease Triangle and IPM Hierarchy rather than through single-product thinking. A susceptible host, a viable pathogen or pest population, and favorable weather or canopy conditions produce damage. Cultural controls - timing, rotation, spacing, drip irrigation, sanitation, mulch, row cover, trellising, resistant varieties, and harvest discipline - carry more of the system than rescue sprays. Once the crop has passed the correct growth stage, a product cannot usually restore the missing developmental window.

      1. Planting and Establishment

      Disease-resistant varieties: use Bloomsdale, Melody, Space, and Tyee as Texas A&M baseline varieties, then select disease-resistant seed where white rust or downy mildew has occurred. Cornell lists Space F1 with Cladosporium leaf spot and multiple downy mildew race resistance; Sunangel with downy mildew race 1-19 and white rust resistance; Ashley, Baboon RZ, Hammerhead, Mandolin, Oceanside, Persius, Thor, Vancouver, and Viceroy with various downy mildew and/or white rust packages. Because spinach downy mildew resistance is race-specific, current seed-label confirmation is required before final recommendation. current spinach downy mildew race package and seed availability.

      Spring window: January 20-March 10, with February 10-March 10 at 50 F soil in local soil tables. Fall window: September 15-November 1, with August 1-October 15 in broader lists if heat is managed. Soil temperature: 50 F spring threshold; in fall, excessive heat is the risk. Seed 1/2 inch deep, thin to 3-4 inches, and succession sow every 10-14 days. Resistant varieties from catalogs include Space, Bloomsdale Long Standing, Ashley, Hammerhead, Mandolin, Oceanside, Persius, Samish, Sunangel, Thor, Vancouver, and Viceroy race packages.

      Use the planting window as a production boundary, not a casual suggestion. For North Texas, the working calendar is: spring January 20-March 10 with February 10-March 10 soil-temperature line; fall September 15-November 1, with August-October possible under heat management. The soil-temperature requirement is 50 F spring threshold; fall must avoid excessive seed-zone heat. Soil temperature should be checked in the morning before sun warms the bed and should be stable, not a one-afternoon spike. When the crop is transplanted, transplant size and hardening matter; when direct-seeded, seedbed crusting and moisture continuity matter more than most amendments.

      Establishment should be built around: direct seed 1/2 inch, keep surface moist, cover fall seed with light material to cool soil, thin to 3-4 inches, and succession sow every 10-14 days. In Blackland clay, bed shape is a first-order practice. Raised beds, broad ridges, and controlled traffic prevent the common pattern of a crop looking acceptable aboveground while roots sit in compacted or poorly aerated soil. Drip irrigation is the default because it reduces leaf wetness, preserves soil structure, and allows water to be delivered without splashing pathogens onto lower leaves or fruit.

      Recommended and defensible varieties or variety classes include: Melody, Coho, Fall Green, Tyee, Bloomsdale, Space, and race-specific downy mildew/white rust resistant varieties such as Hammerhead, Mandolin, Persius, Samish, Viceroy race package. Disease-resistant variety language must be read literally. A resistance code means resistance or tolerance to a named pathogen, race, or disease complex; it does not mean general garden toughness. For manuscript lock, current seed availability, exact resistance race codes, and regional performance should be verified against the final seed-source list and county extension notes.

      1. Interactions

      Spinach pairs with fine seedbeds, drip irrigation, row cover, compost-amended raised beds, and succession planting. It interacts poorly with hot dry seedbeds and wet foliage in cool weather. Adequate nitrogen supports dark leaves, but heavy soft growth does not prevent bolting threshold.

      1. Contraindications & Failure Modes

      Do not plant spring spinach late or hot fall spinach without shade/moisture management. Do not confuse heat-season substitutes with true spinach. Failures include hot-soil germination failure, rapid bolting, aphids, leaf miners, white rust, downy mildew, damping-off, and leaf spots. Neem/sulfur and Bt boundaries apply.

      Do not plant this crop when the known limiter is already present and unmanaged: hot fall seed zone, late spring planting, white rust, downy mildew, aphids, leaf miners, and old seed. Do not buy diseased transplants, do not use unknown planting stock for production beds, and do not leave infected residue as surface mulch in a bed that will host a related crop. The most expensive failure is the one that carries pathogen or pest pressure forward into the next planting window.

      1. Diagnostics & Verification

      Harvest when plants are 6-8 inches tall. In spring, pull whole plants before heat; in fall, cut 1-2 inches above the crown for regrowth. White rust appears as white pustules on lower leaf surfaces; diagnose before assuming nutrient issues.

      Verification starts with the crop-specific harvest indicator: 6-8 inch plants; spring often whole-plant pull, fall cut above crown for regrowth. This is the standard to use before interpreting plant size as success. A large plant without the correct harvest-stage signal is not a successful crop; it is biomass. Record planting date, soil temperature, cultivar, first flower or head date where applicable, first harvest date, and the reason the crop ended. Those notes are the basis for improving the next season.

      1. Lifecycle, Storage & Shelf Life

      Maturity is 40-60 days. Baby leaf can be harvested earlier. Store cold after removing damaged or diseased leaves packaging duration.

      Lifecycle handling should follow the crop's real endpoint. Storage and postharvest rule: cool quickly and store cold; discard leaf-mined or diseased leaves. For annual vegetables, the lifecycle also includes residue decisions. Healthy residue can be composted or used in appropriate mulch systems; diseased residue, nematode-infested roots, and pest-infested fruit should be removed or hot-composted where the composting system is capable of sanitation compost sanitation protocol.

      1. Sourcing & Quality Control

      Buy seed by leaf type, days, bolt resistance, and disease package. Do not rely on old seed for critical fall plantings. Pelleted seed may help spacing but must stay moist protocol.

      For this crop, the quality-control emphasis is the match between source and North Texas use: Melody, Coho, Fall Green, Tyee, Bloomsdale, Space, and race-specific downy mildew/white rust resistant varieties such as Hammerhead, Mandolin, Persius, Samish, Viceroy race package. A novelty variety can be grown as a trial, but production beds should reserve most space for adapted, documented cultivars. Keep seed lots labeled through harvest so that failures can be tied to cultivar, planting date, and bed rather than being remembered as a vague seasonal disappointment.

      1. North Texas Note

      Cool-only in North Texas. Primary diseases: white rust, downy mildew, damping-off, cool-weather leaf spot complex. Primary pests: aphids, leaf miners, cutworms, and possibly other local leafy-green pests.

      North Texas management priority: match the crop to alkaline Blackland Prairie clay, erratic rainfall, chloraminated municipal water, and routine 100-110 F summer heat. The primary disease priorities are white rust as major Texas spinach disease, downy mildew, damping-off, leaf spot complex, and root/crown issues in wet beds. The primary pest priorities are aphids, leaf miners, cutworms, slugs/snails in damp beds, and mites in stress conditions. These are not abstract lists; they determine the planting window, rotation group, irrigation method, row-cover timing, scouting route, and whether the bed should be pivoted to a different crop.

      1. Cross-References

      Disease Triangle; IPM Hierarchy; Seasonal Succession and Bed Pivoting SOP; white rust; leaf miners; Internal provisional chapters 20, 29, 39, 42.

      1. Sources & Confidence Note

      Sources by class: Extension: Rockwall/Tarrant guides; Texas A&M spinach and greens; Texas Plant Disease Handbook spinach; Cornell spinach varieties. Confidence note: High confidence for fall-primary timing, 50 F soil threshold, bolting mechanism, harvest method, and white rust priority; race-specific resistance needs verification.

      Source classes for this entry: Extension/government: Texas A&M Spinach and Other Greens; Rockwall/Tarrant guides; Texas Plant Disease Handbook spinach; Cornell disease-resistant spinach list.       Chapter 12: Roots, Bulbs, and Alliums

      Garlic

      1. Identity Block

      Scientific name: Allium sativum. Family: Amaryllidaceae. Common names: garlic, softneck garlic, hardneck garlic, Creole garlic. Source class: extension-supported practice for regional planting and management; peer-reviewed mechanism where specifically cited; practitioner doctrine only when explicitly labeled. Functional definition: a fall-planted allium grown from cloves for mature bulbs.

      1. Composition & Properties

      Garlic is propagated by cloves. The harvested bulb is a compound structure of multiple cloves enclosed in wrappers. Softneck and Creole types usually fit mild-winter/hot-summer regions better than many hardnecks. Hardneck types produce scapes and often require more winter cold. Garlic needs fall rooting, winter chilling, spring leaf growth, then daylength/temperature-triggered bulbing.

      For manuscript use, classify this entry as a fall-planted clonal allium bulb crop rather than by grocery category alone. Its practical properties in a North Texas garden are defined by season, plant architecture, marketable organ, and how quickly quality is lost when the crop is mistimed. It fills the role of cool-season allium that uses fall rooting, winter vernalization, spring leaf growth, and early summer curing. That role matters because the same bed can only hold one crop at a time; the crop must justify its season slot, water demand, and pest-management burden.

      1. Mechanism of Action

      Garlic succeeds through vernalization and photoperiod-linked bulbing. Fall-planted cloves root and leaf through cool months, receive cold exposure, and bulb as days lengthen and soil warms. Each healthy leaf contributes to bulb size. Garlic does NOT behave like a spring short-season vegetable in North Texas; spring planting usually produces greens or small bulbs. It also does NOT reliably produce true seed for ordinary garden propagation, so planting-stock disease moves clonally.

      The operating mechanism is clove roots in fall, vernalizes in winter, builds leaves in spring, then bulbs as daylength and soil temperature rise. The grower is not merely keeping a plant alive; the grower is protecting the specific developmental stage that creates the harvest. When that stage is interrupted, later vigor rarely repairs the lost yield. This is especially important in North Texas, where spring can move from cool to hot quickly and where late-summer plantings must beat the first freeze. Management should therefore begin with the calendar and the target harvest organ, then move to fertility, irrigation, and pest response.

      The primary limiter is late planting, insufficient cold for hardneck types, waterlogged clay, late nitrogen, delayed harvest, and curing in direct summer heat. This limiter should be handled through the Disease Triangle and IPM Hierarchy rather than through single-product thinking. A susceptible host, a viable pathogen or pest population, and favorable weather or canopy conditions produce damage. Cultural controls - timing, rotation, spacing, drip irrigation, sanitation, mulch, row cover, trellising, resistant varieties, and harvest discipline - carry more of the system than rescue sprays. Once the crop has passed the correct growth stage, a product cannot usually restore the missing developmental window.

      1. Planting and Establishment

      Recommended varieties and disease-resistance boundary: for North Texas, the safe production baseline is softneck and Creole/adapted garlic: Texas White, California Early, California Softneck, Mexican Purple, Mexican Pink/Creole, and silverskin/artichoke softneck types. Hardneck cultivars can be trialed for scapes and flavor, but they are not the default unless locally proven. No white-rot-resistant garlic varieties are available, so white rot is managed through clean planting stock, exclusion, sanitation, and avoiding infested beds rather than cultivar resistance. If a supplier claims Fusarium, Penicillium, or storage-rot tolerance, verify the disease, trial region, and cultivar evidence before treating it as a recommendation. hardneck cultivar reliability and disease-tolerance claims for North Texas.

      Fall window: September 15-October 30, with mid-October the main target. Spring window January 1-February 15 is emergency/experimental, not the main bulb strategy. Soil temperature: use cooling fall soil; exact planting threshold. Bulbing mechanism requires cold exposure and later soil above about 60 F. Plant cloves basal plate down, 1-2 inches deep, 3-6 inches apart, in raised well-drained beds. Varieties: Texas White, Early Louisiana, White Mexican, Creole/artichoke softnecks; hardnecks are trial crops.

      Use the planting window as a production boundary, not a casual suggestion. For North Texas, the working calendar is: fall September 15-October 30 with mid-October as core; spring January 1-February 15 is emergency/experimental for bulbs. The soil-temperature requirement is no verified NTX planting soil temperature; use cooling workable fall soil and avoid saturation threshold. Soil temperature should be checked in the morning before sun warms the bed and should be stable, not a one-afternoon spike. When the crop is transplanted, transplant size and hardening matter; when direct-seeded, seedbed crusting and moisture continuity matter more than most amendments.

      Establishment should be built around: raised beds, individual cloves basal plate down, 1-2 inches deep, 3-6 inches apart, moderate fall fertility, spring nitrogen before bulbing. In Blackland clay, bed shape is a first-order practice. Raised beds, broad ridges, and controlled traffic prevent the common pattern of a crop looking acceptable aboveground while roots sit in compacted or poorly aerated soil. Drip irrigation is the default because it reduces leaf wetness, preserves soil structure, and allows water to be delivered without splashing pathogens onto lower leaves or fruit.

      Recommended and defensible varieties or variety classes include: Texas White, Early Louisiana, White Mexican, Creole/artichoke softneck types, and trial hardnecks only with local evidence cultivar availability. Disease-resistant variety language must be read literally. A resistance code means resistance or tolerance to a named pathogen, race, or disease complex; it does not mean general garden toughness. For manuscript lock, current seed availability, exact resistance race codes, and regional performance should be verified against the final seed-source list and county extension notes.

      1. Interactions

      Garlic pairs with raised beds, compost, drip irrigation, fall planting, and spring split nitrogen. It interacts poorly with waterlogged clay, fresh manure, late nitrogen, and allium-on-allium rotation. Do not use high phosphorus plus AMF claims as a mechanism; high phosphorus suppresses AMF, and ectomycorrhizal products do not apply to annual vegetables.

      1. Contraindications & Failure Modes

      Do not plant grocery garlic for production unless experimental. Do not plant in saturated clay, apply late nitrogen, or harvest after wrapper breakdown. Failures include spring planting too late, hardneck cloving failure, small bulbs, Fusarium basal rot, Botrytis neck rot, white rot, thrips, bulb mites, and curing damage. Neem/sulfur safety applies.

      Do not plant this crop when the known limiter is already present and unmanaged: late planting, insufficient cold for hardneck types, waterlogged clay, late nitrogen, delayed harvest, and curing in direct summer heat. Do not buy diseased transplants, do not use unknown planting stock for production beds, and do not leave infected residue as surface mulch in a bed that will host a related crop. The most expensive failure is the one that carries pathogen or pest pressure forward into the next planting window.

      1. Diagnostics & Verification

      Successful fall garlic roots and sprouts before winter, then grows steadily in spring. Harvest in North Texas mid-May to early June when bottom 3-4 leaves have yellowed/browned and upper leaves remain green. Do not wait until all leaves are brown. Lift gently and keep bulbs out of direct sun.

      Verification starts with the crop-specific harvest indicator: mid-May to early June when lower 3-4 leaves yellow/brown and upper leaves remain green. This is the standard to use before interpreting plant size as success. A large plant without the correct harvest-stage signal is not a successful crop; it is biomass. Record planting date, soil temperature, cultivar, first flower or head date where applicable, first harvest date, and the reason the crop ended. Those notes are the basis for improving the next season.

      1. Lifecycle, Storage & Shelf Life

      Lifecycle is clove planting, fall rooting, winter vernalization, spring growth, bulbing, harvest, curing, trimming, storage, and replanting. Cure in shade with airflow for 3-6 weeks exact home conditions; direct summer sun can cook bulbs.

      Lifecycle handling should follow the crop's real endpoint. Storage and postharvest rule: cure 3-6 weeks in shade with strong airflow; avoid direct North Texas sun or hot sealed garages. For annual vegetables, the lifecycle also includes residue decisions. Healthy residue can be composted or used in appropriate mulch systems; diseased residue, nematode-infested roots, and pest-infested fruit should be removed or hot-composted where the composting system is capable of sanitation compost sanitation protocol.

      1. Sourcing & Quality Control

      Buy named seed garlic from reputable suppliers. Look for firm bulbs with no basal rot, mold, mite dust, or soft cloves. Softneck/Creole types are the production baseline; hardnecks are cultivar-specific trials.

      For this crop, the quality-control emphasis is the match between source and North Texas use: Texas White, Early Louisiana, White Mexican, Creole/artichoke softneck types, and trial hardnecks only with local evidence cultivar availability. A novelty variety can be grown as a trial, but production beds should reserve most space for adapted, documented cultivars. Keep seed lots labeled through harvest so that failures can be tied to cultivar, planting date, and bed rather than being remembered as a vague seasonal disappointment.

      1. North Texas Note

      Fall-planted, early-summer-harvest allium. Primary diseases: Botrytis neck rot, Fusarium basal rot, white rot where present, purple blotch/Stemphylium, storage rots. Primary pests: onion thrips, bulb mites, allium nematodes where contaminated stock exists.

      North Texas management priority: match the crop to alkaline Blackland Prairie clay, erratic rainfall, chloraminated municipal water, and routine 100-110 F summer heat. The primary disease priorities are Botrytis neck rot, Fusarium basal rot, white rot where present, purple blotch/Stemphylium leaf blight, storage rots, and virus issues in poor stock. The primary pest priorities are onion thrips, bulb mites, allium leafminers where present local status, and nematodes through contaminated stock. These are not abstract lists; they determine the planting window, rotation group, irrigation method, row-cover timing, scouting route, and whether the bed should be pivoted to a different crop.

      1. Cross-References

      Disease Triangle; IPM Hierarchy; Seasonal Succession and Bed Pivoting SOP; Botrytis neck rot; onion thrips; Internal provisional chapters 29, 39, 42.

      1. Sources & Confidence Note

      Extension/government: University of Maine Extension white rot guidance for the statement that no white-rot-resistant garlic or onion varieties are available and that biological approaches have variable results rather than reliable control.

      Sources by class: Extension: Texas A&M garlic production; Bexar and Ellis County AgriLife garlic guidance; Rockwall/Tarrant guides; UMass Botrytis neck rot. Confidence note: High confidence for fall planting, vernalization/bulbing logic, softneck advantage, harvest timing, and curing risk; resistant cultivar claims need verification.

      Source classes for this entry: Extension/government: Texas A&M Garlic Production in Texas; Bexar County garlic guidance; Ellis County Master Gardeners garlic guidance; Rockwall/Tarrant guides; UMass Botrytis neck rot guidance. Practitioner doctrine: companion-planting, tonic, foliar-feed, microbial-inoculant, and input-stacking claims are not treated as mechanism unless a source class supports the mechanism directly. items remain for current seed availability, exact Phase K entry titles, and county-level pest/disease ranking.

      Onion / Shallot

      1. Identity Block

      Scientific name: Allium cepa; shallot as Allium cepa Aggregatum Group. Family: Amaryllidaceae. Common names: onion, bulb onion, dry onion, green onion, shallot, multiplier onion. Source class: extension-supported practice for regional planting and management; peer-reviewed mechanism where specifically cited; practitioner doctrine only when explicitly labeled. Functional definition: cool-season alliums grown for bulbs or greens, with bulb onion success determined by daylength class.

      1. Composition & Properties

      Onions form bulbs from leaf bases; each leaf corresponds to a bulb ring, so more healthy leaves before bulbing generally means larger bulbs. Shallots form clustered bulbs. Onion types include short-day, intermediate-day, and long-day. North Texas requires short-day or intermediate-day onions; long-day onions do not bulb properly this far south.

      For manuscript use, classify this entry as a cool-season photoperiod-sensitive allium bulb crop rather than by grocery category alone. Its practical properties in a North Texas garden are defined by season, plant architecture, marketable organ, and how quickly quality is lost when the crop is mistimed. It fills the role of short-day and intermediate-day bulb crop where variety class determines success. That role matters because the same bed can only hold one crop at a time; the crop must justify its season slot, water demand, and pest-management burden.

      1. Mechanism of Action

      Onion production is a leaf-count and photoperiod process. Plants grow leaves in cool weather, then shift to bulb enlargement when daylength reaches the cultivar threshold. Onions do NOT bulb correctly when the wrong daylength class is planted. Long-day onions are a structural failure in North Texas, not a fertility problem. Onions also do NOT tolerate leaf disease casually because leaf loss reduces bulb size.

      The operating mechanism is leaf production in cool weather followed by daylength-triggered bulbing; each leaf contributes a bulb ring. The grower is not merely keeping a plant alive; the grower is protecting the specific developmental stage that creates the harvest. When that stage is interrupted, later vigor rarely repairs the lost yield. This is especially important in North Texas, where spring can move from cool to hot quickly and where late-summer plantings must beat the first freeze. Management should therefore begin with the calendar and the target harvest organ, then move to fertility, irrigation, and pest response.

      The primary limiter is wrong daylength class, late transplants, weeds, thrips, leaf diseases, late nitrogen, and poor curing. This limiter should be handled through the Disease Triangle and IPM Hierarchy rather than through single-product thinking. A susceptible host, a viable pathogen or pest population, and favorable weather or canopy conditions produce damage. Cultural controls - timing, rotation, spacing, drip irrigation, sanitation, mulch, row cover, trellising, resistant varieties, and harvest discipline - carry more of the system than rescue sprays. Once the crop has passed the correct growth stage, a product cannot usually restore the missing developmental window.

      1. Planting and Establishment

      Disease-resistant varieties: use disease-resistant onion lists as screening tools, then filter by North Texas daylength. Cornell lists Frontier with bacterial soft rot, Botrytis leaf blight, downy mildew, Fusarium basal rot, and pink root resistance; Highlander with Botrytis leaf blight, downy mildew, and neck rot resistance; Talon with Botrytis leaf blight, downy mildew, and pink root resistance; Sweet Uno with downy mildew and pink root resistance; Yellow Granex with pink root resistance; Purple Haze with downy mildew and pink root resistance; Sierra Blanca / Superstar with pink root resistance; and Crystal White / Wax Pearl with pink root resistance. These are not automatic North Texas recommendations until the seed label confirms short-day or intermediate-day adaptation, current availability, and the named disease package. current onion cultivar availability, daylength class, and disease package by supplier.

      Seed window: fall seed September-October depending on guide, or January-February for some local seed lines. Transplant/set window: January 1-February 15, with mid-to-late January to early March in local guidance. Soil temperature: 50 F for seed, 45 F for transplants. Plant transplants about 3/4 inch deep, not more than 1 inch, 3 inches apart. Varieties: Yellow Texas Supersweet/Grano 1015Y, Yellow Granex, Red Granex, Burgundy, Crystal Wax, White Granex, Red Bone, Red Burgundy, Sierra Blanca, Mercedes, TX1015Y. Disease-resistant catalog options require both resistance and daylength verification.

      Use the planting window as a production boundary, not a casual suggestion. For North Texas, the working calendar is: seed September-October or January-February depending system; transplants/sets January-February for dry bulbs; fall plantings for green onions and overwintering starts local shallot calendar. The soil-temperature requirement is onion seed 50 F and transplants 45 F in Rockwall guide. Soil temperature should be checked in the morning before sun warms the bed and should be stable, not a one-afternoon spike. When the crop is transplanted, transplant size and hardening matter; when direct-seeded, seedbed crusting and moisture continuity matter more than most amendments.

      Establishment should be built around: raised beds, shallow transplanting no deeper than 1 inch, weed-free rows, drip irrigation, and allium rotation. In Blackland clay, bed shape is a first-order practice. Raised beds, broad ridges, and controlled traffic prevent the common pattern of a crop looking acceptable aboveground while roots sit in compacted or poorly aerated soil. Drip irrigation is the default because it reduces leaf wetness, preserves soil structure, and allows water to be delivered without splashing pathogens onto lower leaves or fruit.

      Recommended and defensible varieties or variety classes include: Texas Supersweet/1015Y, Yellow Granex, Red Granex, Burgundy, Crystal Wax, White Granex, Red Bone, Red Burgundy, Cirrus, Sierra Blanca, Cougar, Legend, Mercedes, Prowler current short/intermediate classification. Disease-resistant variety language must be read literally. A resistance code means resistance or tolerance to a named pathogen, race, or disease complex; it does not mean general garden toughness. For manuscript lock, current seed availability, exact resistance race codes, and regional performance should be verified against the final seed-source list and county extension notes.

      1. Interactions

      Onions pair with weed-free beds, shallow cultivation, drip irrigation, and raised beds. They interact poorly with weeds, overhead irrigation, late nitrogen, and allium rotation. Excess late nitrogen delays maturity and increases storage-rot risk. Shallots follow the same allium rotation logic.

      1. Contraindications & Failure Modes

      Do not plant long-day onions for bulbs in North Texas. Do not plant transplants too deep or let weeds compete during leaf growth. Failures include wrong daylength, late transplants, bolting, thrips, purple blotch, Botrytis leaf blight, downy mildew, pink root, basal rot, neck rot, and poor curing. Neem/sulfur safety applies.

      Do not plant this crop when the known limiter is already present and unmanaged: wrong daylength class, late transplants, weeds, thrips, leaf diseases, late nitrogen, and poor curing. Do not buy diseased transplants, do not use unknown planting stock for production beds, and do not leave infected residue as surface mulch in a bed that will host a related crop. The most expensive failure is the one that carries pathogen or pest pressure forward into the next planting window.

      1. Diagnostics & Verification

      Good onions show active leaf growth before bulbing. If tops are healthy but bulbs fail, verify daylength class. Harvest green onions when pencil-sized. Dry bulbs are ready when the neck weakens and tops fall over. Cure in shade with airflow until necks dry and skins become papery. Shallots harvest similarly when tops yellow and fall.

      Verification starts with the crop-specific harvest indicator: green onions at pencil size; dry bulbs when necks soften and tops fall; cure in shade with airflow. This is the standard to use before interpreting plant size as success. A large plant without the correct harvest-stage signal is not a successful crop; it is biomass. Record planting date, soil temperature, cultivar, first flower or head date where applicable, first harvest date, and the reason the crop ended. Those notes are the basis for improving the next season.

      1. Lifecycle, Storage & Shelf Life

      Lifecycle is seed/transplant establishment, winter/spring leaf production, photoperiod-triggered bulbing, neck softening, harvest, curing, trimming, and storage. Short-day sweet onions often store less well than pungent types cultivar storage.

      Lifecycle handling should follow the crop's real endpoint. Storage and postharvest rule: short-day sweet onions store less than pungent types; store only cured, firm, undamaged bulbs. For annual vegetables, the lifecycle also includes residue decisions. Healthy residue can be composted or used in appropriate mulch systems; diseased residue, nematode-infested roots, and pest-infested fruit should be removed or hot-composted where the composting system is capable of sanitation compost sanitation protocol.

      1. Sourcing & Quality Control

      Buy by named variety and daylength class. “Yellow onion” is not enough. Transplants should be pencil-thin to moderate, not oversized and stressed. For shallots, use named disease-free stock and reject soft, moldy, sprouting, or unlabeled bulbs.

      For this crop, the quality-control emphasis is the match between source and North Texas use: Texas Supersweet/1015Y, Yellow Granex, Red Granex, Burgundy, Crystal Wax, White Granex, Red Bone, Red Burgundy, Cirrus, Sierra Blanca, Cougar, Legend, Mercedes, Prowler current short/intermediate classification. A novelty variety can be grown as a trial, but production beds should reserve most space for adapted, documented cultivars. Keep seed lots labeled through harvest so that failures can be tied to cultivar, planting date, and bed rather than being remembered as a vague seasonal disappointment.

      1. North Texas Note

      North Texas rule: short-day and intermediate-day varieties only. Primary diseases: Botrytis leaf blight, purple blotch, downy mildew, pink root, Fusarium basal rot, Botrytis neck rot. Primary pests: onion thrips, mites, and bulb pests where stock or rotation is contaminated.

      North Texas management priority: match the crop to alkaline Blackland Prairie clay, erratic rainfall, chloraminated municipal water, and routine 100-110 F summer heat. The primary disease priorities are Botrytis leaf blight, purple blotch, downy mildew, pink root, Fusarium basal rot, Botrytis neck rot, and bacterial soft rot. The primary pest priorities are onion thrips, mites, soilborne bulb pests, and leaf miners where present local status. These are not abstract lists; they determine the planting window, rotation group, irrigation method, row-cover timing, scouting route, and whether the bed should be pivoted to a different crop.

      1. Cross-References

      Disease Triangle; IPM Hierarchy; Seasonal Succession and Bed Pivoting SOP; onion thrips; Botrytis leaf blight; Internal provisional chapters 29, 39, 42.

      1. Sources & Confidence Note

      Variety database: Cornell's onion disease-resistant variety list is catalog-derived and includes named resistance packages for pink root, Fusarium basal rot, Botrytis leaf blight, downy mildew, neck rot, and bacterial soft rot; each cultivar still requires daylength and current-supplier confirmation before North Texas recommendation.

      Sources by class: Extension/government: Rockwall/Tarrant guides; Texas A&M onion guidance; Ellis and Collin County onion guidance; Texas onion crop profile; UMass Botrytis neck rot; Cornell onion variety list. Confidence note: High confidence for daylength requirements, planting windows, harvest indicators, and curing; shallot specifics and disease-resistant cultivar adaptation need verification.

      Source classes for this entry: Extension/government: Texas A&M onion guidance; Ellis and Collin County Master Gardener onion guidance; Rockwall/Tarrant guides; Texas onion crop disease profile; Cornell disease-resistant onion list; UMass neck rot guidance. Practitioner doctrine: companion-planting, tonic, foliar-feed, microbial-inoculant, and input-stacking claims are not treated as mechanism unless a source class supports the mechanism directly. items remain for current seed availability, exact Phase K entry titles, and county-level pest/disease ranking.

      Session 4 - Legume, grain, and root crops

      Carrot / Parsnip

      1. Identity Block

      Scientific name: Daucus carota subsp. sativus; Pastinaca sativa. Family: Apiaceae. Common names: carrot, Nantes carrot, Chantenay carrot, Danvers carrot, parsnip. Source class: extension-supported practice for regional planting and management; peer-reviewed mechanism where specifically cited; practitioner doctrine only when explicitly labeled. Functional definition: cool-season direct-seeded taproot crops where soil texture determines root quality.

      1. Composition & Properties

      Carrot and parsnip are biennial taproot crops grown as annual vegetables. The edible part is an enlarged storage root, so forking, galling, cracking, and stubbing are market defects. Carrot types include Nantes, Chantenay, Danvers, Imperator, round, and colored types. Parsnips are slower, white roots that improve after cold exposure.

      For manuscript use, classify this entry as a cool-season direct-seeded taproot crop group rather than by grocery category alone. Its practical properties in a North Texas garden are defined by season, plant architecture, marketable organ, and how quickly quality is lost when the crop is mistimed. It fills the role of root-quality crop where Blackland clay preparation determines success. That role matters because the same bed can only hold one crop at a time; the crop must justify its season slot, water demand, and pest-management burden.

      1. Mechanism of Action

      These crops succeed by uninterrupted taproot elongation followed by storage-root enlargement. Obstructed or injured root tips fork early and cannot be repaired later. Carrot and parsnip do NOT tolerate transplanting as a normal production method because the harvested organ is the primary root. They also do NOT perform well in raw compacted Blackland clay. Root-knot nematode directly damages the marketable root, and Steinernema nematodes do NOT control Meloidogyne root-knot nematodes.

      The operating mechanism is uninterrupted taproot elongation followed by storage-root enlargement in cool conditions. The grower is not merely keeping a plant alive; the grower is protecting the specific developmental stage that creates the harvest. When that stage is interrupted, later vigor rarely repairs the lost yield. This is especially important in North Texas, where spring can move from cool to hot quickly and where late-summer plantings must beat the first freeze. Management should therefore begin with the calendar and the target harvest organ, then move to fertility, irrigation, and pest response.

      The primary limiter is compacted/cloddy clay, crusting, old parsnip seed, drought during germination, root-knot nematode, and leaf blights. This limiter should be handled through the Disease Triangle and IPM Hierarchy rather than through single-product thinking. A susceptible host, a viable pathogen or pest population, and favorable weather or canopy conditions produce damage. Cultural controls - timing, rotation, spacing, drip irrigation, sanitation, mulch, row cover, trellising, resistant varieties, and harvest discipline - carry more of the system than rescue sprays. Once the crop has passed the correct growth stage, a product cannot usually restore the missing developmental window.

      1. Planting and Establishment

      Carrot spring window: February 1-February 15 or February 10-March 1. Fall window: August 15-September 30. Parsnip: early March trial or August 1-August 25 fall/winter production NTX calendar. Soil temperature: carrot 50 F; parsnip threshold. Prepare raised beds 8-12 inches deep, removing rocks and chunks. Carrot varieties: Royal Chantenay, Burpee’s Toudo, Parks Nandor, Danvers 126, Red Cored Chantenay, Scarlet Nantes, Sugar Snax. Resistant options include Bolero, Sugarsnax 54, Yaya, Napoli, Siroco, Romance, Kuroda, Orange Blaze. Parsnip options: Andover, Javelin, All American, Hollow Crown, Harris Model, Gladiator.

      Use the planting window as a production boundary, not a casual suggestion. For North Texas, the working calendar is: carrot spring February 1-March 1 and fall August 15-September 30; parsnip early March or August for fall/winter production NTX parsnip calendar. The soil-temperature requirement is carrot 50 F minimum; parsnip cool root-crop threshold parsnip soil temperature. Soil temperature should be checked in the morning before sun warms the bed and should be stable, not a one-afternoon spike. When the crop is transplanted, transplant size and hardening matter; when direct-seeded, seedbed crusting and moisture continuity matter more than most amendments.

      Establishment should be built around: raised beds or ridges, 8-12 inch prepared fine soil, removal of stones/chunks, direct seeding only, and surface moisture until emergence. In Blackland clay, bed shape is a first-order practice. Raised beds, broad ridges, and controlled traffic prevent the common pattern of a crop looking acceptable aboveground while roots sit in compacted or poorly aerated soil. Drip irrigation is the default because it reduces leaf wetness, preserves soil structure, and allows water to be delivered without splashing pathogens onto lower leaves or fruit.

      Recommended and defensible varieties or variety classes include: Royal Chantenay, Danvers 126, Red Cored Chantenay, Nantes, Scarlet Nantes, Sugar Snax, Bolero, Yaya, Napoli, Red Core Chantenay; parsnip Andover, Javelin, All American, Hollow Crown, Harris Model, Gladiator seed availability. Disease-resistant variety language must be read literally. A resistance code means resistance or tolerance to a named pathogen, race, or disease complex; it does not mean general garden toughness. For manuscript lock, current seed availability, exact resistance race codes, and regional performance should be verified against the final seed-source list and county extension notes.

      1. Interactions

      They pair with fine seedbeds, fully finished compost, steady surface moisture, and direct seeding. Fresh chunky organic matter, rocks, compacted clay, drought during germination, and crusted rows cause poor stands and distorted roots. Avoid repeating Apiaceae crops in diseased beds rotation group.

      1. Contraindications & Failure Modes

      Do not transplant for production or plant into cloddy soil. Do not overapply nitrogen; tops do not fix distorted roots threshold. Failures include poor germination, crusting, forked roots, hairy roots, root-knot nematode, leaf blights, wireworms, cutworms, and woody overmature roots. Neem/sulfur and Bt boundaries apply.

      Do not plant this crop when the known limiter is already present and unmanaged: compacted/cloddy clay, crusting, old parsnip seed, drought during germination, root-knot nematode, and leaf blights. Do not buy diseased transplants, do not use unknown planting stock for production beds, and do not leave infected residue as surface mulch in a bed that will host a related crop. The most expensive failure is the one that carries pathogen or pest pressure forward into the next planting window.

      1. Diagnostics & Verification

      Successful stands emerge evenly and keep fine foliage. Carrot harvest: 70-80 days, roots about 1-1.5 inches diameter. Parsnip harvest: after frost, when roots are about 1.5-2 inches diameter and 7-8 inches long. Poor emergence should trigger seed age, depth, moisture, heat, and crusting checks.

      Verification starts with the crop-specific harvest indicator: carrots 1-1.5 inch diameter; parsnips after cold exposure when roots are firm and full-size. This is the standard to use before interpreting plant size as success. A large plant without the correct harvest-stage signal is not a successful crop; it is biomass. Record planting date, soil temperature, cultivar, first flower or head date where applicable, first harvest date, and the reason the crop ended. Those notes are the basis for improving the next season.

      1. Lifecycle, Storage & Shelf Life

      Carrot maturity is about 70-80 days; parsnip often 100-120+ cultivar. Carrots store well in cool fall soil or refrigerated without tops. Parsnips can remain in mulched ground or store cold and humid.

      Lifecycle handling should follow the crop's real endpoint. Storage and postharvest rule: remove carrot tops and store cold/humid; parsnips can be mulched in soil or refrigerated. For annual vegetables, the lifecycle also includes residue decisions. Healthy residue can be composted or used in appropriate mulch systems; diseased residue, nematode-infested roots, and pest-infested fruit should be removed or hot-composted where the composting system is capable of sanitation compost sanitation protocol.

      1. Sourcing & Quality Control

      Buy fresh seed, especially parsnip, which is short-lived. Choose shorter carrot types for clay unless beds are deeply built. For disease beds, choose leaf blight or Pythium/cavity spot resistance where stated.

      For this crop, the quality-control emphasis is the match between source and North Texas use: Royal Chantenay, Danvers 126, Red Cored Chantenay, Nantes, Scarlet Nantes, Sugar Snax, Bolero, Yaya, Napoli, Red Core Chantenay; parsnip Andover, Javelin, All American, Hollow Crown, Harris Model, Gladiator seed availability. A novelty variety can be grown as a trial, but production beds should reserve most space for adapted, documented cultivars. Keep seed lots labeled through harvest so that failures can be tied to cultivar, planting date, and bed rather than being remembered as a vague seasonal disappointment.

      1. North Texas Note

      Blackland clay is the limiting factor. Primary diseases/problems: carrot leaf blight complex, root-knot nematode, Pythium/cavity spot/root rot, parsnip canker. Primary pests: cutworms, wireworms, aphids/leafhoppers, root-knot nematode.

      North Texas management priority: match the crop to alkaline Blackland Prairie clay, erratic rainfall, chloraminated municipal water, and routine 100-110 F summer heat. The primary disease priorities are carrot leaf blight complex, root-knot nematode, Pythium/cavity spot/root rot, parsnip canker, and damping-off. The primary pest priorities are cutworms, wireworms, aphids, leafhoppers, nematodes, and soil insect complex. These are not abstract lists; they determine the planting window, rotation group, irrigation method, row-cover timing, scouting route, and whether the bed should be pivoted to a different crop.

      1. Cross-References

      Disease Triangle; IPM Hierarchy; Seasonal Succession and Bed Pivoting SOP; Raised Bed Soil Preparation; root-knot nematode; wireworms; Internal provisional chapters 20, 29, 39, 42.

      1. Sources & Confidence Note

      Sources by class: Extension: Rockwall/Tarrant guides; Texas A&M carrot; Texas Plant Disease Handbook root-knot; UC IPM carrot nematodes; Arkansas/Illinois parsnip guidance; Cornell carrot varieties.
