      Session 5 - Leafy greens, herbs, and small fruits

      LSE-233 — Radish and Turnip

      Radish and turnip are cool-season root crops in the mustard family, Brassicaceae. Radish is usually Raphanus sativus; turnip is Brassica rapa subsp. rapa. Both are direct-seeded, fast to mature, and grown for swollen edible roots and edible greens. Radishes include small spring types, long daikon types, and winter storage types. Turnips include salad turnips, purple-top storage types, greens types, and dual-purpose cultivars.

      Functionally, both crops are short-window vegetables. They reward a loose seedbed, steady moisture, correct spacing, and timely harvest. They are not summer root crops for hot climates, not crops that tolerate crusted seedbeds well, and not good candidates for fixing with fertilizer after poor establishment. The lifecycle should be managed tightly: sow, keep the surface moist, thin early, protect seedlings from pests, harvest on time, clear residue, and rotate.

      The most important growing requirement is seedbed quality. Radish and turnip roots expand near the soil surface. If the top few inches are compacted, crusted, cloddy, salty, or unevenly moist, roots may become forked, pithy, woody, misshapen, or small. Mature compost can improve tilth, but immature compost, fresh manure, or excessive nitrogen can cause rank greens, poor root quality, salt stress, or pest attraction. Phosphorus and potassium should be managed by soil test rather than by adding bone meal or guano by habit.

      Direct seeding is the default. Seed at the depth and spacing on the packet, then thin early. Crowding is one of the most common causes of poor roots. If several seedlings remain shoulder-to-shoulder, they compete before the grower notices. Thin radishes when they are small; thin turnips to match the intended harvest size. Small salad turnips can be closer than large storage types, but no type produces quality roots when left in a crowded mat.

      Water must be consistent. Drought followed by heavy irrigation can create splitting, pithiness, hot flavor, or uneven growth. Overwatering in poorly drained clay can encourage root disease, damping-off, and poor oxygen in the seed zone. Mulch can help once seedlings are established, but do not bury tiny seedlings or keep the crown constantly wet.

      Pests are predictable because both crops are brassicas. Flea beetles can damage seedlings, especially in warm shoulder seasons. Cabbageworms, loopers, aphids, harlequin bugs, and root maggots may appear depending on season and site. Row cover can be valuable when installed immediately after seeding and sealed at the edges, but it must be managed for heat. If row cover is installed after pests are already present, it can protect the pest as much as the crop.

      Harvest timing is quality control. Small spring radishes decline quickly after maturity; they become woody, hot, hollow, or pithy when left too long. Daikon and winter radishes need more spacing and time. Salad turnips are best harvested while tender; storage turnips can size up more but still lose quality if heat or stress arrives. Turnip greens can be harvested selectively if the crop is grown for leaves, but heavy leaf removal reduces root growth.

      Sourcing should match season and use. Choose small red spring radishes for speed, daikon or winter radishes for fall space and deeper roots, and salad turnips for tender fresh eating. Use fresh seed for succession plantings. Avoid treating food radishes as equivalent to forage “tillage radish” cover-crop cultivars; the cover-crop function and kitchen root quality are different goals.

      NORTH TEXAS NOTE: In Lewisville, Denton, and north DFW, radish and turnip belong primarily in fall and early spring. Blackland Prairie clay can grow excellent roots when the surface is loosened, compost is mature, and moisture prevents crusting, but that same clay can seal after rain and bake hard. Fall is often more forgiving because soil is warm enough for germination while air temperatures are declining. Late-summer establishment may need shade cloth and careful watering. Spring crops must finish before heat. In pH 7.5–8.2 soil, do not chase every defect with phosphorus or lime; check seedbed texture, moisture swings, thinning, flea beetles, row-cover timing, and harvest date first.

      LSE-234 — Beet

      Beet is Beta vulgaris subsp. vulgaris, a cool-season biennial grown as an annual vegetable for swollen roots and edible greens. Garden beets are closely related to Swiss chard, sugar beet, and fodder beet. A beet “seed” is usually a multigerm fruit cluster unless the cultivar is monogerm, which means one planted unit can produce several seedlings. That biological detail matters because thinning is not optional.

      Functionally, beet is a moderate-speed cool-season crop that rewards uniform moisture, loose seedbed structure, early thinning, and steady nutrition. It is not a hot-weather root crop, not a crop that likes crusted or salty seedbeds, and not as forgiving of neglect as its tough-looking leaves suggest. Beets can provide both roots and greens, but the grower should manage the planting for the intended harvest. Heavy green harvest can reduce root size, while dense root production requires timely thinning and stable growth.

      Beet roots are rich in sugars, pigments, minerals, and fiber. Red beets contain betalain pigments, not anthocyanins. Golden, white, and striped cultivars differ in pigment, staining, flavor, heat response, and kitchen use. Greens are edible and can be cooked like chard, but tender greens are easiest to harvest when the crop is not under drought, heat, or pest stress.

      The seedbed should be fine, moist, and open enough for expansion. In heavy clay, clods and crusting are the main enemies. Compost can help when it is mature and fully incorporated into the surface zone, but unfinished compost, fresh manure, or excessive dry amendments can create salt, ammonia, or texture problems. Beets need balanced fertility, not a high-nitrogen push. Too much readily available nitrogen can favor tops over roots, while potassium, boron, and other nutrients should be corrected only from soil or sap-test guidance where deficiency is plausible.

      Direct seeding is usually best. Sow according to packet depth, keep the surface evenly moist until emergence, and thin early. Because most beet seed is multigerm, several seedlings may emerge from one seed cluster. Snip extras rather than yanking if roots are intertwined. For uniform roots, spacing must match the intended harvest size: baby beets can be closer, full-size beets need more room. Transplants can work when handled as young plugs, but root restriction, circling, or transplant shock can reduce root quality.

      Water consistency drives texture. Drought can make beets tough, small, and earthy-bitter; sudden heavy watering after stress can encourage cracking or uneven growth. Poor drainage can encourage root disease and low-oxygen stress. Mulch after establishment can moderate moisture, but do not bury crowns or keep seedlings wet under heavy residue.

      Pest and disease pressure overlaps with leafy greens and root crops. Leaf miners can mark beet leaves, aphids can colonize stressed plants, cutworms can clip seedlings, and flea beetles may damage young leaves. Cercospora leaf spot and other foliar diseases can increase in humid, crowded, or overhead-irrigated plantings. Manage with rotation, airflow, sanitation, morning irrigation where overhead water is unavoidable, and row cover only when heat and pest timing justify it.

      Harvest should be based on size and quality, not calendar alone. Pull sample roots to check diameter and texture. Beets left too long in warming weather can become woody or oversized. Greens can be harvested lightly from growing plants, but do not strip the canopy if roots are the main crop. After harvest, remove diseased residue and rotate away from related crops where pest or disease pressure has built.

      Sourcing should match the season and harvest goal. Choose early, uniform cultivars for spring; reliable fall cultivars for main crops; golden or striped types for specialty kitchen use; and monogerm seed if uniform spacing is a priority. Fresh seed improves stand reliability. Keep seed lots labeled through harvest so poor stands can be traced to cultivar, seed age, planting date, crusting, or heat rather than remembered as “beets failed.”

      NORTH TEXAS NOTE: In North Texas, beets are strongest in fall and early spring. Blackland clay can produce good beets if the top layer is friable and moisture is steady, but rain-crusting followed by sun-baking can ruin germination. In alkaline pH 7.5–8.2 soils, do not assume poor beet growth means the bed needs lime or phosphorus. First check seed freshness, crusting, thinning, irrigation consistency, salinity, leaf miners, and planting window. In 100–110°F weather, beets are not quality root crops; pivot beds to heat-season crops or cover instead of forcing summer beets.

      Chapter 13: Culinary Annuals and Specialty Fruit

      Basil

      1. Identity Block

      Scientific name: Ocimum basilicum primarily. Family: Lamiaceae. Common names: basil, sweet basil, Genovese basil, Thai basil, lemon basil, purple basil, spicy globe basil. Source class: extension-supported practice for regional planting and management; peer-reviewed mechanism where specifically cited; practitioner doctrine only when explicitly labeled. Functional definition: a heat-loving annual culinary herb grown for aromatic leaves.

      1. Composition & Properties

      Basil is a frost-sensitive annual with square stems, opposite aromatic leaves, and terminal flower spikes. Types include Genovese, sweet basil, Thai, lemon, purple, and globe forms. North Central Texas lists Sweet and Spicy Globe; modern downy mildew resistant sweet basils include Rutgers DMR lines, Amazel, and Prospera lines where available.

      For manuscript use, classify this entry as a heat-loving annual culinary herb rather than by grocery category alone. Its practical properties in a North Texas garden are defined by season, plant architecture, marketable organ, and how quickly quality is lost when the crop is mistimed. It fills the role of warm-season leaf herb that replaces cool-season herbs during heat if irrigated and pruned. That role matters because the same bed can only hold one crop at a time; the crop must justify its season slot, water demand, and pest-management burden.

      1. Mechanism of Action

      Basil succeeds through warm soil, sun, steady moisture, and frequent pruning that keeps plants vegetative. It does NOT stay productive if allowed to flower unchecked. Flowering shifts energy into reproduction and changes leaf quality. Basil also does NOT behave like drought-adapted woody Mediterranean herbs; it wants warmth plus moisture and drainage. Basil downy mildew is a major modern disease and aromatic leaves do not prevent it.

      The operating mechanism is warm-root vegetative shoot growth, aromatic leaf production, branching after pinching, and repeated harvest before flowering. The grower is not merely keeping a plant alive; the grower is protecting the specific developmental stage that creates the harvest. When that stage is interrupted, later vigor rarely repairs the lost yield. This is especially important in North Texas, where spring can move from cool to hot quickly and where late-summer plantings must beat the first freeze. Management should therefore begin with the calendar and the target harvest organ, then move to fertility, irrigation, and pest response.

      The primary limiter is cold soil, chilling injury after harvest, bolting/flowering, basil downy mildew, Fusarium wilt, and mite/aphid pressure. This limiter should be handled through the Disease Triangle and IPM Hierarchy rather than through single-product thinking. A susceptible host, a viable pathogen or pest population, and favorable weather or canopy conditions produce damage. Cultural controls - timing, rotation, spacing, drip irrigation, sanitation, mulch, row cover, trellising, resistant varieties, and harvest discipline - carry more of the system than rescue sprays. Once the crop has passed the correct growth stage, a product cannot usually restore the missing developmental window.

      1. Planting and Establishment

      Spring/summer window: after frost and after soil reaches about 70 F, generally April-July for successions exact NTX calendar. Fall window: seed August 1-August 15 or transplant August 20-September 15, frost-limited. Soil temperature: about 70 F. Space 10-12 inches apart in raised well-drained beds. Succession every 3-4 weeks where continuous clean foliage is desired. Resistant varieties: Rutgers Devotion DMR, Obsession DMR, Passion DMR, Thunderstruck DMR, Amazel, Prospera, Prospera Compact DMR; standard flavor types remain useful in lower disease pressure.

      Use the planting window as a production boundary, not a casual suggestion. For North Texas, the working calendar is: spring/summer after frost and soil warmth, roughly April-July; late summer/fall seed August 1-August 15 and transplants August 20-September 15 spring calendar. The soil-temperature requirement is 70 F soil target. Soil temperature should be checked in the morning before sun warms the bed and should be stable, not a one-afternoon spike. When the crop is transplanted, transplant size and hardening matter; when direct-seeded, seedbed crusting and moisture continuity matter more than most amendments.

      Establishment should be built around: transplant or direct seed shallowly, space 10-12 inches, use raised/drained beds, drip irrigation, and succession planting every 3-4 weeks for clean tender shoots interval. In Blackland clay, bed shape is a first-order practice. Raised beds, broad ridges, and controlled traffic prevent the common pattern of a crop looking acceptable aboveground while roots sit in compacted or poorly aerated soil. Drip irrigation is the default because it reduces leaf wetness, preserves soil structure, and allows water to be delivered without splashing pathogens onto lower leaves or fruit.

      Recommended and defensible varieties or variety classes include: Sweet, Genovese, Italian Large Leaf, Thai, Lemon, Purple, Spicy Globe, Rutgers Devotion DMR, Obsession DMR, Passion DMR, Thunderstruck DMR, Amazel, Prospera, Prospera Compact DMR current DMR status. Disease-resistant variety language must be read literally. A resistance code means resistance or tolerance to a named pathogen, race, or disease complex; it does not mean general garden toughness. For manuscript lock, current seed availability, exact resistance race codes, and regional performance should be verified against the final seed-source list and county extension notes.

      1. Interactions

      Basil pairs with drip irrigation, compost-amended raised beds, airflow, and pruning above nodes. It interacts poorly with saturated clay, overhead irrigation at dusk, and dense humid plantings. Postharvest temperature is critical: basil is chilling-sensitive and should be stored above 50 F, optimally around 55 F home method.

      1. Contraindications & Failure Modes

      Do not plant before soil warmth is stable or store harvested basil below 50 F. Do not keep downy mildew plants as mulch. Failures include cold injury, root rot, flowering/bolting, basil downy mildew, Fusarium wilt, bacterial leaf spot, leaf spots, southern blight, aphids, flea beetles, mites, thrips, leafhoppers, and slugs. Neem/sulfur and Bt boundaries apply.

      Do not plant this crop when the known limiter is already present and unmanaged: cold soil, chilling injury after harvest, bolting/flowering, basil downy mildew, Fusarium wilt, and mite/aphid pressure. Do not buy diseased transplants, do not use unknown planting stock for production beds, and do not leave infected residue as surface mulch in a bed that will host a related crop. The most expensive failure is the one that carries pathogen or pest pressure forward into the next planting window.

      1. Diagnostics & Verification

      Harvest once plants are established with several leaf sets. Pick often, remove flower heads promptly, and never strip the plant bare. Cut stems above leaf nodes to force branching. Basil downy mildew often begins as yellowing and underside gray sporulation.

      Verification starts with the crop-specific harvest indicator: pinch or cut above nodes before flowers dominate; remove flower heads promptly. This is the standard to use before interpreting plant size as success. A large plant without the correct harvest-stage signal is not a successful crop; it is biomass. Record planting date, soil temperature, cultivar, first flower or head date where applicable, first harvest date, and the reason the crop ended. Those notes are the basis for improving the next season.

      1. Lifecycle, Storage & Shelf Life

      Lifecycle is warm-season seeding/transplanting, vegetative branching, repeated harvest, flowering pressure, and frost death. It can be potted indoors in a sunny window. Store fresh basil above 50 F; refrigerator temperatures cause blackening and aroma loss.

      Lifecycle handling should follow the crop's real endpoint. Storage and postharvest rule: store above 50 F, ideally around 55 F; do not refrigerate below 50 F for quality basil. For annual vegetables, the lifecycle also includes residue decisions. Healthy residue can be composted or used in appropriate mulch systems; diseased residue, nematode-infested roots, and pest-infested fruit should be removed or hot-composted where the composting system is capable of sanitation compost sanitation protocol.

      1. Sourcing & Quality Control

      Buy by flavor type and disease package. In known downy mildew areas, DMR lines are insurance. Inspect transplants for yellow lower leaves, underside gray growth, black stems, mites, aphids, and weak stretched growth.

      For this crop, the quality-control emphasis is the match between source and North Texas use: Sweet, Genovese, Italian Large Leaf, Thai, Lemon, Purple, Spicy Globe, Rutgers Devotion DMR, Obsession DMR, Passion DMR, Thunderstruck DMR, Amazel, Prospera, Prospera Compact DMR current DMR status. A novelty variety can be grown as a trial, but production beds should reserve most space for adapted, documented cultivars. Keep seed lots labeled through harvest so that failures can be tied to cultivar, planting date, and bed rather than being remembered as a vague seasonal disappointment.

      1. North Texas Note

      Heat-loving but not drought-proof. Primary diseases: basil downy mildew, Fusarium wilt, bacterial leaf spot, Cercospora/Alternaria leaf spots, southern blight. Primary pests: aphids, flea beetles, spider mites, thrips, leafhoppers, slugs.

      North Texas management priority: match the crop to alkaline Blackland Prairie clay, erratic rainfall, chloraminated municipal water, and routine 100-110 F summer heat. The primary disease priorities are basil downy mildew, Fusarium wilt, bacterial leaf spot, Cercospora/Alternaria leaf spots, southern blight, damping-off. The primary pest priorities are aphids, flea beetles, spider mites, thrips, leafhoppers, slugs, and whiteflies. These are not abstract lists; they determine the planting window, rotation group, irrigation method, row-cover timing, scouting route, and whether the bed should be pivoted to a different crop.

      1. Cross-References

      Disease Triangle; IPM Hierarchy; Seasonal Succession and Bed Pivoting SOP; basil downy mildew; Fusarium wilt; Internal provisional chapters 20, 29, 39, 42.

      1. Sources & Confidence Note

      Extension/government: University of Maryland and Rutgers basil breeding resources for downy-mildew-resistant basil cultivars, including Rutgers Devotion, Obsession, Passion, Thunderstruck, plus Prospera and Amazel as non-Rutgers resistant options; cultivar performance still requires local verification. current North Texas DMR basil availability and performance.

      Sources by class: Extension/government: Texas A&M herbs; Tarrant variety list; NDSU basil; University of Minnesota basil downy mildew; Rutgers basil guidance; UC Davis postharvest herbs; University of Maryland cultivar study. Confidence note: High confidence for 70 F planting target, pruning requirement, DMR varieties, and no-storage-below-50 F rule; exact NTX calendar and current DMR performance need verification.

      Source classes for this entry: Extension/government: Texas A&M Growing Herbs in Texas; Tarrant variety guide; NDSU basil; University of Minnesota basil downy mildew; Rutgers/UC Davis postharvest basil; University of Maryland cultivar study. Practitioner doctrine: companion-planting, tonic, foliar-feed, microbial-inoculant, and input-stacking claims are not treated as mechanism unless a source class supports the mechanism directly. items remain for current seed availability, exact Phase K entry titles, and county-level pest/disease ranking.

      Cilantro / Dill / Fennel

      1. Identity Block

      Scientific name: Coriandrum sativum; Anethum graveolens; Foeniculum vulgare. Family: Apiaceae. Common names: cilantro, coriander, dill weed, dill seed, fennel, Florence fennel, bronze fennel. Source class: extension-supported practice for regional planting and management; peer-reviewed mechanism where specifically cited; practitioner doctrine only when explicitly labeled. Functional definition: aromatic Apiaceae herbs grown for leaves, seed, pollen, or bulb fennel, with heat bolting as the main North Texas constraint.

      1. Composition & Properties

      These herbs have taproots, divided foliage, umbels, and aromatic leaves or seeds. Cilantro is grown for leaves or coriander seed. Dill is grown for fronds, heads, or seed. Fennel includes common/bronze fennel for fronds/seed and Florence fennel for swollen bulb-like bases. They are often grouped but are not interchangeable.

      For manuscript use, classify this entry as a cool-to-mild-season aromatic Apiaceae herb group rather than by grocery category alone. Its practical properties in a North Texas garden are defined by season, plant architecture, marketable organ, and how quickly quality is lost when the crop is mistimed. It fills the role of leaf/seed/bulb herb group managed around bolting and taproot sensitivity. That role matters because the same bed can only hold one crop at a time; the crop must justify its season slot, water demand, and pest-management burden.

      1. Mechanism of Action

      They succeed by cool-to-mild-season vegetative growth followed by flowering and seed. Cilantro is most heat-sensitive; dill is short-lived; Florence fennel requires cool steady growth to size before bolting. These crops do NOT stay leafy indefinitely because the gardener keeps cutting them. Cutting delays flowering but heat, daylength, drought, and age still drive bolting. Fennel companion-planting claims should be disciplined: allelopathic potential exists in studies, but universal garden suppression claims are practitioner doctrine unless directly supported.

      The operating mechanism is cool-season vegetative leaf growth followed by umbel flowering and seed formation; Florence fennel additionally forms a swollen stem base under cool steady growth. The grower is not merely keeping a plant alive; the grower is protecting the specific developmental stage that creates the harvest. When that stage is interrupted, later vigor rarely repairs the lost yield. This is especially important in North Texas, where spring can move from cool to hot quickly and where late-summer plantings must beat the first freeze. Management should therefore begin with the calendar and the target harvest organ, then move to fertility, irrigation, and pest response.

      The primary limiter is heat bolting, transplant shock, hot seedbeds, fennel allelopathy/competition concerns, aphids, leafhoppers, swallowtail caterpillars, and damping-off. This limiter should be handled through the Disease Triangle and IPM Hierarchy rather than through single-product thinking. A susceptible host, a viable pathogen or pest population, and favorable weather or canopy conditions produce damage. Cultural controls - timing, rotation, spacing, drip irrigation, sanitation, mulch, row cover, trellising, resistant varieties, and harvest discipline - carry more of the system than rescue sprays. Once the crop has passed the correct growth stage, a product cannot usually restore the missing developmental window.

      1. Planting and Establishment

      Cilantro spring: February 1-March 15; fall: September 1-October 31, with fall primary. Soil: best germination 55-68 F and below about 75 F. Dill spring: March 1-April 15 or April-May after frost; fall: September 1-October 15. Soil around 70 F for fast establishment. Fennel spring: March-April trial; Florence fennel fall: August 25-October 1. Fennel soil: 50-75 F. Varieties: cilantro Long Standing, Slo Bolt, Leisure, Santo, Delfino, Calypso; dill Bouquet, Dukat, Fernleaf, Long Island, Superdukat; fennel Zefa Fino, Orion, Cantino, Victoria, Mantavo, Rhondo. Disease-resistant herb cultivars are not well verified.

      Use the planting window as a production boundary, not a casual suggestion. For North Texas, the working calendar is: cilantro February 1-March 15 and September 1-October 31; dill March 1-April 15 and September 1-October 15; Florence fennel primarily August 25-October 1 county lines. The soil-temperature requirement is cilantro 55-68 F best germination; dill around 70 F for fast establishment; fennel 50-75 F. Soil temperature should be checked in the morning before sun warms the bed and should be stable, not a one-afternoon spike. When the crop is transplanted, transplant size and hardening matter; when direct-seeded, seedbed crusting and moisture continuity matter more than most amendments.

      Establishment should be built around: direct seed into fine deep soil, thin early, avoid root disturbance, succession sow cilantro, stake dill if windy, isolate common fennel from precision vegetable beds. In Blackland clay, bed shape is a first-order practice. Raised beds, broad ridges, and controlled traffic prevent the common pattern of a crop looking acceptable aboveground while roots sit in compacted or poorly aerated soil. Drip irrigation is the default because it reduces leaf wetness, preserves soil structure, and allows water to be delivered without splashing pathogens onto lower leaves or fruit.

      Recommended and defensible varieties or variety classes include: cilantro Long Standing, Slo Bolt, Leisure, Santo, Delfino, Calypso; dill Bouquet, Dukat, Fernleaf, Long Island, Superdukat; fennel Zefa Fino, Orion, Cantino, Victoria, Mantavo, Rhondo seed availability. Disease-resistant variety language must be read literally. A resistance code means resistance or tolerance to a named pathogen, race, or disease complex; it does not mean general garden toughness. For manuscript lock, current seed availability, exact resistance race codes, and regional performance should be verified against the final seed-source list and county extension notes.

      1. Interactions

      They pair with direct seeding, fine seedbeds, moderate fertility, cool-season succession, and insectary use. They interact poorly with transplant shock, hot exposed soil, saturated clay, and high nitrogen that dilutes herb quality. Dill and fennel host swallowtail caterpillars; tolerate or manage based on crop goal. Grow common fennel at garden edges or containers to avoid self-seeding and companion concerns.

      1. Contraindications & Failure Modes

      Do not plant cilantro for summer leaf harvest. Do not transplant cilantro/dill for production unless experimental. Do not let common fennel naturalize if volunteers are unwanted. Failures include bolting, poor hot-soil germination, dill lodging, fennel bolting, aphids, leafhoppers, Aster yellows, cilantro bacterial leaf spot, damping-off, mites, thrips, and caterpillars. Neem/sulfur and Bt boundaries apply.

      Do not plant this crop when the known limiter is already present and unmanaged: heat bolting, transplant shock, hot seedbeds, fennel allelopathy/competition concerns, aphids, leafhoppers, swallowtail caterpillars, and damping-off. Do not buy diseased transplants, do not use unknown planting stock for production beds, and do not leave infected residue as surface mulch in a bed that will host a related crop. The most expensive failure is the one that carries pathogen or pest pressure forward into the next planting window.

      1. Diagnostics & Verification

      Cilantro leaves are ready about 45-70 days; cut outer leaves at 4-6 inches or cut above soil. Dill fronds taste best before flowering; seed heads are harvested after bloom when browning. Florence fennel is harvested at firm bases under 4 inches wide, before bolting.

      Verification starts with the crop-specific harvest indicator: cilantro leaves at 4-6 inches or cut above crown; dill fronds before flowering and seed heads after bloom; Florence fennel firm bulbs before bolting. This is the standard to use before interpreting plant size as success. A large plant without the correct harvest-stage signal is not a successful crop; it is biomass. Record planting date, soil temperature, cultivar, first flower or head date where applicable, first harvest date, and the reason the crop ended. Those notes are the basis for improving the next season.

      1. Lifecycle, Storage & Shelf Life

      Cilantro and dill are short-lived annuals. Fennel can self-seed or persist depending on type. Fresh leaves are short-storage herbs; seeds must dry fully before storage.

      Lifecycle handling should follow the crop's real endpoint. Storage and postharvest rule: fresh leaves short storage cold/humid; fully dry coriander/dill/fennel seed before sealed storage. For annual vegetables, the lifecycle also includes residue decisions. Healthy residue can be composted or used in appropriate mulch systems; diseased residue, nematode-infested roots, and pest-infested fruit should be removed or hot-composted where the composting system is capable of sanitation compost sanitation protocol.

      1. Sourcing & Quality Control

      Buy seed by intended use: cilantro leaf versus coriander seed, dill frond versus seed, common fennel versus Florence fennel. “Fennel” alone is not enough. Avoid old cilantro or parsnip-family herb seed for critical fall plantings seed longevity.

      For this crop, the quality-control emphasis is the match between source and North Texas use: cilantro Long Standing, Slo Bolt, Leisure, Santo, Delfino, Calypso; dill Bouquet, Dukat, Fernleaf, Long Island, Superdukat; fennel Zefa Fino, Orion, Cantino, Victoria, Mantavo, Rhondo seed availability. A novelty variety can be grown as a trial, but production beds should reserve most space for adapted, documented cultivars. Keep seed lots labeled through harvest so that failures can be tied to cultivar, planting date, and bed rather than being remembered as a vague seasonal disappointment.

      1. North Texas Note

      Cilantro is fall-primary; dill is spring or fall; Florence fennel is a fall bulb crop. Primary diseases: cilantro bacterial leaf spot, damping-off, Aster yellows, wet-clay root/stem rots. Primary pests: aphids, leafhoppers, swallowtail caterpillars, thrips, spider mites, armyworm/looper pressure.

      North Texas management priority: match the crop to alkaline Blackland Prairie clay, erratic rainfall, chloraminated municipal water, and routine 100-110 F summer heat. The primary disease priorities are cilantro bacterial leaf spot, damping-off, Aster yellows, root/stem rots, minor leaf spots, and disease in overcrowded humid canopies. The primary pest priorities are aphids, leafhoppers, swallowtail caterpillars, thrips, spider mites, armyworms, and loopers. These are not abstract lists; they determine the planting window, rotation group, irrigation method, row-cover timing, scouting route, and whether the bed should be pivoted to a different crop.

      1. Cross-References

      Disease Triangle; IPM Hierarchy; Seasonal Succession and Bed Pivoting SOP; bacterial leaf spot of cilantro; leafhoppers/Aster yellows; Internal provisional chapters 20, 29, 39, 42.

      1. Sources & Confidence Note

      Peer-reviewed: Ravlic, M., Balicevic, R., Nikolic, M., and Sarajlic, A. 2016. Assessment of Allelopathic Potential of Fennel, Rue and Sage on Weed Species Hoary Cress (Lepidium draba). Notulae Botanicae Horti Agrobotanici Cluj-Napoca 44(1):48-52. DOI: 10.15835/nbha44110097.

      Sources by class: Extension: Texas A&M cilantro and dill; Utah State cilantro/fennel; University of Maryland herb problems; University of Minnesota herbs; Illinois and Oklahoma State herb guidance. Peer-reviewed: Ravlic et al. 2016 fennel allelopathic potential, DOI 10.15835/nbha44110097. Confidence note: High confidence for cilantro fall timing, heat bolting, dill direct seeding, fennel bolting risk, and harvest indicators; NTX dill/fennel cutoffs and cultivar disease resistance need verification.

      Source classes for this entry: Extension/government: Texas A&M cilantro and dill guides; Utah State cilantro and fennel guides; University of Maryland herb problems; University of Minnesota herbs. Peer-reviewed: Ravlic et al. 2016 fennel allelopathic potential DOI.       Strawberry — Annual Hill System

      1. Identity Block

      Scientific name: Fragaria x ananassa. Family: Rosaceae. Common names: strawberry, garden strawberry, short-day strawberry, June-bearing strawberry. Source class: extension-supported practice for regional planting and management; peer-reviewed mechanism where specifically cited; practitioner doctrine only when explicitly labeled. Functional definition: a fall-planted, spring-harvested small fruit crop managed as an annual hill system in North Texas.

      1. Composition & Properties

      Strawberry is a shallow-rooted perennial fruit crop managed as an annual in warm climates. Plants grow from crowns, establish roots in fall, initiate flower buds under cool/short-day conditions, flower in late winter/spring, and fruit before summer heat. Types include June-bearing/short-day, everbearing, and day-neutral. For North Texas annual hill production, June-bearing/short-day cultivars are the default.

      For manuscript use, classify this entry as a fall-planted short-day perennial fruit managed as annual hill crop rather than by grocery category alone. Its practical properties in a North Texas garden are defined by season, plant architecture, marketable organ, and how quickly quality is lost when the crop is mistimed. It fills the role of North Texas fall-plant/spring-harvest fruit crop that exits the bed before summer heat. That role matters because the same bed can only hold one crop at a time; the crop must justify its season slot, water demand, and pest-management burden.

      1. Mechanism of Action

      The annual hill system succeeds by avoiding the North Texas summer survival problem: plant in fall, grow through winter, harvest in spring, then remove plants and pivot the bed. Strawberries do NOT become easy perennial crops in North Texas because they are perennial botanically. Keeping them through summer ties up bed space and increases mite, weed, drought, crown/root disease, and heat stress. Strawberries also do NOT tolerate raw wet Blackland clay; drainage and crown health are central.

      The operating mechanism is fall root/crown establishment, short-day flower bud initiation, winter chilling, spring bloom/fruiting, then bed termination before summer survival stress. The grower is not merely keeping a plant alive; the grower is protecting the specific developmental stage that creates the harvest. When that stage is interrupted, later vigor rarely repairs the lost yield. This is especially important in North Texas, where spring can move from cool to hot quickly and where late-summer plantings must beat the first freeze. Management should therefore begin with the calendar and the target harvest organ, then move to fertility, irrigation, and pest response.

      The primary limiter is spring planting, heavy clay drainage, crown depth errors, late planting, Botrytis, anthracnose crown/fruit rot, mites, birds, and frost-damaged blooms. This limiter should be handled through the Disease Triangle and IPM Hierarchy rather than through single-product thinking. A susceptible host, a viable pathogen or pest population, and favorable weather or canopy conditions produce damage. Cultural controls - timing, rotation, spacing, drip irrigation, sanitation, mulch, row cover, trellising, resistant varieties, and harvest discipline - carry more of the system than rescue sprays. Once the crop has passed the correct growth stage, a product cannot usually restore the missing developmental window.

      1. Planting and Establishment

      Primary planting window: late September through mid-October, with early to mid-October the target. Late planting into early November is risky; spring planting is not the recommended production system. Soil/root-zone target: cooling fall soil, roughly around 72-75 F daytime conditions and not hot exposed beds root-zone threshold. Plant in raised beds or raised rows, crowns at correct depth, about 12 inches apart in staggered rows. Varieties: Festival, Chandler, Camarosa, Oso Grande, Radiance, Sequoia, Sweet Charlie; day-neutral types such as Albion, San Andreas, and Seascape are trials, not default availability.

      Use the planting window as a production boundary, not a casual suggestion. For North Texas, the working calendar is: late September through mid-October preferred; early November risky; spring planting is not the production system. The soil-temperature requirement is cooling fall root-zone with daytime conditions around 72-75 F; avoid hot root zones above about 85 F precise NTX root-zone threshold. Soil temperature should be checked in the morning before sun warms the bed and should be stable, not a one-afternoon spike. When the crop is transplanted, transplant size and hardening matter; when direct-seeded, seedbed crusting and moisture continuity matter more than most amendments.

      Establishment should be built around: raised beds or raised rows, sandy well-drained media, crowns at exact depth, 12-inch staggered spacing, drip after establishment, frost cloth for freezes and removal for bee access. In Blackland clay, bed shape is a first-order practice. Raised beds, broad ridges, and controlled traffic prevent the common pattern of a crop looking acceptable aboveground while roots sit in compacted or poorly aerated soil. Drip irrigation is the default because it reduces leaf wetness, preserves soil structure, and allows water to be delivered without splashing pathogens onto lower leaves or fruit.

      Recommended and defensible varieties or variety classes include: Festival, Chandler, Camarosa, Oso Grande, Radiance, Sequoia, Sweet Charlie where anthracnose resistance is priority, and day-neutral trials such as Albion/San Andreas only as secondary local availability. Disease-resistant variety language must be read literally. A resistance code means resistance or tolerance to a named pathogen, race, or disease complex; it does not mean general garden toughness. For manuscript lock, current seed availability, exact resistance race codes, and regional performance should be verified against the final seed-source list and county extension notes.

      1. Interactions

      Strawberry pairs with raised beds, drip irrigation after establishment, clean mulch, frost cloth, bee access during bloom, and spring-to-summer bed pivoting. Overhead irrigation can reduce transplant shock initially but increases disease if continued. Row cover protects from freezes but must be removed for bee pollination except during frost events. Avoid lime recommendations without soil tests in alkaline/high-Mg soils.

      1. Contraindications & Failure Modes

      Do not plant into low wet clay, bury crowns, expose roots, or keep annual hill plants through summer by default. Failures include late planting, weak fall growth, hot transplant shock, crown rot, Botrytis gray mold, anthracnose crown/fruit rot, Phytophthora/root rot, leaf spots, aphids, mites, thrips, slugs/snails, pillbugs, birds, and freeze injury to blooms. Neem/sulfur and nematode boundaries apply.

      Do not plant this crop when the known limiter is already present and unmanaged: spring planting, heavy clay drainage, crown depth errors, late planting, Botrytis, anthracnose crown/fruit rot, mites, birds, and frost-damaged blooms. Do not buy diseased transplants, do not use unknown planting stock for production beds, and do not leave infected residue as surface mulch in a bed that will host a related crop. The most expensive failure is the one that carries pathogen or pest pressure forward into the next planting window.

      1. Diagnostics & Verification

      Successful establishment shows new leaves, firm crowns, and active white roots. Harvest berries when at least 75 percent red, preferably fully red for flavor, every other day or three times weekly during peak. Misshapen fruit may indicate poor pollination, freeze injury, thrips, or drought. Botrytis shows soft gray mold; anthracnose fruit rot shows sunken brown lesions.

      Verification starts with the crop-specific harvest indicator: pick berries at least 75 percent red to fully red; harvest cool and frequently. This is the standard to use before interpreting plant size as success. A large plant without the correct harvest-stage signal is not a successful crop; it is biomass. Record planting date, soil temperature, cultivar, first flower or head date where applicable, first harvest date, and the reason the crop ended. Those notes are the basis for improving the next season.

      1. Lifecycle, Storage & Shelf Life

      Lifecycle is bed prep, fall planting, establishment irrigation, winter crown growth, frost protection, late-winter bloom, spring harvest, sanitation, and crop termination. Fresh berries are highly perishable; harvest cool, handle gently, refrigerate promptly, and avoid washing until use storage protocol.

      Lifecycle handling should follow the crop's real endpoint. Storage and postharvest rule: highly perishable; refrigerate promptly and wash near use; remove diseased fruit from beds. For annual vegetables, the lifecycle also includes residue decisions. Healthy residue can be composted or used in appropriate mulch systems; diseased residue, nematode-infested roots, and pest-infested fruit should be removed or hot-composted where the composting system is capable of sanitation compost sanitation protocol.

      1. Sourcing & Quality Control

      Order plants early and plant promptly. Choose named certified disease-free plants. Avoid weak crowns, black crowns, rotted roots, desiccated plants, and leaf-spotted stock. Festival is a strong Texas-tested default; Chandler can be productive but softer and more disease-sensitive; Sweet Charlie is useful where anthracnose resistance is prioritized.

      For this crop, the quality-control emphasis is the match between source and North Texas use: Festival, Chandler, Camarosa, Oso Grande, Radiance, Sequoia, Sweet Charlie where anthracnose resistance is priority, and day-neutral trials such as Albion/San Andreas only as secondary local availability. A novelty variety can be grown as a trial, but production beds should reserve most space for adapted, documented cultivars. Keep seed lots labeled through harvest so that failures can be tied to cultivar, planting date, and bed rather than being remembered as a vague seasonal disappointment.

      1. North Texas Note

      Fall-plant, spring-harvest annual hill crop. Primary diseases: Botrytis gray mold, anthracnose crown/fruit rot, Phytophthora/red stele/root rot, Rhizoctonia crown rot, leaf spot/scorch, angular leaf spot. Primary pests: birds, spider mites, aphids, thrips, slugs/snails, pillbugs, nematodes where assays confirm.

      North Texas management priority: match the crop to alkaline Blackland Prairie clay, erratic rainfall, chloraminated municipal water, and routine 100-110 F summer heat. The primary disease priorities are Botrytis gray mold, anthracnose crown and fruit rot, Phytophthora/red stele root rot, Rhizoctonia crown rot, leaf spot/scorch, angular leaf spot. The primary pest priorities are birds, spider mites, aphids, thrips, slugs/snails, pillbugs, and nematodes where assays confirm. These are not abstract lists; they determine the planting window, rotation group, irrigation method, row-cover timing, scouting route, and whether the bed should be pivoted to a different crop.

      1. Sources & Confidence Note

      Extension/government: Travis County / Texas A&M strawberry guidance for the annual hill system, fall planting, 12-inch staggered spacing, spring harvest timing, row-cover pollination management, and summer bed pivot. Extension/government: Texas A&M AgriLife fall strawberry planting guidance for the Texas position that fall planting improves rooting, spring yield, and fruit quality.

      Sources by class: Extension/government: Texas A&M AgriLife strawberry planting guidance and trials; Travis County/Texas A&M annual system; Texas Plant Disease Handbook strawberry; Clemson strawberry annual hill and diseases; NC State strawberry plasticulture guide; University of Minnesota day-neutral temperature guidance. Confidence note: High confidence for annual hill logic, fall planting, June-bearing default, raised-bed need, harvest indicators, and disease priorities; root-zone threshold and cultivar ranking need verification.
